# <span id="page-7-0"></span>

--- SENTENCE 14 ---
American Samoa Coral Reef Fishery Workshop October 21-23, 2008 Introduction, Summary and Recommendations

#### Introduction

Part 1: Historical Overview of Fisheries in American Samoa

Part 2: Present Status and Management of Fisheries In American Samoa Session 1: Present status and trend in coral reef fish population and fishery Session 2: Current management status for coral reef and associated fishery

Part 3: Strategic Planning for Fishery Management Consensus on the status of American Samoa's coral reef fisheries Recommendations on ways to address non-consensus issues Recommendations on ways to move forward with management

Specific Data Needs

Recommendations for Future Workshops

#### Introduction

Over the course of three days (Oct. 21-23, 2008), local and regional resource managers and scientists from NOAA-Pacific Islands Regional Office, NOAA-Pacific Island Fishery Science Center, Department of Marine and Wildlife Resources, Department of Fishery, Samoa, Department of Commerce, American Samoa Environmental Protection Agency, American Samoa Community College, American Samoa Coastal Management Program, Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary, National Park Service of American Samoa, The University of Hawaii, Western Pacific Regional Fishery Management Council and community members gathered in Pago Pago, American Samoa.

#### Objectives

The workshop addressed the following main objectives:

- 1.

--- SENTENCE 15 ---
Document the historical use, catch, and effort of fishery resources in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 16 ---
- 2. Assess the current state of fishery resources, availability and quality of fisheries data in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 17 ---
Engage community members in fisheries management.

#### Outcomes

Based on the above objectives, the workshop was designed to stimulate open group discussion among participants to reach the following outcomes:

- 1.

--- SENTENCE 18 ---
Recommendations for improved data collection
- 2.

--- SENTENCE 19 ---
Recommended management measures based upon available data
- 3.

--- SENTENCE 20 ---
Determined management tools needed to maintain a sustainable coral reef fishery
- 4.

--- SENTENCE 21 ---
Integrated management regime between American Samoa and Independent Samoa ("Two Samoas")

--- SENTENCE 22 ---
In efforts to reach the main objectives, the workshop was divided into three focused sections, with the following themes: (1) Historical overview of fisheries, (2) Present status and management of fisheries, and (3) Strategic planning for fishery management.

--- SENTENCE 23 ---
The first two sections' specific objectives, key outcomes, gaps, and recommendations are described below.

--- SENTENCE 24 ---
Full summaries of each presentation follow this report.

--- SENTENCE 25 ---
The last section resulted in a list of consensus/non-consensus issues which were assembled based on the first two sections' presentations and group discussions.

--- SENTENCE 26 ---
The final day of the workshop was also used to provide recommendations on ways to revise the fisheries local action strategy (LAS).

--- SENTENCE 27 ---
Historical Overview of Fisheries in American Samoa 1a.

--- SENTENCE 28 ---
Objectives

Three presentations (Table 1) were given in the first session which provided information on past fishing patterns, traditional fishery management, and fish population status based on historical accounts, elder fishermen interviews and archaeological studies.

--- SENTENCE 29 ---
The main objectives of this first session on the historical overview of fisheries in American Samoa included the following:

- 1.

--- SENTENCE 30 ---
To assess the historical, traditional fishing patterns/practices in American Samoa.
- 2.

--- SENTENCE 31 ---
To reveal what is known about fish consumption from the past in American Samoa.
- 3.

--- SENTENCE 32 ---
To generate a "baseline" of the nearshore fishery based on the historical accounts of traditional fishing practices and management, fish populations, and consumption patterns.

#### **Table 1 Presentations in Session 1**

| Part 1: Historical Overview of Fisheries in American Samoa                                                                  |                            |
|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------|
| 2500 years of marine procurement in American Samoa: Patterns and<br>implications based on archeological records             | Alex Morrison - UH, Manoa  |
| Historic fishing methods and marine management in American<br>Samoa based on archival records                               | Arielle Levine – NOAAPIFSC |
| Traditional knowledge of marine resource use, management, and<br>changes over time based on interviews with elder fishermen | Bert Fuiava – DMWR         |

#### **1b.

--- SENTENCE 33 ---
Key outcomes**

Historic fishing methods and marine management in American Samoa based on archival records Very little is known about the historical fishing and consumption patterns in American Samoa, and no baseline data exists.

--- SENTENCE 34 ---
However, through written records of early explorers, missionaries, colonial administrators, naval officers and anthropologists, as well as through interviews with elder fishermen, a picture of the past fishery resource patterns and management can be composed.

--- SENTENCE 35 ---
Families and villages were central to management and decision-making in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 36 ---
This traditional value system was based on sharing and distribution of food, and not on individual accumulation.

--- SENTENCE 37 ---
Thus, in the past, fishing was performed as a practice oriented around culture and consumption purposes rather than for economic gain.

--- SENTENCE 38 ---
Traditional knowledge of marine resource use, management, and changes over time based on interviews with elder fishermen

Based on elder fishermen interviews, as presented by B. Fuiava, there is a general consensus about the decline in reef fish populations and that there has been a decrease in fishing effort.

--- SENTENCE 39 ---
Information from the interviews indicate that elder fisherman are concerned about habitat degradation.

2500 years of marine procurement in American Samoa: Patterns and implications based on archeological records

As presented by A. Morrison, archaeological evidence and analysis of ancient fishbone assemblages on Tutuila suggest that the prehistoric fish community composition and catch in American Samoa (1000- 3000 years ago) is very comparable to modern patterns.

--- SENTENCE 40 ---
For example, assemblages from both ancient and current times are dominated by species of the families Acanthuridae (surgeonfish) and Scaridae (parrotfish).

--- SENTENCE 41 ---
Similarly, analysis of molluscan assemblages from the two sites also suggests reliance on a few important species and very little change in community structure through time.

--- SENTENCE 42 ---
This suggests that the marine resources may have been utilized in a sustainable manner (Nagaoka 1993; Morrison and Addison 2008).

--- SENTENCE 43 ---
Key issues**

Potential of incorporating traditional management systems

Based on the information presented, workshop participants discussed the potential of incorporating more traditional types of management systems (i.e. village-based systems) into current management strategies.

--- SENTENCE 44 ---
Key Gaps

Evaluating effectiveness of traditional systems

One concern that was raised regarding this type of traditional management was the difficulty in accurately evaluating the effectiveness of systems from the past.

--- SENTENCE 45 ---
For example, participants questioned if previous patterns were due to an effective management regime, population control, or if there was merely an abundance of resources.

--- SENTENCE 46 ---
The inability to accurately evaluate the effectiveness of historical management systems remains a gap, but should not prevent them from being incorporated into modern regimes.

#### Limitations of archaeology

While the archaeological evidence of past fish assemblages provides a glimpse into historical fish populations and consumption patterns, archaeology has limitations, in terms of what is revealed.

--- SENTENCE 47 ---
To date, little archaeological data exists in American Samoa (only 2 sites) to make broad generalizations about the nearshore fishery throughout the islands.

--- SENTENCE 48 ---
Additionally, two major questions remain about the current assemblages: 1) Are the two datasets, which are separated by nearly a millennium, good representations of long term human reef exploitation in American Samoa? 2) Does the initial human exploitation of pristine coral reef environments in American Samoa show a different pattern?

#### Limited living memory

The fishery management regime and fishing practices prior to 50 years ago is not known because those events cannot be recalled by people who are alive now (limited living memory).

#### 1e.

--- SENTENCE 49 ---
Recommendations

As demonstrated in the presentations, very little is known about historical fishing pressure, but archeology is a tool that can help to establish a more definitive baseline.

--- SENTENCE 50 ---
Thus, there is a need to expand archeology studies and data collection to establish the baseline and gain a clearer picture of fishing through time (archeology, traditional, current), as well as to differentiate between the human and environmental changes which have altered the natural environment in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 51 ---
Present status of and management of fisheries in American Samoa 2a.

--- SENTENCE 52 ---
Objectives

The second session was designed to reach the following objectives:

- 1.

--- SENTENCE 53 ---
To quantitatively describe the present status of nearshore fisheries and habitat in American Samoa, and to contrast that with Independent Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 54 ---
To report on the current management in American Samoa and Independent Samoa
- 3.

--- SENTENCE 55 ---
To discuss the current status of the Community Based Fishery Management Program (CBFMP).

--- SENTENCE 56 ---
Seventeen presentations were given to provide information and overviews on the present status of and management of the fisheries in American Samoa and Independent Samoa (Table 2).

--- SENTENCE 57 ---
This included information from the National Parks Service of American Samoa on fisheries management challenges; American Samoa Coastal Management Program on the Pala Lagoon restoration; DMWR on the Community-Based Fishery Management Program, no-take MPA program, fish and coral data and regulations; Samoa Fisheries Division on Samoa's MPA program; NOAA-PIRO on the PLA process for Tula; and Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary on management plan review and expansion.

#### **2b.

--- SENTENCE 67 ---
Key outcomes**

Status of reef fish from fishery-independent (visual census) surveys across American Samoan Islands Research presented by R. Schroeder indicated an inverse relationship between island populations and fish biomass for the outer reef slope habitat.

--- SENTENCE 68 ---
In comparison to the other islands of American Samoa, Tutuila revealed the lowest total fish biomass, the lowest apex predator biomass, and the lowest snapper, grouper and soliderfish biomass.

--- SENTENCE 69 ---
Demography of a target reef fish in Tutuila Island: implications for fishery management In efforts to help understand the population dynamics of fish species in the islands, DMWR scientists are studying population information to understand spawning and recruitment of reef fish.

--- SENTENCE 70 ---
DMWR fish biologist, D. Ochavillo, presented information on current research of growth parameters, age-structures, and areas of recruitment of the bristletooth surgeonfish (C. striatus) at reef sites around Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 71 ---
The information presented indicates areas of main nurseries and spawning sites (specifically in the north-east and south-west locations of Tutuila).

--- SENTENCE 72 ---
This type of species-specific information can provide useful insights for management strategies (i.e. placement of MPAs).

--- SENTENCE 73 ---
Trends and patterns in American Samoa reef fish populations and coral reef fishery over the past three decades: an integrated analysis

DMWR Chief Fishery Biologist, M.Sabater, presented an analysis of data from multiple databases and reviews of technical reports which revealed a significant decline in shoreline fishing effort, resulting in constant catch lands and catch-per-unit effort (CPUE).

--- SENTENCE 74 ---
Reflecting the social and economic changes within the territory, results also indicated a non-dependence of the population on fishing (Sabater and Carroll 2009).

--- SENTENCE 75 ---
Status of bottomfish fishery in American Samoa

Bottomfish fishery trends, as presented by I. Tomanongi, indicate no changes in CPUE over the past 20 years.

--- SENTENCE 76 ---
Coral reef habitat issues in American Samoa

Coral reef habitat appears to be in good condition, as presented by DMWR Coral Reef Ecologist, D.Fenner.

--- SENTENCE 77 ---
Reefs in the territory seem quite healthy (specifically the forereef), compared to other areas of the Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 78 ---
Management plan review and site expansion of the Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary Management plan review and the site expansion process of Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary was presented by Fagatele Bay NMS Superintendent, G. Brighouse.

--- SENTENCE 79 ---
At the time of the workshop, the site expansion process was beginning.

--- SENTENCE 80 ---
Updates to the process can be found here [http://fagatelebay.noaa.gov/html/management\\_plan.html.](http://fagatelebay.noaa.gov/html/management_plan.html)

Brief summary reports were presented on current fishing regulations in American Samoa, by P. Eves, and on the CBFMP, by T. Toliniu.

--- SENTENCE 81 ---
Representatives from Samoa's Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries provided information on the current use and status of fishery management strategies in Independent Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 82 ---
Fishery regulations include size limits, seasonal closures, destructive fishing and live fish trade bans.

--- SENTENCE 83 ---
Additionally, the effective community-based fisheries management program imposes village-bylaws.

#### **2c.

--- SENTENCE 84 ---
Key issues**

Based on the presentations in this section, several key issues emerged.

--- SENTENCE 85 ---
Those identified by the participants include:

- Lack of agreement about status of reef fishery stocks in Samoa
- Need for more village monitoring and feedback for useful catch and effort information
- Need for meta-analysis of fisheries independent data
- Need to gain a better understanding of the strengths and limitations of WPACFIN (fishery-dependent data) and all data sources

#### **2d.

--- SENTENCE 86 ---
Key Gaps**

Several major data gaps were identified by the workshop participants that, if addressed, could enable more effective management decisions.

--- SENTENCE 87 ---
Key data gaps include:

- Habitat specific information (deep water fish refuges)
- A better understanding of gear shift and species composition in the catch
- A differentiation between the shallow and deep water component in the bottomfish fishery.
- Coral recruitment information

#### **2e.

--- SENTENCE 88 ---
Recommendations**

Based on workshop presentations and identification of specific data gaps that currently limit management, several key recommendations emerged from participant discussions.

--- SENTENCE 89 ---
First, participants agreed metaanalysis of fisheries independent data be performed.

--- SENTENCE 90 ---
This would also include more village monitoring and feedback regarding catch and effort as well as linking population information with nearshore habitat quality.

--- SENTENCE 91 ---
Secondly, participants identified several specific research recommendations including: expansion of coral recruitment studies, examination of habitat complexity and connection to fish community structure, and assessment of deep water refuges.

--- SENTENCE 92 ---
Lastly, participants agreed that a better understanding of the strengths and limitations of Western Pacific Fisheries Information Network (WPacFIN) and all available data sources need be gained, in order to more comprehensively assess the current state of the systems through the application of these data tools.

--- SENTENCE 93 ---
Part 3. Strategic Planning for Fishery Management.

--- SENTENCE 94 ---
The last day of the workshop was designed to help prioritize and plan for future management strategies.

--- SENTENCE 95 ---
As a starting point to stimulate a strategic way for the multiple management bodies to move forward, a list of general consensus statements on the status of American Samoa's coral reef fisheries was developed.

--- SENTENCE 96 ---
The intention of this consensus list was for the workshop participants to reach agreement on some aspects of the current state of the resources so that collaborative management actions could progress.

--- SENTENCE 97 ---
Listed below is the list of consensus items and a set of recommendations to address the data and management gaps (also referred to as the non-consensus issues).

#### **3a.

--- SENTENCE 98 ---
General consensus on the status of American Samoa's coral reef fisheries:**

- Reef fish population structure (abundance and fish size) around Tutuila has declined over the past years.

--- SENTENCE 99 ---
Populations of coral reef fish in the nearshore waters are composed primarily of small individuals.
- There has been a decline in overall fishing effort over the past 30 years and remains low.
- The near-shore habitat around Tutuila has been degraded.

#### **3b.

--- SENTENCE 100 ---
Recommendations to address non-consensus issues:**

- Assess the current status of deep water refugia on the outer banks of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 101 ---
Since the time of the workshop, DMWR has initiated research of offshore banks, to explore the status of deep water sea mounts through the use of drop cameras, as led by M. Sabater.
- Develop site selection for management targets (criteria for selecting MPAs).
- To better inform future management, data collection and type need to be enhanced.

--- SENTENCE 102 ---
Some suggested data collection include: expand creel survey to include north shore of Tutuila, identification of location where fishing occurs, quantify the exports of fish from Independent Samoa, quantify amount of subsistence harvesting.

--- SENTENCE 103 ---
- Analyze and integrate existing databases.

--- SENTENCE 104 ---
- Perform meta-analyses of fisheries independent data.

--- SENTENCE 105 ---
- Perform stock assessment for keystone species.

--- SENTENCE 106 ---
- Quantitatively define a ―sustainable fishery‖ for multispecies on a coral reef, to aid local management agencies in developing strategies.

--- SENTENCE 107 ---
Recommendations on ways to move forward for management:**

- Local enforcement effort needs improvement and enhancement.

--- SENTENCE 108 ---
- The opening/closing of the community based fishery management MPAs needs to be monitored in a consistent manner.
- DMWR's no-take MPA program should be linked (through effective communication and coordination) to the community-based efforts.
- A range of management tools are available for use in conserving, protecting and managing reef fish populations.

--- SENTENCE 109 ---
Management tools suggested by workshop participants include: MPAs, seasonal closures, species-specific bans, size limits, setting Total Allowable Catch limits (TACs), licensing, and FADs as an alternative livelihood.

--- SENTENCE 110 ---
While not one tool was agreed upon, participants did concur that the use of multiple tools is more important than agreeing to one tool.

#### Specific Data Needs

Workshop participants identified specific data needs and challenges in efforts to move forward.

--- SENTENCE 111 ---
These are organized in broad categories, as follows:

#### Community Fishery Management Program (CFMP)

--- SENTENCE 112 ---
Workshop participants agreed that CFMP villagers need to receive more education in basic fisheries management principles and concepts.

--- SENTENCE 113 ---
Currently, some village MPAs are opened for fishing and are quickly fished beyond a sustainable amount.

--- SENTENCE 114 ---
Additionally, the monitoring protocols and methods in the village CFMP needs to be enhanced.

--- SENTENCE 115 ---
This could also be incorporated into a more extensive training/education program provided to the village MPA communities.

--- SENTENCE 116 ---
#### Larval transport

Participants agreed that information about larval transport around the Territory, as well as the connectivity between American Samoa and Independent Samoa needs to be examined, as this could better inform monitoring protocols and future management strategies.

--- SENTENCE 117 ---
Regarding larval transport and connectivity between the islands, currently no information on source/sink areas is known.

--- SENTENCE 118 ---
Furthermore, details on the oceanographic and nearshore current information would be highly informative and is greatly needed.

#### Non-fishing impacts

Three non-fishing impacts on the coastal environment were discussed:

- 1.

--- SENTENCE 119 ---
Habitat degradation
  - While workshop participants could not reach a consensus on the status of the amount of habitat degradation that has occurred in the recent past, it was agreed that eutrophication, as well as sedimentation, appear to be issues concerning the quality of habitat in nearshore waters around Tutuila and may be causing habitat degradation.

--- SENTENCE 120 ---
The effects of eutrophication on the inner reef areas, as well as outer reef slope areas, need to be examined.
- 2.

--- SENTENCE 121 ---
Climate change
  - Workshop participants agreed that climate change impacts need to be more fully addressed and incorporated into local management strategies.

--- SENTENCE 122 ---
Specifically, participants were concerned how climate change will impact future fish harvests and ecosystem health
- 3. Land-based pollution Participants agreed that the land use practices and the impacts they have on the reef system (coastal development, direct and indirect impacts on nearshore and essential fish habitat) need to be evaluated

#### Enforcement

Attaining effective enforcement was identified as a significant challenge for management to achieve sustainable fish harvests.

--- SENTENCE 123 ---
Specifically, workshop participants discussed lack of personnel, lack of compliance, lack of effective regulations, public unawareness of regulations, and lack of information to immigrants as key enforcement challenges.

--- SENTENCE 124 ---
The need for community level training for managers and deputized enforcement agents was discussed as one key method to enhance enforcement.

#### Local Action Strategy (LAS)

--- SENTENCE 125 ---
At the time of the workshop, it was recommended that the discussions and outcomes be used in the LAS process review.

--- SENTENCE 126 ---
Since that time, the LAS has been developed, incorporating much of the information and key points pulled from the workshop.

--- SENTENCE 127 ---
However, based on the workshop outcomes, there remain outstanding recommendations to be included in the fisheries LAS, including:

- 1.

--- SENTENCE 128 ---
Coordinate the Fisheries LAS with the Land-based pollution LAS
- 2.

--- SENTENCE 129 ---
Develop community level training in management and enforcement
- 3.

--- SENTENCE 130 ---
Continue with the ―2 Samoa‖ effort, extension to archipelago

#### Recommendations for Future Workshops

In efforts to assist in the development of future workshops, constructive feedback was gathered from the attendees.

--- SENTENCE 131 ---
Participants voiced a common opinion that the ―consensus‖ items listed at the meeting on the last day were not summarized and developed in a participatory process, thus leading to an inaccurate representation of the issues discussed and agreed upon at the workshop.

--- SENTENCE 132 ---
There are several divergent views about the status of the reef fish community structure, and while the workshop was intended to reach some consensus on such issues, many of the local resources managers did not agree with the final list.

--- SENTENCE 133 ---
Contentious issues (i.e. overfishing, historical events/baselines) appear to remain, which can inhibit interagency collaboration, communication and development of management strategies.

--- SENTENCE 134 ---
Several suggested methods to encourage greater group participation were identified.

--- SENTENCE 135 ---
More workshop activities and small group discussions, with less formal presentations could facilitate greater group dialogue and outcomes.

--- SENTENCE 136 ---
For example, presentation information could be prepared as pre-workshop reading material, providing ample time for more discussions and small group activities.

--- SENTENCE 137 ---
Additionally, it was suggested that the objectives and expectations of the workshop be clearly articulated to the attendees to ensure more effective outcomes result.

--- SENTENCE 138 ---
Lastly, all participants agreed that the diversity of attendees (e.g. community members, representatives from Independent Samoa, local and regional resources managers, etc.) present at the workshop was beneficial.

--- SENTENCE 139 ---
While all participants agreed that community involvement is critical, for future workshops, it was suggested that community members be incorporated in a different capacity to avoid confusion, translation issues, and distractions from the agenda.

--- SENTENCE 140 ---
In the future, it may be more effective to engage community members on a separate day from the formal management meeting that is specifically designed for community engagement.

#### References

Sabater, M.G., B. P. Carroll.

--- SENTENCE 141 ---
Trends in Reef Fish Population and Associated Fishery after Three Millennia of Resource Utilization and a Century of Socio-Economic Changes in American Samoa.

# **Long-term marine resource use in American Samoa: Some preliminary thoughts**

# **Alex E. Morrison<sup>1</sup>**

<sup>1</sup>Department of Anthropology, University of Hawaii, 2424 Maile Way, Saunders Hall 346, Honolulu, Hawai'i 96822-2223, and International Archaeological Research Institute Incorporated 2081 Young St., Honolulu, Hawai'i 96816

# Introduction

Natural resource managers and conservation biologists are increasingly recognizing the contribution archaeological studies can make to contemporary wildlife management strategies (e.g., Lyman and Cannon 2004).

--- SENTENCE 148 ---
Specifically, archaeology offers a long term perspective of human-environmental interactions which is often missing from historical and modern resource management agendas and strategies.

--- SENTENCE 149 ---
Consequently, programmatic natural resource management agendas have much to gain from systematically integrating archaeological research into planning efforts.

--- SENTENCE 150 ---
Since archaeological studies often include multidisciplinary research on long term environmental processes such as paleoclimate, geomorphologic changes, and human impacts to ecological systems, insights into complex soci-ecological relationships forms the subject of a variety of research projects from such diverse areas of the world as the Hawaiian Islands to the Mediterranean region (e.g., Kirch 2007**;** Barton et al. 2004).

--- SENTENCE 151 ---
Recently, a number of successful multidisciplinary research programs have focused on generating long-term perspectives of the use of marine resources and marine ecosystems (e.g., Aswani and Allen 2009; Jones 2009).

--- SENTENCE 152 ---
These studies emphasize explicitly that the integration of archaeological data with both historical and modern studies generates more holistic and better informed sets of data from which to make contemporary management decisions.

--- SENTENCE 153 ---
Here I briefly outline two of these projects.

--- SENTENCE 154 ---
Next I provide a very brief introduction into the prehistoric use of fish and invertebrate species in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 155 ---
Studying long term trends in marine fish and invertebrates in the Lau group, Fiji Islands, Jones (2009) demonstrates the utility of multidisciplinary research including archaeological data, ethnographic interviews, and modern biological surveys.

--- SENTENCE 156 ---
Her results indicate that in general prehistoric Lauans relied predominately on small inshore fish taxa such as Acanthuridae, Serranidae, Scaridae, and Balistidae.

--- SENTENCE 157 ---
The archaeological datasets did not show any clear signs of human impacts to fish populations nor conclusive changes in fish diversity during the prehistoric era.

--- SENTENCE 158 ---
However, modern biologic and ethnographic data suggest that on several of the islands some fish and invertebrate species are currently in a state of decline.

--- SENTENCE 159 ---
Jones suggests that the relative intensity of human exploitation, as measured by island population size, land use, and degree of agricultural development, determines modern fish diversity and composition (Jones 2009: 639).

--- SENTENCE 160 ---
It is also noteworthy that some fish species appear to show resilience after 3,000 years of

exploitation suggesting that life history and demographic characteristics of these taxa may be important for the development of effective management strategies (Jones 2009:640)

A multidisciplinary approach integrating archaeology, ethnography, and biological data, has also been successfully conducted at Anaho Bay, Nuku Hiva, Marquesas Islands (Aswani and Allen 2009).

--- SENTENCE 161 ---
Aswani and Allen offer comparable results to those discussed by Jones (2009).

--- SENTENCE 162 ---
The biological survey data indicate that the reef is in a state of decline.

--- SENTENCE 163 ---
A benthic substrate study revealed the presence of high percentages of dead coral (Aswani and Allen 2009: 621).

--- SENTENCE 164 ---
The archaeological data suggest that overharvesting was not a significant factor during prehistory.

--- SENTENCE 165 ---
For example there are no indications of signs which are commonly used by archaeologists to infer human impacts, such as declining species size, increase in diet breadth, or decreases in the use of large bodied prey items (Aswani and Allen 2009: 622).

--- SENTENCE 166 ---
Sedimentation data derived from the archaeological study suggest that increasing slope wash may have lead to higher clay content in the inshore environment resulting in negative consequences to the inshore marine environments.

--- SENTENCE 167 ---
This process is thought to have started by the mid 17<sup>th</sup> century and continued into the late 19<sup>th</sup> century.

--- SENTENCE 168 ---
Based on the contemporary and archaeological datasets, the authors conclude that modern management aimed at restoration and protection of the coral reefs should focus on slope erosion control.

#### Long-term fish and marine invertebrate use in American Samoa

Data regarding prehistoric marine resource use in American Samoa are derived from two locations: Fatu-ma-futi, Tutuila Island and To'aga, Ofu Island.

--- SENTENCE 169 ---
Together the two datasets span an approximately 2900 year period (Hunt and Kirch 1997; Addison et al. 2008; Morrison and Addison 2008, 2009).

--- SENTENCE 170 ---
Fatu-ma-futi was excavated in several stages from 2003-2007.

--- SENTENCE 171 ---
The archaeological deposits date between approximately 1600 B.P. until around 200 years ago.

--- SENTENCE 172 ---
To'aga was excavated in the late 1980's.

--- SENTENCE 173 ---
Recent reinterpretation of the radiocarbon chronology suggests that To'aga was occupied from at least 2500 years ago until the late prehistoric or early historic era (Rieth et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 174 ---
Admittedly, the presence of data from only two archaeological sites significantly limits the ability to make meaningful generalizations across the archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 175 ---
However, a few important preliminary trends are worth discussion.

--- SENTENCE 176 ---
Results from both Fatu-ma-futi and To'aga suggest that fish use was focused primarily on inshore species.

--- SENTENCE 177 ---
Specifically, high abundances of Acanthuridae, Scaridae, and Serranidae, were found in both archaeological assemblages.

--- SENTENCE 178 ---
At Fatu-ma-futi, two herbivore families, Acanthuridae and Scaridae always make up the majority of each excavated layer (Morrison and Addison 2009).

--- SENTENCE 179 ---
Morrison and Addison (2009) applied a trophic level analysis to the assemblage.

--- SENTENCE 180 ---
The results did not show any substantial temporal changes in mean trophic level exploitation.

--- SENTENCE 181 ---
Examination of invertebrate remains from Fatu-ma-futi also indicates relative stability in species utilization through time (Morrison and Addison 2008).

--- SENTENCE 182 ---
The majority of species recovered from archeological contexts can be acquired in coral reef habitats.

--- SENTENCE 183 ---
These include species of the genera Turbo and Trochus.

--- SENTENCE 184 ---
Bivalve species were rare probably as a result of limitations in nearby habitats favoring these taxa.

--- SENTENCE 185 ---
At To'aga, bones from the fish families Acanthuridae, Serranidae, Scaridae, and Holocentridae make up the majority of the archaeological assemblages.

--- SENTENCE 186 ---
Nagaoka (1993) suggests that fish use at To'aga was stable through time with no indications of change in taxonomic structure or

species use.

--- SENTENCE 187 ---
Invertebrate remains exhibit the same pattern of stability.

--- SENTENCE 188 ---
These results led Hunt and Kirch to conclude that the remarkable resilience in marine resources through time may be related to the high natural productivity of the Samoan marine ecosystems.

!

--- SENTENCE 189 ---
The Samoan Islands with key archaeological sites discussed in the text.

--- SENTENCE 190 ---
# Discussion and Conclusion

The archaeological record of marine resource use in American Samoa comes from only two deposits: Fatu-ma-futi, Tutuila Island, and To'aga, Ofu Island.

--- SENTENCE 191 ---
These datasets that have been analyzed include approximately 2500 years of fish and marine invertebrate use.

--- SENTENCE 192 ---
Analytical results demonstrate that marine resource use was focused predominately on inshore areas and that species use was continuous and stable.

--- SENTENCE 193 ---
Archaeological datasets should be integrated with modern data acquired from biological surveys (e.g., Craig et al. 2007, Sabater and Tofaeono 2007).

--- SENTENCE 194 ---
A fundamental limitation of the results presented here is the lack of excavated archaeological deposits in American Samoa that contain fish and invertebrate remains.

--- SENTENCE 195 ---
Furthermore, future studies should attempt to locate and excavate archaeological deposits that are conclusively linked with initial colonization of the Samoa archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 196 ---
Morrison et al. (in press) and Rieth et al. (2008) have conducted a GIS based model for locating areas in American Samoa that are likely to contain evidence for early settlement (Figure 2-3).

--- SENTENCE 197 ---
Future archaeological research will focus on excavation of these locales including systematic recovery and analysis of fish and invertebrate remains.

!

--- SENTENCE 198 ---
Areas on Tutuila that have a high probability for early archaeological deposits.

!

--- SENTENCE 199 ---
Areas in the Manu'a Islands(Ofu,* Olosega, and Tau) that have a high probability for archaeological deposits.

--- SENTENCE 235 ---
<span id="page-24-0"></span>**Status of Reef Fish from Fishery-Independent (Visual Census) Surveys Across the American Samoan Islands**

# **Robert E. Schroeder<sup>1</sup>**

<sup>1</sup>NOAA Fisheries - PIRO-Habitat Conservation Division

# Introduction

The U.S. Territory of American Samoa (~14<sup>o</sup> S, 170<sup>o</sup>W) includes five islands and two atolls, with coral reefs typical of the surrounding central South Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 236 ---
A primary objective of this survey was to quantify spatial and temporal variability of coral reef fish (actual or potential fishery targets) relative to proximity to human population densities (proxy for fishing or other anthropogenic impacts).

--- SENTENCE 237 ---
Human population (and density) by island was two-orders of magnitude greater at Tutuila than at the next highest island (Ofu/Olosega, ~500 people); remote Swains was occupied by a small family and Rose, a National Wildlife Refuge, was uninhabited.

--- SENTENCE 238 ---
A related objective was to document the status of large fish species of special concern.

--- SENTENCE 239 ---
# Methods

Coral reef fish assemblages in American Samoa were surveyed biennially during February-March of 2002-2008 to assess their spatial and temporal variation in abundance.

--- SENTENCE 240 ---
Surveys were part of the multi-disciplinary coral reef ecosystem Resource Assessment and Monitoring Program (RAMP) conducted from large NOAA ships.

--- SENTENCE 241 ---
Two underwater visual monitoring methods along the outer reef slope were used: belt transect (for all diurnally active fish of all sizes), and towed-diver count (only for fish >50 cm).

--- SENTENCE 242 ---
At each site, three 25 m belt transect lines were set and all diurnally active fish along each side of the line were recorded for density estimates by species and individual length-class, for biomass conversion.

--- SENTENCE 243 ---
Towed diver surveys estimated density by species and size class along a 10 m swath, completely encircling the small islands during each visit, and alternating coverage around most of the reef perimeter of the large islands.

--- SENTENCE 244 ---
All islands in the American Samoa Archipelago were surveyed: Tutuila/Aunuu, Manua Group (Ofu/Olosega, and Tau), Swains, and Rose Atoll.

--- SENTENCE 245 ---
# Preliminary Results/Discussion

Total (all fish, all sizes) mean fish biomass and numeric density were highest at Rose and Swains (islands with none-very low human population density) and lowest at Tutuila/Aunuu (high population density) and Manua (low-moderate population density).

--- SENTENCE 246 ---
The low total fish abundance around Tutuila ranked similar with other major human population centers of the U.S. Pacific Islands (Guam-Saipan, Main Hawaiian Islands

--- SENTENCE 247 ---
[each with total reef fish <0.5 t/ha]), and was an order of magnitude below that of some remote uninhabited U.S. Pacific Islands.

--- SENTENCE 248 ---
Total herbivorous fish biomass (mainly surgeonfish and parrotfish) was highest at Rose, in part due to their enhanced abundance around the site of the 1993 ship grounding, where turf algae and

cyanobacteria continued to persist.

--- SENTENCE 249 ---
Similarly, biomass of large fish (mainly jacks, barracuda, snapper, and shark) was initially several times higher at Rose and Swains than at Tutuila/Aunuu and Manua, but decreased over time.

--- SENTENCE 250 ---
Fishery targets that dominated in the surveys were surgeonfish and parrotfish, except at Swains (characterized by high live coral cover), where grouper and variable schools of jacks were common.

--- SENTENCE 251 ---
Biomass of grouper, shark, and wrasse were generally low at all islands.

--- SENTENCE 252 ---
Numerically these same taxa were common, while shark and large grouper were few.

--- SENTENCE 253 ---
Regarding the status of other large species of special concern, humphead wrasse (Cheilinus undulatus) were present at all islands, mostly small-medium in size, except at Swains where several large individuals occurred.

--- SENTENCE 254 ---
Only two sightings of moderate-sized bumphead parrotfish (Bolbometopon muricatum) were recorded across all islands and survey years.

--- SENTENCE 255 ---
# Workshop Discussion

The main issue identified at the workshop was concern regarding the apparent decline in large fish over the past 6 yr at Rose and Swains, while the causality remains uncertain.

--- SENTENCE 256 ---
Information gaps identified during the workshop included: 1) lack of habitat specific information from shallow reef habitats (<6m) and deep reef habitats (>20 m; e.g., submerged barrier reef around Tutuila); 2) the relatively short temporal period of the survey (6 yr); and 3) the need to expand RAMP surveys to Independent Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 257 ---
Presentation of full results of this survey will be included in a manuscript (Schroeder, R.E., et al., [in prep.]

--- SENTENCE 258 ---
Reef fish densities inversely follow human population levels across the American Samoan Islands).

--- SENTENCE 259 ---
# <span id="page-26-0"></span>**Demographic analysis of a target reef fish species in Tutuila Island, American Samoa: implications for fishery management**

**Domingo Ochavillo <sup>1</sup> , Saolotoga Tofaeono <sup>1</sup> , Marlowe Sabater <sup>1</sup> and Elizabeth L. Trip <sup>2</sup>**

Population demographics of a number of species have been shown to vary in spatial scales ranging from 100's of m to 1000's of km (Gillanders 1995, Meekan et al. 2001, Gust et al 2002).

--- SENTENCE 260 ---
It is important to determine these scales of the variabilities since they may suggest geographic and/or reproductive isolation.

--- SENTENCE 261 ---
The spatial scales of demographic parameter differences can then be used in stock identification for fisheries assessment and management purposes (e.g. Begg et al., 1999).

--- SENTENCE 262 ---
The identification of these ‗unit stocks' is also important for fisheries management because potential yields might vary among sub-populations (Caddy 1975).

--- SENTENCE 263 ---
Moreover, the analysis of population structures is important in identifying and mapping nursery and spawning sites that can be prioritized for protection.

--- SENTENCE 264 ---
For marine protected area design, the scale of the differences can be used to determine the spatial scale in the network of sites.

--- SENTENCE 265 ---
The lack of information about the stock structure of, and connectivity among, adult populations has hindered MPA design (Walters and Bonfil 1999).

--- SENTENCE 266 ---
We have used a combination of size-at-age data with multiple population survey data on various spatial and temporal scales to derive insights on a reef fish population structure.

--- SENTENCE 267 ---
The size-at-age data were derived from fish collections and otolith-aging of fishes from all sizes of the bristletooth surgeonfish Ctenochaetus striatus.

--- SENTENCE 268 ---
These data were applied to underwater surveys conducted around Tutuila Island for two years.

--- SENTENCE 269 ---
The main objectives of this study were: (1) to determine the growth rate and the size-at-age relationships in the bristletooth surgeonfish, (2) to determine the spatial and temporal patterns in the distribution of its various post-settlement stages (recruits to spawners), (3) to identify substrata correlates to their distributions, and (4) to identify population structuring.

--- SENTENCE 270 ---
We have chosen the striated bristletooth surgeonfish C. striatus as a model species for this study because this is a highly common reef fish.

--- SENTENCE 271 ---
This fish is widely distributed in the Indo-Pacific from East Africa, Indonesia, Southwest Japan to the Great Barrier Reef, Micronesia and French

<sup>1</sup> Department of Marine and Wildlife Resources, P.O. Box 3730, Pago Pago, American Samoa 96799

<sup>2</sup> Ecology and Evolution, School of Biological Sciences, University of Auckland, Private Bag 92019, Auckland, New Zealand

Polynesia.

--- SENTENCE 272 ---
It inhabits reef flats, lagoons and seaward reefs to a depth of over 30 m. They occur over coral, rock, pavement or rubble substrata.

--- SENTENCE 273 ---
They swim singly or in small groups feeding mainly on detritus and sediments and secondarily on blue-green algae, diatoms, and small invertebrates.

--- SENTENCE 274 ---
In American Samoa, C. striatus is the dominant species in terms of abundance and biomass (Sabater and Tofaeono 2007).

--- SENTENCE 275 ---
It is targeted by various fishing gears and constitutes a significant part of the over-all fisheries.

--- SENTENCE 276 ---
The bristletooth surgeonfish accounts for up to 16% of the landed catch of spearfishing, the major form of fishing.

--- SENTENCE 277 ---
The growth and size-at-age data analyses indicate that this surgeonfish has a fast growth rate and high longevity similar to other surgeonfishes.

--- SENTENCE 278 ---
They attain almost 90% of their maximum length within the first two and can live up to 36 years.

--- SENTENCE 279 ---
Size attains an asymptote in the fifth year and apparently most of the fish energy is spent on reproduction after this point.

--- SENTENCE 280 ---
The analyses of sizeage classes indicated significant differences in the spatial and temporal distribution of recruits, juveniles, pre-adults, and adult fishes (> 5 years old).

--- SENTENCE 281 ---
Recruitment seems to be concentrated in a few reef areas.

--- SENTENCE 282 ---
There was a general trend of highest densities in the northeast and southwest reef areas of the island.

--- SENTENCE 283 ---
We postulate major recruitment and accumulation of different ages in these areas to explain these patterns of distribution.

--- SENTENCE 284 ---
We also postulate that recruitment is driving this population structuring.

--- SENTENCE 285 ---
Because of limited post-settlement movement, this leads to accumulation of older individuals in these same areas.

--- SENTENCE 286 ---
We hypothesize a larval transport model wherein pathways end in these reef areas.

--- SENTENCE 287 ---
Moreover, the differences in the distribution of age-size indicated the occurrence of weak but significant ‗subpopulations'.

--- SENTENCE 288 ---
It is proposed that fishery management and the spatial design of marine protected areas would have to include the protection of these ‗nursery' and ‗spawning' sites, and the inclusion of potential ‗subpopulations'.

--- SENTENCE 289 ---
The major findings and detailed analyses of the study are contained in a report submitted for publication (Ochavillo et al., submitted).

--- SENTENCE 290 ---
This study was funded by the Office of Federal Assistance, under the Sportfish Restoration Grant, to the American Samoa Government.

--- SENTENCE 291 ---
We would like to thank Mr. Ray Tulafono, Director of the Department of Marine and Wildlife Resources (DMWR), and the department's administration staff for facilitating our field work.

--- SENTENCE 292 ---
We also thank the following for assisting our fieldwork: Alama Tua, Ekueta Schuster, Mika Letuane, TJ Letalie and Hymal Vaimaona.

# <span id="page-29-0"></span>**2007 Status of bottomfish fishery in American Samoa**

# **Ioane Tomanogi<sup>1</sup>**

<sup>1</sup>Department of Marine and Wildlife, American Samoa

# Summary

American Samoa's bottomfish fishery was relatively bigger between 1982 and 1985 when this fisheries was new and booming (Figure 1).

--- SENTENCE 308 ---
In 1988 a decline in bottomfish fisheries occurred as many skilled and full-time commercial fishermen converted to trolling.

--- SENTENCE 309 ---
Profits and revenues in bottomfishing suffered devastating blows from four separate hurricanes; Tusi in 1987,Ofa in February of 1990 and Val in December of 1991 and Heta in January of 2004 (Figure 2).

--- SENTENCE 310 ---
The gradual depletion of newly discovered banks and migration of many fishermen into other fishing vendors resulted in the decline of landings in the mid 1980s (Figure 1).

--- SENTENCE 311 ---
Fuel prices have gradually soared in the past four years causing yet another strain in the bottomfish fisheries (Figure 3).

--- SENTENCE 312 ---
The average price of bottomfish has also declined due to the shift of local bottomfish demand to imported bottomfish competing closely with local prices.

--- SENTENCE 313 ---
In 2004, 60% of coolers imported from the independent state of Samoa on the Lady Naomi Ferry are designated for commercial purposes; from the Commercial Invoice System 50% of these coolers are bottomfish.

--- SENTENCE 314 ---
During 2007, a total of 27 local boats landed an estimated 39,281 pounds of both commercial and recreational bottomfish in the territory.

--- SENTENCE 315 ---
Revenues from the commercial fishery this year was estimated around \$87,025, with all catch being sold locally.

--- SENTENCE 316 ---
The CPUE for 2007 ( 12.5 lb/triphr) shows a 74% increase from that of last year.

--- SENTENCE 317 ---
Effort (hours and trips) has been increasing since 1998 as some of the Alias that normally troll and/or longline perform bottomfishing when trolling and longline prices and catches decline.

--- SENTENCE 318 ---
Effort (hours and trips) has been increasing due to the increase of Alias performing bottomfishing.

--- SENTENCE 319 ---
Regarding some of the SFA amendments: Commercial Bottomfish Landings and Revenues statistics for American Samoa is presented in Figure 2.

--- SENTENCE 320 ---
No bottomfish Recreational trip was recorded this year.

--- SENTENCE 321 ---
Recreational fishing is more associated with the pelagic fisheries and usually never occur in this fishery.

--- SENTENCE 322 ---
There was no chartered bottomfish trip during this year and no bottomfish by catch was recorded this year (Table 3).

--- SENTENCE 323 ---
In the Preliminary Draft of EFH, Amendment for Bottomfish, WPRFMC Feb.1998, the approximate MSY estimate for American Samoa [196 nautical miles 100-fathom isobath] is estimated at 79,000 lbs.

--- SENTENCE 324 ---
Only about 50% was reached this year.

--- SENTENCE 325 ---
Indicators derived from current data do not dictate immediate management response at this time.

--- SENTENCE 326 ---
The following selected annual statistics dating back to 1982 provides a brief historical snapshot of American Samoa's bottomfish fishery

#

--- SENTENCE 365 ---
Introduction

Bottomfishing utilizing traditional canoes by the indigenous residents of American Samoa has been a subsistence practice since the Samoans settled into the Tutuila, Man'ua and Aunu'u islands.

--- SENTENCE 366 ---
It was not until the early 1970's that the bottomfish fishery developed into a commercial scheme utilizing motorized boats.

--- SENTENCE 367 ---
A government subsidized program, called the Dory Project, was initiated in 1972 to develop the offshore fisheries into a commercial venture, and resulted in an abrupt increase in the fishing fleet and total landings.

--- SENTENCE 368 ---
In 1982, a fisheries development project aimed at exporting high-priced deep-water snappers to Hawaii caused another notable increase in bottomfish landings and revenues.

--- SENTENCE 369 ---
Between 1982 and 1988, the botttomfish fishery comprised as much as 50% (by weight) of the total commercial landings.

--- SENTENCE 370 ---
Beginning in 1988, the nature of American Samoa's fisheries changed dramatically with a shift in importance from bottomfish fishing towards trolling.

--- SENTENCE 371 ---
The dominant (by weight of fish landed) fishing method for the past years was longlining, however, more Alias are now performing bottomfish fishing.

--- SENTENCE 372 ---
During the early 1980's, fisheries data was collected from the bottomfish fishery by interviewing only commercial vessels.

--- SENTENCE 373 ---
In the current Offshore Creel Survey on Tutuila that started on October 1, 1985, commercial, subsistence and recreational domestic fishing boats landing catch in five designated areas were interviewed and their catch recorded.

--- SENTENCE 374 ---
Every two weeks a total of seven weekdays and one weekend of regular morning and evening shift surveys are conducted, with two days of regular office hours where opportunistic interviews are collected.

--- SENTENCE 375 ---
In the past three years, the sampling period was increased and modified to encompass boats that come in earlier or after the normal sampling period.

--- SENTENCE 376 ---
Two DMWR samplers based on Tau and Ofu collect fisheries data from the Manu'a islands fleet and one in Aunuu.

--- SENTENCE 377 ---
Boat-based fishing in American Samoa used to be mainly trolling and/or bottomfish.

--- SENTENCE 378 ---
In the past years, record longline landings were recorded with revenues around the one million-dollar mark.

--- SENTENCE 379 ---
Bigger foreign boats are entering the local fisheries but these are rigged for longlining and more of these are expected to enter the territory's longline fishery.

--- SENTENCE 380 ---
Limited entry options have been initiated to check this increase.

--- SENTENCE 381 ---
The bottomfish fishery of American Samoa was typically commercial overnight bottomfish handlining using skipjack as bait, on 28-30 feet aluminum/plywood Alias.

--- SENTENCE 382 ---
Imported bottomfish from the independent state of Samoa help satisfy the demand for bottomfish however it weakens the local bottomfish fishery.

--- SENTENCE 383 ---
The adverse effects of four hurricanes that struck American Samoa can be seen throughout the various trends depicted in this report.

--- SENTENCE 384 ---
Recent changes in the fishery and improvements in the Offshore Creel Survey requires modifications to algorithms used to process the data for this report.

--- SENTENCE 385 ---
Hence the continuous improvements to DMWR's data processing systems by WPacFIN staff.

--- SENTENCE 386 ---
# Recommendations

#### **2004 Recommendation:**

- 1. DMWR should enhance internal development through training for staff in order to minimize chances of misidentification.

--- SENTENCE 387 ---
Incorporate market data from Market surveys into the database.
- 3.

--- SENTENCE 388 ---
Include Import data from Western Samoa into the database for further enhancement of this report.

--- SENTENCE 389 ---
#### **Status of 2004 Recommendation:**

1.

--- SENTENCE 390 ---
The DMWR biologist hired in 2003 compiled a local bottomfish identification charts that the technicians are able to take with them to help with identification.

--- SENTENCE 391 ---
This same

biologist whom is responsible for the offshore creel survey, has participated in many surveys, to further illustrate to technicians the proper methods of data collecting, fish identification, measuring and surveying.

--- SENTENCE 392 ---
In 2004 Market survey was on hol
- 3.

--- SENTENCE 393 ---
Incorporation of Market data raises concern of double sampling; Offshore Creel Survey and Markey Survey.

--- SENTENCE 394 ---
Import data from western Samoa should only be used as a comparison tool.

--- SENTENCE 395 ---
Including import data to the territory's database proves irrelevant to our goals in the fisheries.

#### **2005 Recommendation:**

- 1.

--- SENTENCE 396 ---
Technicians require intensive fish identification training, requesting council to compose training workshop for all Western Pacific members to standardize data.
- 2.

--- SENTENCE 397 ---
Establish a centralized fish market for fishermen and businessmen.
- 3. DMWR should mandate fishermen and store owners to allow technicians to conduct interviews.
- 4.

--- SENTENCE 398 ---
FoxPro data collecting system should enter data using scientific names and not use common names or local names.
- 5.

--- SENTENCE 399 ---
A data sampling port should be established near the boat docks to not only centralize interviews but to maximize the quantity of interviews.

#### **Status of 2005 Recommendation:**

- 1.

--- SENTENCE 400 ---
No progress, still in the working process.
- 2.

--- SENTENCE 401 ---
A fish market is to be built in place of the old market.

--- SENTENCE 402 ---
This will be a Government Agencies collaboration effort.
- 3.

--- SENTENCE 403 ---
In the working process.
- 4. No progress.

--- SENTENCE 404 ---
Common/local names of species are still being used for data entry.
- 5.

--- SENTENCE 405 ---
The existing Marina near DMWR is the only access to most Alias therefore a port at the boat docks is unnecessary.

--- SENTENCE 406 ---
There are also problems with incorporating a port in the existing upgrading plans for the Marina.

- 1.

--- SENTENCE 407 ---
Technicians require intensive fish identification training, requesting council to compose training workshop for all Western Pacific member to standardize data.
- 2.

--- SENTENCE 408 ---
FoxPro data collecting system should enter data using scientific names and not use common names or local names.

--- SENTENCE 484 ---
American Samoa Total Bottomfish Landings

**Interpretation:** Landings have varied throughout the years as a result of shifts in the fisheries, natural events and modernization.

--- SENTENCE 485 ---
From 1982-1985 bottomfish landings was at the highest ever due to it being a new fishery.

--- SENTENCE 486 ---
The steep drop from 1985 to 1987 occurred as a result of the introduction of longlining, a much lucrative fishery compared to bottomfish.

--- SENTENCE 487 ---
Hurricane Tusi in 1987, Ofa in 1990, Val in 1991 and Heta in 2004 caused severe damages to the fishery that echoed in the following years.

--- SENTENCE 488 ---
In addition, locals are turning to imported bottomfish mainly because they get more for their dollar.

--- SENTENCE 489 ---
The increase in 1994 was due primarily to improved sampling on Tutuila and increased efforts by the Tutuila highliners.

--- SENTENCE 490 ---
The 1998 decline mirror the 33% decrease in the number of boats participating.

--- SENTENCE 491 ---
The affects of Hurricane Heta is inevitable in 2004 as landings decreased significantly compared to the previous year.

--- SENTENCE 492 ---
The trend continued to 2006 with an overall 60% decline in pounds landed compared to the year 2005.

--- SENTENCE 493 ---
In 2007 landings increased by 79% compared to 2006, due to improved sampling on Tutuila, increased efforts by Tutuila highliners, and the increase in the number of boats participating by 14%.

--- SENTENCE 494 ---
In past years, landings have declined steadily as fuel price rise, however landings this year increased despite this fact.

--- SENTENCE 495 ---
Offshore Creel Survey Database

**Calculation:** Bottomfish landings for 1982-84 were calculated by adjusting the sampled Tutuila data by the calculated annual percent coverage of the fleet, and then adding the similarly adjusted Manu'a landings.

--- SENTENCE 496 ---
The landings from 1986 to Present were calculated by expanding the Offsfore Creel Survey Data for Tutuila for the species listed in Table 1.

--- SENTENCE 497 ---
The sampled Manu'a landings were adjusted by adjusting for the monthly perecent coverage of the fleet and added to the Tutuila data.

--- SENTENCE 498 ---
Since the Offshore Creel Survey started in October 1, 1985, The first nine month of

the 1985 landings were calculated as it was in 1982-84 and the last three months of the 1985 landings were calculated as it is now.

!

--- SENTENCE 499 ---
American Samoa Estimated Commercial Bottomfish Landings

**Interpretation:** Commercial landings mirror the total fishery's low catches in recent years compared to the robust 1982-1986 period.

--- SENTENCE 500 ---
The peak in 1983 portrays the high prices of deepwater snappers exported to Hawaii.

--- SENTENCE 501 ---
The trough in 1987 can be attributed to effects of the 1987 hurricane.

--- SENTENCE 502 ---
The February 1990 and December 1991 hurricanes contributed largely to the decreased landings and subsequently a decrease in revenues in the early 1990s.

--- SENTENCE 503 ---
Relative to total landings, commercial landings decreased even more substantially in 1989, because the percent of the catch sold by bottomfish fishermen dropped from an average of about 97% in 1982-88 to 78% in 1989., Increased efforts in 1994 produced a notable increase in revenues and no major changes in commercial landings have been recorded since then.

--- SENTENCE 504 ---
A dramatic drop in commercial landings trailing as far as four years prior 2006, is a result of gradual commercial shift of demand catered by imported fish, hurricane effects, gas prices, loss of experienced fishermen, many preferring trolling and longlining, and data sampling inconsistencies.

--- SENTENCE 505 ---
Commercial landings for this year has increased by 81%, resulting in a 81% gain in total adjusted revenue in 2007 due to many preferring bottomfishing then trolling or longlining.

--- SENTENCE 506 ---
Offshore Creel Survey Database

**Calculation:**

--- SENTENCE 507 ---
A relatively complex set of algorithms are used to estimate the commercial landings from estimates of total landings created by the creel survey data expansion system.

--- SENTENCE 508 ---
In short the percent sold by species and by fishing method is calculated annually and multiplied by the estimated total landings by that method for that year.

--- SENTENCE 509 ---
For 1982-85 sampling was conducted on the commercial fleet only (which included nearly all of the fishing boats), whereas since the

1985 creel sampling has covered all boats (commercial and recreational).

--- SENTENCE 510 ---
Analysis of creel data for 1986-87 indicates that over 98% of the landed bottomfish was being sold.

--- SENTENCE 511 ---
Therefore is it believed to be valid to compare commercial data for years prior to 1986 to creel survey totals for years since 1986.

--- SENTENCE 537 ---
American Samoa Estimated Bottomfish Hours and Trips

**Interpretation:**

--- SENTENCE 538 ---
The sharp decline in the bottomfish landings since 1986, noted in Fig.1 is mirrored in this figure by a sharp decline in the level of effort expended in that fishery.

--- SENTENCE 539 ---
Rather than indicating a problem with the resource , this decline depicts an actual trend of commercial boat owners and fishermen seeking other more lucrative and stable work.

--- SENTENCE 540 ---
The 1994-1996 estimated efforts were greater than those for the 1990-93 period due to the highliners increased efforts, with some boat owners employing teams (usually 2-3 fishermen) in continuous shifts during good weather.

--- SENTENCE 541 ---
In 1997 and 1998 the number of boats participating in this fishery dropped significantly (see Figure 4) resulting in the notable declines in the number of trips and hours fished that period.

--- SENTENCE 542 ---
The 1999 increase in effort can be attributed to some Alias that normally longline and troll, doing occasional bottomfishing.

--- SENTENCE 543 ---
The increase in hours and trips in 2004 in bottomfishing is due to the slowing down of longlining.

--- SENTENCE 544 ---
However, in 2006, a 76% decline in hours and 69% decline of trips compared to 2004 is noted.

--- SENTENCE 545 ---
With so many vessels in the bottomfish fishery this year, there is that much more hours invested in the effort to create a profit efficiently.

--- SENTENCE 546 ---
The hours have increased by 72% and trips have increased by 69% compared to the previous year.

--- SENTENCE 547 ---
Offshore Creel Survey Database

**Calculation:**

--- SENTENCE 548 ---
The annual estimated hours spent bottomfishing is calculated by dividing the annual total bottomfish catch by the average CPUE (pounds per hour) from trips doing only bottomfish fishing.

--- SENTENCE 549 ---
The annual estimated number of trips is calculated by dividing the estimated annual hours by the average length of a bottomfish fishing trip.

--- SENTENCE 550 ---
The average length of a bottomfish fishing trip (not shown) is calculated by using only trips which exclusively bottomfished and for which the trip length was recorded.

--- SENTENCE 551 ---
The total hours fished from those trips is then divided by the number of trips.

--- SENTENCE 552 ---
Recorded hours are trip hours.

!

--- SENTENCE 553 ---
American Samoa Annual Estimated Number of Boats Landing Bottomfish

**Interpretation:**

--- SENTENCE 554 ---
The decline in the fishery since 1985-86 is reflected by a decline in the number of boats participating in it.

--- SENTENCE 555 ---
The 1987 hurricane caused the loss of the whole Manu'a fleet, plus some of the Tutuila fleet.

--- SENTENCE 556 ---
Several Boats that contributed to the 1989 bottomfish annual landings did not land any bottomfish in 1990, due to much needed boat repairs and their participation in non-bottomfish chartered trips.

--- SENTENCE 557 ---
About 90% of the domestic fishing fleet was affected by the December 1991 hurricane, hence the slight decline in 1992.

--- SENTENCE 558 ---
The increase in 1993 is due mainly to the re-entry to this fishery of a few boats after repairs, trips by two 14-foot vessels that didn't bottomfish in 1992, and the entry of one new Alia into the sampling area.

--- SENTENCE 559 ---
A few new Alias were bought from western Samoa and entered the fishery in 1995-1996.

--- SENTENCE 560 ---
The continued increase in the number of bottomfish Alias electing to longline, attracted by the relatively higher revenues obtained mainly from albacore sold to the canneries, is reflected in the significant drop in the number of boats bottomfishing in 1998.

--- SENTENCE 561 ---
In 2004, a 44% increase in boats landing any bottomfish species suggesting many unsuccessful small scale longlining Alias returning to bottomfishing.

--- SENTENCE 562 ---
For this year, four more Alias returned to bottomfishing, making it 27 boats instead of 23 from last year.

--- SENTENCE 563 ---
Offshore Creel Survey database

**Calculation:**

--- SENTENCE 564 ---
The annual estimate of the number of boats in the bottomfish fishery is obtained from the data base by counting the unique boats sampled during the year which landed any bottomfish species regardless of fishing method.

!

--- SENTENCE 565 ---
American Samoa Average Price of Bottomfish

**Interpretation:** Prices were generally higher between 1982 and 1984 during the exportation of high-priced deepwater snappers to Hawaii.

--- SENTENCE 566 ---
After this period, inflation-adjusted local prices have generally been stable.

--- SENTENCE 567 ---
Prices of locally caught bottomfish are generally higher than imported fish, and could have been even higher had the local markets not been flooded by imported fish, which are usually of lower quality.

--- SENTENCE 568 ---
The only imported bottomfish in 1994 were from western Samoa and these were sold at an average price of \$1.67/lb, this year it is \$3.07/lb (2006 \$2.42).

--- SENTENCE 569 ---
Imported bottomfish (mainly from western Samoa) have always helped in meeting the demand for bottomfish.

--- SENTENCE 570 ---
Since 1999 there has been a general increase (16% in 1999 and 48% in 2004) in pounds of fish (miscellaneous bottomfish and pelagics) imported from western Samoa creating a (increase supply) price drop in the markets.

--- SENTENCE 571 ---
For this year, 6% increase of price is recorded compared to that of last year (\$2.30 to \$2.45).

--- SENTENCE 572 ---
Offshore Creel Survey database

**Calculation:**

--- SENTENCE 573 ---
The average price of all bottomfish species combined is calculated by dividing total bottomfish revenue by total sold weight.

--- SENTENCE 574 ---
The inflation-adjusted price is calculated by multiplying the unadjusted annual average price by the annual calculated consumer price index (CPI) for American Samoa using the current year as base.

!

--- SENTENCE 575 ---
American Samoa Annual Bottomfish CPUE

**Interpretation:**

--- SENTENCE 576 ---
The initial increased CPUE in 1983 and 1984 occurred during the intense fishing of some new fishing grounds for deepwater snappers for export to Hawaii.

--- SENTENCE 577 ---
A relatively high number of boats and local fishermen participated in the fishery during this period.

--- SENTENCE 578 ---
The decline in 1985 and 1986 might be expected following the ardent harvesting of the limited fishing grounds.

--- SENTENCE 579 ---
Reasons for the CPUE peak in 1988-89 are unknown.

--- SENTENCE 580 ---
The decline in CPUE from 1989 to 1991 can be partially attributed to a combination of some new inexperienced fishermen entering the fishery and the exit of experienced and full-time commercial fishermen.

--- SENTENCE 581 ---
CPUE has essentially remained stable during 1990-1992, increased for a few years and was relatively stable in 1996-1998.

--- SENTENCE 582 ---
Bottomfishing techniques and gear have generally remained the same in the past years with the Alias being the highliners since the early 1970's.

--- SENTENCE 583 ---
The 1996 high CPUE estimates (and most probably the 1988-89 CPUE increase) can be attributed mainly to improved sampling and may also be related to favorable environmental conditions.

--- SENTENCE 584 ---
2005 marks the lowest CPUE ever recorded.

--- SENTENCE 585 ---
A combination of many factors contribute to the drop, such as inexperience fishermen, everyone fishing in the same banks (more effort - less fish), data collection inconsistencies, hurricane aftermath effects and shift in fish preference from bottomfish to reef fish.

--- SENTENCE 586 ---
However for this year, there is a 25% increase of the CPUE due to increased efforts and consistent sampling in Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 587 ---
Offshore Creel Survey database

**Calculation:** CPUE is calculated using only trips in which only the bottomfish method was used and trip hours were recorded.

--- SENTENCE 588 ---
The average is calculated by using each CPUE from each trip as an observation and dividing by the number of trips.

!

--- SENTENCE 589 ---
American Samoa Average Inflation-Adjusted Revenue Per Trip Landing Bottomfish

**Interpretation:**

--- SENTENCE 590 ---
There has been no notable change in revenues since 1990.

--- SENTENCE 591 ---
The distance between these two lines reflects the relative importance of bottomfish species in the total catch whenever any bottomfish are landed.

--- SENTENCE 592 ---
The prominent importance of bottomfish between 1982 and 1985 occurred during the targeting of deepwater snappers (mainly **Etelis** and **Prisitipomoides**) for export to Hawaii.

--- SENTENCE 593 ---
Bottomfish fishing was also the more profitable method of fishing during that period.

--- SENTENCE 594 ---
The relative importance of bottomfish has generally been declining since 1985 as most of the full-time commercial fishermen quit this fishery with the remaining opting for trolling and lately, longlining.

--- SENTENCE 595 ---
The supply of locally caught bottomfish has been supplemented by bottomfish imported from Western Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 596 ---
These values are higher in this year's report than they were in previous year's reports because the trips included are only those that sold their catch commercially to be more consistant with the revenue/trip values from other islands which are based on sales receipt data.

--- SENTENCE 597 ---
Offshore Creel Survey database

**Calculation:**

--- SENTENCE 598 ---
The average revenue per trip for all species is calculated by summing the revenues of all sales for any trip which landed any bottomfish species and sold all or part of their catch commercially, and dividing by the number of such trips.

--- SENTENCE 599 ---
The average bottomfish revenue per trip is calculated from those same trips by summing the sales of only bottomfish species and dividing by the number of trips that sold their catch.

--- SENTENCE 600 ---
Figure 7 plots the inflation-adjusted bottomfish and all species revenue per trip for the period 1982-2007.

# <span id="page-45-0"></span>**The state of the coral reef habitat in American Samoa, 2008**

# **Douglas Fenner, Ph.D.**<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup>Dept. Marine & Wildlife Resources, American Samoa

#

--- SENTENCE 624 ---
Abstract

Most indices of reef health indicate that the reefs of Tutuila, outside of the harbor, Vatia and Fagasa bays, are relatively healthy.

--- SENTENCE 625 ---
Coral cover is currently slightly higher than the whole Pacific and South Pacific averages and much higher than the Caribbean average.

--- SENTENCE 626 ---
Coral has actually increased slightly over the last four years instead of declining as in all major areas of the world's reefs, including the Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 627 ---
A much higher percentage of coral is alive than the average for the Pacific, Indonesia, and the Philippines.

--- SENTENCE 628 ---
Much of the reefs are dominated by coralline algae and coral, with much smaller amounts of macroalgae and turf algae.

--- SENTENCE 629 ---
Brown macroalgae is rare on most of the reefs.

--- SENTENCE 630 ---
There are relatively few introduced species and no invasive species.

--- SENTENCE 631 ---
Water is relatively clear and low in nutrients and so filter feeders and bioeroders are rare.

--- SENTENCE 632 ---
Some streams release much more mud and silt in the water than would happen without people, but most of it is quickly carried off the reef and the few damaged areas are relatively small.

--- SENTENCE 633 ---
On the other hand, coral cover is not as high as the average on pristine reefs or as was estimated on Tutuila before the 1978 crown-of-thorns outbreak.

--- SENTENCE 634 ---
That outbreak killed 90% or more of all corals, and was the largest disturbance in the past 50 years or more.

--- SENTENCE 635 ---
It was primarily a natural event, as were the hurricanes and tsunami that have damaged the reefs.

--- SENTENCE 636 ---
The reefs have a very long history of recovering from natural disturbances, or else they would no longer be here.

--- SENTENCE 637 ---
There is some evidence of damage from non-point pollution, but the effects if any are small enough that they are difficult to replicate.

--- SENTENCE 638 ---
The available literature indicates that the loss of live coral tissue has relatively small effects on fish communities, mostly in community structure as some fish species decrease and others increase.

--- SENTENCE 639 ---
The available evidence shows a 50% increase in fish abundance on Tutuila after the 1978 crown-of-thorns outbreak killed nearly all corals.

--- SENTENCE 640 ---
There are larger effects when the corals are physically damaged.

--- SENTENCE 641 ---
Hurricanes in the last 50 years have done relatively little damage.

--- SENTENCE 642 ---
Reefs in the harbor have been heavily damaged, by dredging and filling that obliterated reefs, and nutrients from the canneries.

--- SENTENCE 643 ---
Little is left of the once lush reefs in the harbor, though they were a small part of all the reefs of Tutuila and even smaller part of all the territorial reefs.

--- SENTENCE 644 ---
Mangrove areas are small, and the only seagrass species is so small it is not habitat for anything.

--- SENTENCE 645 ---
But the literature indicates that relatively few reef fish species use mangroves or seagrass in the Pacific for nurseries, and fish diversity in American Samoa is as high as would be expected from biogeography.

--- SENTENCE 646 ---
Reef flat areas are said to be small, but they are actually larger than the reef slope area, which is the only area the reef flat recruits are likely to go to.

--- SENTENCE 647 ---
Banks 1-3 miles offshore have abundant fish, yet no shallow areas and are separated from the reef flats by long distances of sand that juvenile fish are very unlikely to traverse, indicating that most reef fish do not require shallow juvenile habitat.

--- SENTENCE 648 ---
So far there is no direct evidence that

mangrove, seagrass, or shallow reef habitat is limiting for any reef fish species, and it cannot explain the pattern that the largest fish species are all rare while there are abundant small fish species.

--- SENTENCE 649 ---
Fishing can easily explain that pattern, and there is eyewitness testimony that recounts the near-extirpation of one large species by fishing.

--- SENTENCE 650 ---
# Introduction

This paper describes coral reef habitat primarily around Tutuila, consider reef health, how the reef, the role of factors that are threats or have caused impacts on our coral reefs, and whethere mangroves, seagrass, and shallow reef serve as nurseries for reef species.

--- SENTENCE 651 ---
For background information on the coral reefs of American Samoa, see Wells (1988), Craig et al. (2005), Sabater and Tofaeono (2006, 2007), Whaylen and Fenner (2006), Fenner (2008a,b), Fenner et al. (2008), Birkeland et al. (2008), and Brainard et al. (2008), Craig (2009) and Fenner (2009).

--- SENTENCE 652 ---
American Samoa consists of four high volcanic islands (Tutuila, Aunu'u, Ofu, Olosega, and Ta'u) and two low islands, (Swains and Rose Atoll).

--- SENTENCE 653 ---
The high islands are part of the Samoan hotspot volcanic chain, which also includes the two larger islands of (independent) Samoa, and a variety of seamounts, all in a roughly east-west chain.

--- SENTENCE 654 ---
To the east of Ta'u is a seamount named Vailululu'u which is an active submarine volcano.

--- SENTENCE 655 ---
The chain is similar to the Hawaiian chain, with the youngest islands to the east starting with Ta'u, and increasing age with Ofu-Olosega, Tutuila, Upolu, and Savai'i.

--- SENTENCE 656 ---
Tutuila is about 1.5 million years old.

--- SENTENCE 657 ---
It is the largest island in American Samoa and hosts most of the 69,000 people that live in the territory.

--- SENTENCE 658 ---
Because it has most of the people and infrastructure, access to the reefs is easiest around Tutuila, and the reefs there have been studied more than the other smaller islands.

--- SENTENCE 659 ---
Reefs there are also likely to be the most impacted by humans.

--- SENTENCE 660 ---
# Reef Zonation

!

--- SENTENCE 661 ---
[](_page_46_Figure_5.jpeg)

Reef Flat Figure 1.

--- SENTENCE 662 ---
Shematic diagram of reef zones around Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 663 ---
Not to scale, and not proportional.

--- SENTENCE 664 ---
From Fenner et al. 2008

The diagram above shows the main features of Tutuila's coral reefs diagrammatically.

--- SENTENCE 665 ---
It is not proportional and is only schematic.

--- SENTENCE 666 ---
The main zones of the reefs are the reef flats, reef slope, and banks, while the shelf is primarily sand and rubble, and the deep escarpment and talus slope are too deep for reef building corals.

--- SENTENCE 667 ---
The reef flat also has backreef pools at a few locations.

--- SENTENCE 668 ---
# Reef Flat

The following discussion of the reef flats and other reef zones applies to reefs outside of bays and to most reefs inside bays.

--- SENTENCE 669 ---
Reefs in a few bays such as the harbor have quite different situations than other reefs and will be considered later.

--- SENTENCE 670 ---
All reefs on the north shore of Tutuila are inside bays.

--- SENTENCE 671 ---
This may be because periodic hurricanes rip corals off the substrate outside bays and deposit them in deeper water.

--- SENTENCE 672 ---
Such storm damage limits reefs in the main Hawaiian Islands to bays.

--- SENTENCE 673 ---
Tutuila has several distinct zones in the coral reef that surrounds it, as seen in Figure 1.

--- SENTENCE 674 ---
The most visible zone is the reef flat, which is just slightly deeper than the low tide level.

--- SENTENCE 675 ---
The reef flat begins at the shoreline and extends out from the shore perhaps 20-200 meters.

--- SENTENCE 676 ---
It is nearly continuous on the south side of the Tutuila, except along the ―Tafuna plains,‖ on the southwest section of the island.

--- SENTENCE 677 ---
