The Tafuna plains are the largest nearly flat land in American Samoa, and are composed of relatively young lava flows.

--- SENTENCE 678 ---
From just west of the airport to Leone, there is no reef flat development, except inside Fagatele and perhaps Larson's Bay.

--- SENTENCE 679 ---
But west of Leone the reef flat extends on to the western end of the island.

--- SENTENCE 680 ---
On the north shore, there are only reef buildups and reef flats inside bays.

--- SENTENCE 681 ---
Reef flats currently have lower live coral cover than reef slopes, with just over 20% live coral cover on the outer reef flat, and about 5-10% on inner reef flats (Figure 2; Fenner, 2009).

--- SENTENCE 682 ---
For perspective, compare that to the current coral cover in the Caribbean which averages 8% on reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 683 ---
Reef flats are dominated by algal turf, with coralline algae more common on the outer part near the crest, while the inner part has more dead coral rubble.

--- SENTENCE 684 ---
It should be kept in mind that there is lots of variation between sites, and these are statements about what the average is like.

--- SENTENCE 685 ---
Further, long term observers have noted that there has been more coral cover in the past.

--- SENTENCE 686 ---
In recent years, the lowest tides each year often cause the death of corals on the reef flats that have grown up the most from the level of the reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 687 ---
The lowest tides of the year appear to act like a lawnmower, killing only the coral that projects upward the farthest.

--- SENTENCE 688 ---
This effect of low tides is indeed the reason why the reef flat is so flat, and is at the low tide level.

--- SENTENCE 689 ---
This appears to all be a natural process, but it has had the effect of reducing coral cover on the reef flat significantly since about 1998.

--- SENTENCE 690 ---
On the other hand, recruitment of the table coral Acropora hyacinthus has increased in recent years, with a pulse of recruitment about 2005.

--- SENTENCE 691 ---
Recruitment was highest on the outer few meters of reef flat and upper reef slope above about 2 meters depth.

--- SENTENCE 692 ---
Recruitment was light but noticeable at many locations, but heavy at Fagasa, where tables only 30 cm in diameter are already growing into each other or over each other, and a dense community will result if mortality does not intervene.

--- SENTENCE 693 ---
Reef flats generally have low coral cover, unless the flat is much deeper than the low tide level.

!

--- SENTENCE 694 ---
Cover on the inner and outer reef flats of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 695 ---
All subsequent figures are from Fenner, 2009 unless stated otherwise.

--- SENTENCE 696 ---
# Back Reef Pools

On the south side of Tutuila, there are a series of pools in the reef flat that are man-made.

--- SENTENCE 697 ---
These are pools that were dug out to provide material to build the platform for the airport runway, or to add land to the village.

--- SENTENCE 698 ---
Such pools are at the airport, Coconut Point (Nu'uuli), Faga'alu, Gataivai (near Utulei), and Alofau.

--- SENTENCE 699 ---
Large pieces of reef rock in rows, and sharp edges to the deeper pools, are evidence of the quarrying process; quarrying marks are easily visible in remote images.

--- SENTENCE 700 ---
These pools now have patches of coral in them, dominated by the finger coral (Porites cylindrica) and the staghorn coral (Acropora muricata = formosa).

--- SENTENCE 701 ---
Only a portion of the substrate is covered with coral in any one location, usually less than half, with most of the rest being covered with sand.

--- SENTENCE 702 ---
A portion of the staghorn in each pool is dead, usually still standing though there are some rubble patches where they have collapsed.

--- SENTENCE 703 ---
Coral diversity in the pools is very low, and the suite of species is different from that on the reef flat, which is different in turn from that on the crest, which is in turn different from that on the slope, which is in turn different from that on the banks.

--- SENTENCE 704 ---
Diversity in all of the other zones is higher than in the backreef pools.

--- SENTENCE 705 ---
There are much shallower pools that appear to be natural at Fagaitua and Onesosopo.

--- SENTENCE 706 ---
At Fagaitua, the outer part of the pool is dominated by the staghorn A. muricata.

--- SENTENCE 707 ---
The same species is present at Onososopo along with a variety of others.

--- SENTENCE 708 ---
On Ofu and Olosega, there are back reef pools that appear to be natural.

--- SENTENCE 709 ---
They are located on the south side of Ofu and on the southwest side of Olosega in front of Olosega village.

--- SENTENCE 710 ---
The south side of Ofu has a series of pools, varying in depth from about 3 m to perhaps 10 cm deep or less.

--- SENTENCE 711 ---
The coral diversity in these pools is very high, on the order of 80-100 species (Craig et al. 2001).

--- SENTENCE 712 ---
They are not dominated by the species seen in the Tutuila pools, though those species are

present.

--- SENTENCE 713 ---
Massive Porites species are common and some are large.

--- SENTENCE 714 ---
The Olosega pool is less than one meter deep most places, and has fairly low coral cover and diversity, and is dominated by finger coral (P. cylindrica).

--- SENTENCE 715 ---
# Crest

The reef flat ends at what is called the ―reef crest,‖ which is where waves break.

--- SENTENCE 716 ---
However, here it is no higher than the reef flat as its name might suggest.

--- SENTENCE 717 ---
The reef crest is generally a very dangerous place for humans, as American Samoa is near the center of an ocean that covers nearly half the planet with no protection from oceanic swells from any direction.

--- SENTENCE 718 ---
The reef crest has extensive coralline algae cover, the highest of any zone.

--- SENTENCE 719 ---
It also has good coral cover most places.

--- SENTENCE 720 ---
At Coconut Point, Nu'uuli, coral cover was measured at about 18% cover (Figure 3).

--- SENTENCE 721 ---
Encrusting calcareous algae increases strongly toward the outer part of the crest there, replacing turf and reaching 70% cover at the outer part of the crest.

--- SENTENCE 722 ---
Other crest locations may have different amounts of coral and algae.

--- SENTENCE 723 ---
Most corals on the crest are small branching species, such as Acropora nana, Pocillopora damincornis and Pocillopora verrucosa.

--- SENTENCE 724 ---
Wave splash appears to be able to keep corals on the reef crest alive by keeping them wet and cool during extreme low tides when corals on the reef flat may be killed by desiccation and/or high temperatures (Anthony and Kerswell, 2007).

--- SENTENCE 725 ---
If the extreme low tide occurred during a period of very calm water, however, crest corals could be killed as well as reef flat corals.

--- SENTENCE 726 ---
Coral reefs are very patchy, and the Tutuila reef flats are no exception.

--- SENTENCE 727 ---
There are a series of patches of the bushy species Acropora aspera on the reef flat between Faga'alu and Nu'uuli.

--- SENTENCE 728 ---
This is the species which is often the first affected by low tide events, since it grows highest on the reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 729 ---
The reef flat in Leone was covered with a thin encrusting grey sponge, near 100% cover, in 2007, but by 2008 much of it has disappeared.

--- SENTENCE 730 ---
The sponge seemed to be associated with murky water and what may have been poor water quality.

--- SENTENCE 731 ---
The reef crest is broken many places by what are locally called ―avas.‖ These are deep channels that appear in the outer reef flat usually as a narrow and relatively shallow channel, which expands in width outward towards the ocean and increases in depth.

--- SENTENCE 732 ---
They often are floored by large rubble, which extends from the ava downward on the reef slope.

--- SENTENCE 733 ---
Avas are places with very strong outward flowing water currents produced by wave action on the crest.

--- SENTENCE 734 ---
Waves breaking on the crest pump water over the crest onto the reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 735 ---
The water then flows across the reef flat to the ava, and back out the ava.

--- SENTENCE 736 ---
Water flow on the crest and reef flat is primarily the periodic surge from the waves and can be quite strong.

--- SENTENCE 737 ---
The unidirectional water movement increases in speed as the ava is approached.

--- SENTENCE 738 ---
Water speed is highest near the surface of the ava and less deeper near the substrate, as the water coming rapidly off the reef flat is at the surface.

--- SENTENCE 739 ---
The speed of these currents are strongest when wave heights are greatest and when the tide is highest, because that is when waves pump the most water onto the reef flat.

!

--- SENTENCE 740 ---
Benthic cover at the Coconut Point, Nu'uuli reef crest.

--- SENTENCE 741 ---
*

# Reef Slope

Beyond the crest is the reef slope.

--- SENTENCE 742 ---
The reef slope descends from the low tide level at the crest, down to about 20-30 m (shallower in bays on the north side) where it joins a shelf that appears nearly flat.

--- SENTENCE 743 ---
In some areas the bottom gradually increases in slope from the crest down the reef slope, but in some places the reef crest is flat and ends in a vertical or near vertical drop at the upper reef slope.

--- SENTENCE 744 ---
The angle of the reef slope is variable, but might average around 45 degrees.

--- SENTENCE 745 ---
Small scale spur and groove formations are most common in shallow water on the south side, where wave surge is greatest most of the time.

--- SENTENCE 746 ---
However, the large scale spur and grooves seen in deeper water on some reefs elsewhere appear to be absent here.

--- SENTENCE 747 ---
Several different monitoring programs have recorded quantitative data around Tutuila, with similar results (Figure 4).

--- SENTENCE 748 ---
Algae, primarily encrusting coralline algae, have the most cover, followed by live hard coral, which averages just under 30% for the different data sets.

--- SENTENCE 749 ---
These data were collected using different methods and different sites yet produce similar results.

!

--- SENTENCE 750 ---
Benthic cover recorded by several different monitoring programs around Tutuila (Fenner et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 751 ---
Reef slopes commonly are dominated by encrusting calcareous algae on the south side, but have less of these algae on the north side.

--- SENTENCE 752 ---
Most of the reef slopes have a very ―clean‖ appearance with pink or purple encrusting calcareous algae and little or no macroalgae or sediment.

--- SENTENCE 753 ---
In the Territorial Monitoring Program (TMP), macroalgae are defined as fleshy macroalgae, with parts that are significantly larger than the fine filaments and thin wires that form turf.

--- SENTENCE 754 ---
Turf is also shorter than most macroalgae, turf usually being one cm or less in height.

--- SENTENCE 755 ---
There are more of the encrusting calcareous algae in shallow water than in deep water.

--- SENTENCE 756 ---
In some places, encrusting calcareous algae can be seen to dominate the shallow part of the slope, and there is a fairly sharp demarcation where it ends and is replaced by other organisms.

--- SENTENCE 757 ---
On the south side it is often replaced in deeper water by coral or a mixture of coral and the green macroalgae, Halimeda.

--- SENTENCE 758 ---
Halimeda is a green macroalgae that produces calcium deposits inside the segments called ―thalli‖ which look a bit like small leaves, so it actually contributes to the calcium of the reef.

--- SENTENCE 759 ---
In some areas there is also a fair amount of a calcareous red alga, Peyssonnelia bornetii which forms brown flat plates (Fenner, 2008b), and/or a branching red alga, Cheilosporum spectabile.

--- SENTENCE 760 ---
These three are the only macroalgae species that are all common (other than small amounts of branching coralline algae that looks like toothpicks).

--- SENTENCE 761 ---
Brown macroalgae is very rare on slopes, the author has yet to see any Sargassum, for instance.

--- SENTENCE 762 ---
On the north side at Fagasa, there is a significant amount of smooth reef rock with brown terrestrial sediment caught in turf, giving a dirty look.

--- SENTENCE 763 ---
Reefs on the north side have more turf than on the south side, and reefs on the south side have more visible crustose calcareous algae than on the north (Figure 5; Whaylen and Fenner, 2006; Sabater and Tofaeono, 2007; Fenner, 2008a; Houk and Musberger, 2008).

--- SENTENCE 764 ---
These patterns may be driven by crustose calcareous algae growing best where it is not covered with either sediment or filamentous algae (Dawson, 1961; Steneck, 1986; 1987; Fabricius and De'ath, 2001).

--- SENTENCE 765 ---
The south side gets more steady wave surge for at least half of the year as trade winds blow steadily out of the east (the island is at an angle so wind and waves from the east strike the south side).

--- SENTENCE 766 ---
So crustose calcareous algae is most abundant near the crest where the wave surge is greatest, and on the south side where wave surge is greatest.

--- SENTENCE 767 ---
On the slope, the area dominated by crustose calcareous algae can be seen to extend farther down the slope from the crest.

--- SENTENCE 768 ---
However, this is the visible crustose calcareous algae, but there it also extends under other algae such as Halimeda, under ledges and in holes in low light conditions, and it even grows under some of the turf algae.

--- SENTENCE 769 ---
It often dominates understory communities (Connell, 2003).

!

--- SENTENCE 770 ---
North-South comparisons of benthic cover at 8 m depth.

--- SENTENCE 771 ---
Similar differences have been documented in the Territorial Monitoring Program every year, beginning with 2005.

!

--- SENTENCE 772 ---
Coral life forms in transects.

--- SENTENCE 773 ---
The coral community on upper and middle reef slopes is dominated by encrusting Montipora colonies, though there are moderate amounts of column and branching lifeforms as well (Figure 6).

--- SENTENCE 774 ---
This combined with the dominance of encrusting calcareous algae means that the dominant form of many areas of the reef is encrusting.

--- SENTENCE 775 ---
Encrusting formations may not provide as many hiding

places for fish as other types of coral such as branching.

--- SENTENCE 776 ---
However, on many of our reefs, particularly on the south side, the surface is still rugose with many holes, because the reef material near the surface of the reef is largely composed of coral skeletons partly bonded together, with an abundance of holes between the pieces of skeleton.

--- SENTENCE 777 ---
In addition, column and branching coral lifeforms contribute to the quality of the reef as fish habitat by adding hiding places.

--- SENTENCE 778 ---
Coral cover and the cover of other biota have been quite steady since 2005, with a slight increase in coral cover as of 2008 (Figure 7).

!

--- SENTENCE 779 ---
Trends in mean benthic cover around Tutuila in recent years at 8 m depth

Quantitative surveying shows that coral cover increases going away from the island across the reef flat and onto the reef slope, but once on the reef slope coral cover is steady at around 30% cover down the slope to at least 18.5 m depth (Figure 8).

--- SENTENCE 780 ---
It should be noted that the data for 4.5 m and 18.5 m is based on south side sites and just one north side site; the results could change somewhat when the rest of the data is collected.

--- SENTENCE 781 ---
Turf on the reef flat is replaced by crustose calcareous algae on the slope, which is greatest near the top of the reef slope and decreases with increasing depth down the slope.

!

--- SENTENCE 782 ---
Benthic cover across the reef flat and down the slope.

--- SENTENCE 783 ---
Coral reef communities are notoriously patchy, and the reefs of American Samoa are no exception.

--- SENTENCE 784 ---
At Faga'alu, the upper reef slope down to perhaps 12 m or more depth is covered with dead coral rubble covered with encrusting calcareous algae.

--- SENTENCE 785 ---
Below that there is high coverage of live healthy-looking plating coral.

--- SENTENCE 786 ---
The upper slope looked just like it does in 2008 when first observed by the author in 2004.

--- SENTENCE 787 ---
It consists of round sticks that appear to be from some type of Acropora such as Acropora nobilis or A. abrotanoides.

--- SENTENCE 788 ---
The date and cause of the death of this coral is unknown, but Acropora is among the most vulnerable genera to hurricanes, crown-of-thorns starfish, bleaching, and disease.

--- SENTENCE 789 ---
On the southeast coast of the island at Fagaitua Bay, there are areas with large amounts of Lobophyllia, below about 12 m depth.

--- SENTENCE 790 ---
Many colonies are several meters in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 791 ---
On the southwest of the island at Leone, the upper reef slope is heavily dominated by the encrusting Acropora species that has plating edges, Acropora crateriformis.

--- SENTENCE 792 ---
To the west of that area, still in Leone, is an area of mixed Acropora hyacinthus tables and staghorns that appears very similar to corals in pictures before they were eaten by crown-of-thorns in 1978 (C. Birkeland, personal comm.).

--- SENTENCE 793 ---
In the middle of the shoreline of Fagatele Bay there is a pure stand of Acropora hyacinthus tables that have very high coral cover around 4 m depth.

--- SENTENCE 794 ---
In Larson's Bay at about 8 m deep there is a pure stand of Merulina scabricula, and at about 15 m depth in Fagatele Bay there is a huge mound of Pachyseris rugosa which is fairly rare elsewhere around the island.

--- SENTENCE 795 ---
It is the largest mound of that species the author has ever seen anywhere, by far.

--- SENTENCE 796 ---
At the mouth of Vatia Bay there is an area of diverse Acropora with very high cover.

--- SENTENCE 797 ---
Coral species diversity in American is relatively high.

--- SENTENCE 798 ---
The author has identified about 260 species of coral so far, but it is clear that additional species remain to be identified.

--- SENTENCE 799 ---
The diversity is much higher than Hawaii, where only about 55 species are known (Fenner, 2005).

--- SENTENCE 800 ---
Corals respond to some degree to their habitat, some having a restricted depth range while others have wide depth ranges.

--- SENTENCE 801 ---
Differences in coral communities are strong between the major reef zones, such as reef flat, backreef pool, and slope.

--- SENTENCE 802 ---
Within a major reef zone such as the reef slope, there is often zonation, with some coral species more common in shallow water, others in deep water,

and so on.

--- SENTENCE 803 ---
The primary variables driving this zonation are likely to be light levels and water turbulence.

--- SENTENCE 804 ---
Water turbulence and light levels decrease with increasing depth on the reef slope, and water turbulence decreases with distance from the reef crest on the reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 805 ---
The reefs slopes of American Samoa, broadly speaking, have reasonably good coral cover, high crustose calcareous algae cover, and low macroalgae cover.

--- SENTENCE 806 ---
Most of the macroalgae present is Halimeda, a green calcareous alga which is generally natural and not a problem.

--- SENTENCE 807 ---
There is also some Peysonnellia, which is a red calcareous alga that is also not a problem.

--- SENTENCE 808 ---
Brown algae are rare on the reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 809 ---
The brown alga Dictyota has appeared in recent years on the slope in Vatia Bay.

--- SENTENCE 810 ---
Paul Brown reports some other patches of Dictyota in the National Park, and suggests that at some places it may be seasonal.

--- SENTENCE 811 ---
Some crustose calcareous algae attract coral larvae (e.g., Hayward and Negri, 1999), and although at least one species can grow over living coral (and is present in American Samoa), high levels of crustose calcareous algae and low levels of brown macroalgae are signs of a healthy reef.

--- SENTENCE 812 ---
A recent study of trends in coral cover in the Pacific (Bruno and Selig, 2007) reported mean coral cover for the Pacific to currently be 25% with an average of 23% for the South Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 813 ---
Tutuila currently has higher coral cover than the Pacific average, the South Pacific average, and the Caribbean (Figure 9).

--- SENTENCE 814 ---
Two surveys of the levels of coral cover on pristine reefs found that live coral cover was most commonly in the range of 35-40% (McManus et al. 1995; Miller et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 815 ---
The average coral cover recorded in the Caribbean in the 1975 was 55%, and in the Pacific in 1978 it was 42%, though there are data from relatively few sites back in those times, and sites were not picked randomly.

--- SENTENCE 816 ---
Most of the recent data in the Bruno and Selig (2007) study came from Reef Check.

--- SENTENCE 817 ---
Reef Check instructions include directing data collectors to select the best local reefs.

--- SENTENCE 818 ---
The sites in all studies of Tutuila for which data are currently available, as shown in Figure 4, were also picked non-randomly.

--- SENTENCE 819 ---
Although they all report similar coral cover values, they may all reflect similar biases in choosing sites, that is, picking better than average sites.

--- SENTENCE 820 ---
The Key Reef Species project picked sites initially for consideration without knowledge of their coral cover, and then chose sites randomly for study from that set of sites, so it was closer to random site selection than the other programs, yet found very similar coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 821 ---
The TMP selection process excluded non-reef sites, that is where there was no calcium buildup, like the north shore sites outside of bays, but the Key Reef Species Program included them, yet still produced a similar average coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 822 ---
Live coral cover averages about 30% on Tutuila slopes, so it is a bit less than is typical of pristine coral reefs (Figure 10).

--- SENTENCE 823 ---
Wass (1982) estimated coral cover at several sites in 1978 just before crown-of-thorns starfish ate nearly all the coral around Tutuila and throughout the archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 824 ---
The average of his estimates is 63% coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 825 ---
This is unusually high compared to the surveys of pristine reefs.

--- SENTENCE 826 ---
It is possible that Tutuila had an unusually high natural coral cover, or it could be that those sites were chosen to be particularly good sites, or it could be that the corals were particularly abundant compared to a long term average at Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 827 ---
The Coral Reef Ecosystem Division of NOAA conducts towboard surveys as well as transects in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 828 ---
They record lower coral cover on the towboard surveys than on transects (Brainard et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 829 ---
This may be in part because the transects were not picked randomly, but it may also be in part due to biases in estimating coral cover from a moving towboard.

--- SENTENCE 830 ---
People may not select low coral cover areas for transects if they select transect sites.

--- SENTENCE 831 ---
This is particularly true on the north side, where most of the coastline is outside bays, and has low coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 832 ---
It seems quite likely that Wass's sites were not chosen randomly and had higher coral cover than would an average of sites chosen truly randomly.

--- SENTENCE 833 ---
Still, 63% is higher than virtually any individual site is now, and there is little doubt that coral cover was higher then than now.

--- SENTENCE 834 ---
A traditional view that reefs are static communities and that the way they were first seen is the way they always were, has been largely replaced by the view that reefs are naturally damaged by disturbance events which reduce live coral cover, and spend most of their time recovering from such events.

--- SENTENCE 835 ---
The last previous crown-of-thorns outbreak was in 1938, so perhaps corals had recovered to an unusually luxuriant state.

--- SENTENCE 836 ---
Randomly chosen pristine reef systems would have an average coral cover lower than the peak coral cover that a system could achieve in an unusually long period between disturbances, because many of the sites that go into the average would be recovering from recent disturbances, and have low coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 837 ---
All things considered, it seems highly likely that coral cover was higher before the 1978 crown-of-thorns outbreak than now, but it is also likely that an average of randomly chosen sites would have given a lower percentage than 63%, and the average cover over many years may well have been even lower, perhaps in the 35-40% range.

!

--- SENTENCE 838 ---
Comparison of present mean coral cover in Tutuila with the South Pacific (Secretariat of the Pacific Community, 2005), the Pacific and South Pacific (Bruno and Selig, 2007), and the Caribbean (Gardener et al. 2007).

!

--- SENTENCE 839 ---
Comparison of present mean coral cover on Tutuila with mean coral cover in the Caribbean in 1975 (Gardiner et al. 2007), the Pacific in 1978 (Bruno and Selig, 2007) and American Samoa in 1978 (Wass, 1982).

--- SENTENCE 840 ---
Côté et al. (2006) have reported the average rate of change of coral cover, based on data from many studies, for a variety of areas, including the Pacific, Indian Ocean, Red Sea, Caribbbean, and global reefs.

--- SENTENCE 841 ---
The total change in coral cover in the four years of the Territorial Monitoring Program was an increase of 15%.

--- SENTENCE 842 ---
Figure 11 compares that change with average changes in different areas reported by Côté et al. (2006).

--- SENTENCE 843 ---
Coral cover has been decreasing globally, though that decrease has been smallest in the Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 844 ---
On Tutuila, however, coral cover has increased (as seen in Figure 7).

--- SENTENCE 845 ---
The sample size of just four years for Tutuila is small, and most of the increase occurred in just the last year.

--- SENTENCE 846 ---
However, the Key Reef Species program has also recorded increases in coral cover (D. Ochavillo, personal comm.), supporting the view that this increase is real.

--- SENTENCE 847 ---
This information is consistent with the view that reefs are relatively healthy on Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 848 ---
Voluminous world-wide data in 18 categories from Reef Check summarized by Wilkinson (2006), gives the Pacific Islands one of the highest scores for healthy reefs, only slightly below that of Australia.

--- SENTENCE 849 ---
It gives Atlantic reefs the lowest score (tied with the Arabian Gulf).

--- SENTENCE 850 ---
This is consistent with the other lines of evidence that indicate that Pacific reefs are relatively healthy, and Atlantic (mainly Caribbean) reefs are among the most degraded.

--- SENTENCE 851 ---
This also supports the view that Tutuila reefs are relatively healthy.

!

--- SENTENCE 852 ---
The rate of change of coral cover reported for regions by Wilkinson (2006) and reported in the Territorial Monitoring Program for Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 853 ---
The slight increase in coral cover in the last four years may not be typical of the reefs over long periods of time, and indeed the data from the Birkeland and Green long-term monitoring program show strong increases and decreases in coral cover over the last three decades, as shown in Figure 19 below (reproduced from Craig et al. 2005).

--- SENTENCE 854 ---
Yet even that graph shows an increase in coral cover from the first points to the last point.

--- SENTENCE 855 ---
Further, if the initial coral cover of 11-25% on that graph were compared with the present 30% coral cover, there would still be an increase in coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 856 ---
Another index of reef health that has been proposed comparing the amount of ―good‖ cover consisting of coral plus crustose calcareous algae, and the amount of ―bad‖ cover, consisting of turf plus macroalgae.

--- SENTENCE 857 ---
Trends in these combined variables can be seen in Figure 12.

--- SENTENCE 858 ---
CCA plus coral has high cover (over 60%), and turf plus macroalgae has low cover (about 20%), and both of these were stable over the four years of the program so far.

--- SENTENCE 859 ---
This is consistent with the view that the reefs are relatively healthy.

!

--- SENTENCE 860 ---
Trends in combined benthic categories.

--- SENTENCE 861 ---
An important measure of reef health is the proportion of corals that are alive.

--- SENTENCE 862 ---
There is very little dead coral around Tutuila currently.

--- SENTENCE 863 ---
The PROCFish program based at SPC in New Caledonia surveys coral reefs in many Pacific countries.

--- SENTENCE 864 ---
They have devised a ―live coral index‖ which is the ratio of live coral to all coral (live and dead).

--- SENTENCE 865 ---
A reef where most corals are alive is healthy compared to a reef where most corals are dead.

--- SENTENCE 866 ---
Their index averages about 55% for reef slopes in the South Pacific (Secretariat of the Pacific Community, 2005), but the index averages about 93% here in Tutuila (Figure 13).

--- SENTENCE 867 ---
In the Philippines, 48% of 844 reefs had an index below 50% (Gomez et al. 1994).

--- SENTENCE 868 ---
In Indonesia, unpolluted reefs had an average of index of 75% while polluted reefs averaged 48% (Edinger et al. 1998).

--- SENTENCE 869 ---
Thus, Tutuila has a higher (better) live coral index than the South Pacific average, the Philippines, and Indonesia.

!

--- SENTENCE 870 ---
The "live coral index" average for the South Pacific compared to Tutuila*.

--- SENTENCE 871 ---
Coral species diversity has been found to correlate with known sources of land pollution (e.g., Edinger et al. 1998).

--- SENTENCE 872 ---
Peter Houk has done several surveys around Tutuila, designed to detect and monitor the effects of non-point pollution on the reef slope (Houk et al. 2005; Fenner et al. 2008; Houk and Musburger, 2008).

--- SENTENCE 873 ---
Because non-point pollution runoff is highly variable with storm runoff and other variables, measuring pollutants directly in seawater on the reef slope may miss pulse runoff events.

--- SENTENCE 874 ---
Significant negative correlations were consistently found between three watershed descriptors (size, percent disturbed land and human population) and three biological measures (coral species richness, community evenness and the percentage of substrate favorable for coral growth).

--- SENTENCE 875 ---
However, attempts to replicate these results using data from the sites in the Territorial Monitoring Program were unsuccessful (Fenner, 2008a,b, 2009).

--- SENTENCE 876 ---
The variable combinations used, however, were not the same as in the Houk studies, so an exact replication still needs to be attempted.

--- SENTENCE 877 ---
However, this does illustrate that the results are not generalizable to other similar variable combinations.

--- SENTENCE 878 ---
If the results are real, they do not appear to be powerful effects.

--- SENTENCE 879 ---
American Samoa has low abundances of most filter-feeding and bioeroding organisms on the reefs.

--- SENTENCE 880 ---
Plantivorous fish are moderately abundant, dominated by damsels such as Chromis sp., however Anthias are relatively rare (except in Swains).

--- SENTENCE 881 ---
Sponges on the reef slopes are uncommon to rare, and small, although one community of abundant sponges lives in deep water within the harbor on soft bottom where plankton densities are high (Paul Brown, personal comm.;

--- SENTENCE 882 ---
Fenner et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 883 ---
Fan worms (Sabellidae) are rare, and Christmas tree worms (Serpulidae) are small and rare.

--- SENTENCE 884 ---
Boring clams, other filter feeding clams, crinoids, black coral, azooxanthellate soft corals and ascidians are generally rare, small, and/or cryptic.

--- SENTENCE 885 ---
The author has seen the siphon holes of boring clams on only two corals in six years of diving.

--- SENTENCE 886 ---
Boring sponges have not yet been found, nor is there any evidence of the white dust of expelled microscopic chips of carbonate expelled from boring sponges (which is very obvious on some reefs such as the Florida Keys).

--- SENTENCE 887 ---
In contrast, a small filter-feeding oyster, Saccostrea cucculata, is abundant on the intertidal shoreline in Pago Pago Harbor, where plankton is abundant.

--- SENTENCE 888 ---
Sea urchins, some of which are powerful bioeroders, are uncommon on the outer reefs, and bumphead parrotfish (Bolbometopon muricatum), which are powerful bioeroders, are rare enough that only about one individual per year is currently seen among all scientists combined.

--- SENTENCE 889 ---
Planktivorous fish and filterfeeding species (some of which are bioeroding) are bioindicators for the plankton that they feed on, which in turn is a bioindicator for nutrients.

--- SENTENCE 890 ---
These bioindicators all point towards low plankton levels on outer reef slopes, though high plankton levels in the harbor, which is confirmed by the low visibility and green color of the harbor.

--- SENTENCE 891 ---
Visibility levels on outer reef slopes average around 25 m, with higher levels on outer banks.

--- SENTENCE 892 ---
Visibility on the outer reef slopes may be limited largely by suspended sediments; during periods of heavy swell visibility can be less, and suspended sediments may be obvious, even accumulating in a few seconds on a slate.

--- SENTENCE 893 ---
For more on sedimentation, see the segment on that topic below.

--- SENTENCE 894 ---
Bioerosion removes carbonate from the reef, so it reduces the rate at which the reef grows, or if it exceeds the rate at which corals and algae add carbonate to the reef it can actually remove reef or destroy it.

--- SENTENCE 895 ---
High rates of bioerosion are bad for a reef and low rates are good.

--- SENTENCE 896 ---
The low abundance of bioeroders on Tutuila reefs is a good sign.

--- SENTENCE 897 ---
# Resilience

Observers of coral reefs often report the reefs they first observe and record as though that is the way they have always been.

--- SENTENCE 898 ---
Those who have observed reefs for many years often come to realize that a reef description is a snapshot of a reef going through continual dynamic change.

--- SENTENCE 899 ---
The pioneering studies of J. Connell on the Great Barrier Reef showed just how dynamic coral communities can be, with coral continually increasing or decreasing in abundance and size (Connell et al. 1997).

--- SENTENCE 900 ---
The picture emerged of reefs being subject to relatively brief natural disturbances such as hurricanes and crown-of-thorns starfish outbreaks, and then spending most of their time recovering from these disturbance events.

--- SENTENCE 901 ---
So instead of a static reef, it is a dynamic reef repeatedly cycling between disturbance and recovery.

--- SENTENCE 902 ---
Surveys of natural reefs will reveal great variation, not only from spatial differences, but from temporal differences, with different reefs at different points in the disturbance/recovery cycle.

--- SENTENCE 903 ---
Further, disturbances of moderate intensity and frequency may actually increase diversity, by reducing the dominance of some species and opening spaces where new recruits of subordinate species can recruit.

--- SENTENCE 904 ---
This is called the ―intermediate disturbance hypothesis‖ (Connell, 1978; Grigg, 1983; Connell, 1997).

--- SENTENCE 905 ---
A contrasting view comes from paleontology, where in both the Caribbean and Pacific, fossil reefs show coral assemblages at one location can be very stable over hundreds of thousands of years, with smaller differences than spatially between neighboring locations (Pandolfi and Jackson, 1997; Pandolfi et al. 1999).

--- SENTENCE 906 ---
One possible resolution between these two apparently contradictory views is that over relatively short time periods of a few decades, reefs show strong temporal changes in the abundance of corals, but over longer geological time spans of thousands of years, the species composition of coral communities is remarkably stable.

--- SENTENCE 907 ---
In recent years, the many impacts of humans on reefs and the looming threat of climate change has lead to an emphasis on ―resilience‖ in coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 908 ---
―Resistance‖ is defined as the ability of a reef to not be damaged by events, and ―resilience‖ is defined as the ability of the reef to recover from damage.

--- SENTENCE 909 ---
Resilience is thought to be a critical factor for the future survival of reefs when they are assaulted by an increasing barrage of mass coral bleaching events.

--- SENTENCE 910 ---
Healthy reefs are thought to be resilient, and unhealthy reefs not resilient.

--- SENTENCE 911 ---
The concept of resilience can be illustrated by the author's observation of a reef in the Philippines in the Mabini district of southern Luzon.

--- SENTENCE 912 ---
When first observed, the reef to the right of the Dive 7000 resort consisted of nothing but dead pulverized rubble.

--- SENTENCE 913 ---
A hurricane a month earlier had rolled rocks back and forth, crushing all the coral.

--- SENTENCE 914 ---
Eleven years later the author returned and was unable to find the damaged area, until he realized that the area of near 100% beautiful live coral cover of diverse species in front of him was the same area.

--- SENTENCE 915 ---
In contrast, a reef in southwestern Madagascar which had most corals killed in a mass coral bleaching event in 2000 was mostly covered with algae and showed no sign of recovery several years later.

--- SENTENCE 916 ---
The Philippine reef was resilient; the Madagascar reef was not resilient.

--- SENTENCE 917 ---
A study of the recovery of different reefs on the Great Barrier Reef from crown-of-thorns starfish outbreaks that eat most of the live coral tissues, found that the rate of recovery has been slowing, indicating a gradual loss of resilience and thus of reef health, probably due to human impacts (Seymour and Bradbury, 1999).

--- SENTENCE 918 ---
The reefs of American Samoa have been subjected to a whole series of disturbances such as the crown-of-thorns outbreak that ate most of the coral in 1978, a series of hurricanes, three mass coral bleaching events, and a recent tsunami.

--- SENTENCE 919 ---
These events differed in intensity, with the crown-of-thorns outbreak being by far the most severe single event.

--- SENTENCE 920 ---
Today, reefs around Tutuila vary greatly in coral cover, ranging from reefs that have little coral and are almost completely covered with coralline algae or turf, to reefs that have good coral cover of 40% or better; reefs average about 30% coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 921 ---
To reach an average of 30% coral cover, corals had to recover significantly from the crown-of-thorns outbreak, since 90% or more of all corals were eaten then.

--- SENTENCE 922 ---
Thus, it is sometimes said that the reefs of American Samoa are resilient.

--- SENTENCE 923 ---
The fact that different reefs around Tutuila currently have such different levels of coral cover could imply that different reefs around the island have very different levels of resilience.

--- SENTENCE 924 ---
Or it could be that reefs with low coral cover currently had low levels before crown-of-thorns, we don't know.

--- SENTENCE 925 ---
That itself might be taken as an indication that those reefs were not healthy before the outbreak.

--- SENTENCE 926 ---
But in general, the recovery to 30% coral cover supports the view that reefs here are at least moderately resilient.

--- SENTENCE 927 ---
Corals on the reef flats have not had time to recover, since in recent years tides have been low enough to kill exposed corals almost every year.

--- SENTENCE 928 ---
To summarize the indices of reef health, such as live coral cover, trends in coral cover, live coral index, amount of crustose calcareous algae, all macroalgae, and brown macroalgae, the ratio of ―good‖ cover (coral plus crustose calcareous algae) compared to ―bad‖ cover (turf plus macroalgae), strong recruitment of table corals, good water clarity, low abundances of filter feeders and bioeroders, and recovery from a series of major disturbances, most indices are consistent with the view that that the reefs of American Samoa are relatively healthy, though the live coral cover may not be as high as it has been at some times in the past and water clarity is not as good on the slopes as the outer banks.

--- SENTENCE 929 ---
Caribbean coral reefs currently have about 8% live coral cover, huge amounts of brown marcoalgae and turf.

--- SENTENCE 930 ---
Coral disease has killed over 90% of the two most dominant coral species in the Caribbean, staghorn (Acropora cervicornis) and elkhorn (Acropora palmata).

--- SENTENCE 931 ---
People in the Caribbean and Florida would die for reefs as healthy as ours.

--- SENTENCE 932 ---
This summary of reef health as being relatively good around Tutuila applies only to outer reef slopes (and banks), not to the harbor or some other bays, which will be considered below.

--- SENTENCE 933 ---
# Shelf and Banks

The shelf area (Figure 1) is very large, and ranges from about 20-30 m deep at the bottom of the reef slope (even shallower in some bays on the north side of the island) to as much as 100 m deep.

--- SENTENCE 934 ---
This shelf has been known since at least the 1920's (Chamberlin, 1921; Davis, 1921), and is the basis for the Daly theory of glacial sea level control of the growth of coral reefs (Daly, 1924).

--- SENTENCE 935 ---
At the base of the reef slope it is either rubble or sand with little or no coral.

--- SENTENCE 936 ---
Much of the shelf appears on backscatter from multibeam sonar to be soft bottom (Brainard et al. 2008; Fenner et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 937 ---
The author observed from a submersible a sand bottom on the shelf seaward from the base of Taema Banks at about 50 m depth to the dropoff at 100 m depth.

--- SENTENCE 938 ---
About half of that sandy bottom had many patches of Halimeda on it.

--- SENTENCE 939 ---
Multibeam sonar of the shelf by CRED (Coral Reef Ecosystem Division of NOAA) has revealed a variety of banks and patch reefs on the shelf (Brainard et al. 2008;

--- SENTENCE 940 ---
Fenner et al. 2008) as shown diagramatically in Figure 1.

--- SENTENCE 941 ---
Camera sled studies by CRED have revealed a variety of hard and soft corals on hard bottom areas in deep water, on the sides and bases of banks and patch reefs on the shelf.

--- SENTENCE 942 ---
Quantitative measures of hard coral cover in that study have found that mean hard coral cover decreases from 30 m deep down to 100 m deep (Bare et al. in preparation).

--- SENTENCE 943 ---
In shallower water, the tops and sides of banks on the shelf can in places have high coralline algae cover and low coral cover (as on the

top of part of Taema Bank) or medium coral cover and lower coralline algae cover, or high rubble cover.

--- SENTENCE 944 ---
A transect run near the green navigation buoy at the west end of Taema Bank found 25% coral cover and 60% crustose calcareous algae cover.

--- SENTENCE 945 ---
The coral community was dominated by table corals there, and they are common though not necessarily dominant elsewhere on the banks at less than 30 m depth (Fenner, 2008b).

--- SENTENCE 946 ---
At about 70-90 m depth on the shelf south of Taema Banks there are beds of giant foraminifera discs up to about 5 cm diameter.

--- SENTENCE 947 ---
Each disc is constructed by a single animal cell that lives within pores in the disc and hosts algal symbionts (Song et al. 1994).

--- SENTENCE 948 ---
Seaward of Taema Banks, south of Pago Pago harbor, the shelf ends at 100 m depth and rolls off quickly into a very near vertical escarpment.

--- SENTENCE 949 ---
The escarpment is light colored and has layers of solid material about 5-10 cm thick.

--- SENTENCE 950 ---
This is probably carbonate from fossil reefs.

--- SENTENCE 951 ---
Biota on the escarpment is sparse.

--- SENTENCE 952 ---
At about 350 m depth the escarpment is covered with a talus slope at about a 45 degree angle, with blocks of the solid carbonate from the escarpment resting on finer debris and sand.

--- SENTENCE 953 ---
Deeper on the slope there are some projections of dark rock which are likely to be volcanic basalt.

--- SENTENCE 954 ---
# Bays: Pago Pago Harbor, Vatia Bay, and Fagasa Bay

Discussion so far has concentrated on reefs outside of bays.

--- SENTENCE 955 ---
Reefs and conditions within bays are quite different in some instances than the reefs outside bays, and so will be considered separately.

--- SENTENCE 956 ---
The most impacted marine area in American Samoa is Pago Pago Harbor.

--- SENTENCE 957 ---
The harbor once contained reefs with high coral abundance, clear water, and abundant fish.

--- SENTENCE 958 ---
It is said that there were many fishing canoes inside the harbor, and fishermen did not have to go outside the harbor to catch abundant fish.

--- SENTENCE 959 ---
Those days are long gone.

--- SENTENCE 960 ---
The harbor is a good example of how to destroy coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 961 ---
First, some reefs were dredged to obtain material to add to village land, such as at Gataivai and Aua.

--- SENTENCE 962 ---
Second, roads, canneries, fuel tank farms and other structures were built on fill placed over reefs, as at Gataivai and the tuna canneries.

--- SENTENCE 963 ---
Third, the world's largest and third largest tuna canneries were constructed and their effluent released directly into the harbor.

--- SENTENCE 964 ---
In addition, a shipyard was constructed near the cannery, where paints and antifouling agents were removed from ship hulls and washed into the water, along with other chemical pollutants.

--- SENTENCE 965 ---
Rapid population growth lead to increases in nutrient runoff from piggeries and other sources, and the introduction and sale of high-phosphate detergent in the first decade of the 21st Century, added to the nutrient burden.

--- SENTENCE 966 ---
A million-dollar soccer field was installed in 2007, with the sod placed on beach sand removed from local beaches (forbidden by a Parks and Recreation Dept. ordinance) to improve drainage.

--- SENTENCE 967 ---
The beach sand absorbed nutrients, requiring fertilizer application, and subsequent rain may have washed fertilizer into the harbor.

--- SENTENCE 968 ---
In 1988, the canneries were legally required to carry high nutrient wastes five miles offshore (which is beyond the edge of the shelf) for disposal and in 1991 to divert liquid wastes to near the mouth of the harbor by pipe.

--- SENTENCE 969 ---
The result was a rapid drop in nutrient levels in the harbor (Craig et al. 2005).

--- SENTENCE 970 ---
The net effect of the many insults to the water and reefs of Pago Pago Harbor was that much of the transect lengths established on coral reefs in 1916 no longer exist, the high coral abundance recorded in Aua in 1916 decreased until most of the transect is now dead rubble (Craig et al. 2005), and soft corals on transects went from abundant to zero (Cornish and DiDonato, 2004;

Craig et al. 2005).

--- SENTENCE 971 ---
An early drilling of one reef in the harbor revealed it was primarily constructed of soft coral spicules (C. Birkeland, personal comm.), yet soft corals are rare on that reef today.

--- SENTENCE 972 ---
The remaining hard coral on the outer edges of reef flats in the harbor decreases from high coral cover near the mouth of the harbor to low coral cover and then zero in the inner harbor (Figure 14).

--- SENTENCE 973 ---
Although nutrient levels in the harbor decreased when cannery effluent was diverted outside the harbor, today the harbor water is turbid and green, with low transparency at the head of the harbor and high transparency at the mouth (Figure 16).

--- SENTENCE 974 ---
Further, a small oyster, Saccostrea cucculata, is abundant near the water line near the head of the harbor, and decreases to low levels near the mouth of the harbor (Figure 15).

--- SENTENCE 975 ---
This species, like other oysters, is a filter feeder which consumes plankton, and thus is a bioindicator of plankton levels, which are in turn a bioindicator of nutrient levels.

--- SENTENCE 976 ---
The harbor is long and narrow, and undoubtedly has very little flushing, and the nutrients that wash into the harbor accumulate there, particularly at the head of the harbor where the flushing is least.

--- SENTENCE 977 ---
In recent years, there have been repeated red tide blooms, consisting of the single celled dinoflagellate alga, Ceratium fuscus.

--- SENTENCE 978 ---
The blooms are so intense that they have turned the entire harbor rust red.

--- SENTENCE 979 ---
Blooms have not been reported outside the harbor.

--- SENTENCE 980 ---
High levels of nutrients are implicated in these blooms, which are thankfully non-toxic.

--- SENTENCE 981 ---
These blooms are symptomatic of high nutrients and a highly degraded ecosystem in the harbor.

--- SENTENCE 982 ---
Paul Brown has discovered sponge beds deep in the harbor (Fenner et al. 2008) which undoubtedly feed on the abundant plankton.

!

--- SENTENCE 983 ---
Coral cover as a function of location in Pago Pago Harbor.

!

--- SENTENCE 984 ---
Oyster density as a function of distance from harbor

!

--- SENTENCE 985 ---
Harbor water clarity

Amazingly, some hard coral does remain in the harbor.

--- SENTENCE 986 ---
According to the map made in 1916 of the harbor (Mayor, 1924), coral reefs were not present at the head of the harbor.

--- SENTENCE 987 ---
Corals currently do not live in the inner harbor, though a few live on the subtidal rocks along the shore in Fagatogo.

--- SENTENCE 988 ---
Corals live on the outer edge of the reef flat from near the cannery out to the mouth of the harbor (including at Aua).

--- SENTENCE 989 ---
At Onososopo, corals are continuous from the outer edge of the reef flat to the shore, with 45% coral cover recorded on the outer reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 990 ---
At Gataivai, just inside the harbor from the sewage pipeline, a relatively small patch of reef flat appears to have an undisturbed coral community with about 65% coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 991 ---
Thus, near the harbor mouth corals do well in spots on the reef flat, whereas farther inside only small amounts of corals persist (Figure 12).

--- SENTENCE 992 ---
Vatia Bay on the north shore of Tutuila is shaped like a smaller version of Pago Pago Harbor, being long and narrow.

--- SENTENCE 993 ---
People live around Vatia Bay, but their numbers are much smaller than around Pago Pago Harbor, and most of the activities that have damaged the harbor have not happened in Vatia.

--- SENTENCE 994 ---
However, villages on the north side of the island are isolated, and not connected to the main sewage system that utilizes sewage treatment plants.

--- SENTENCE 995 ---
Septic tank systems are used in the villages on the north side of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 996 ---
Nutrients may escape from these systems into Vatia Bay, in addition to wastes from piggeries and from high-phosphate detergents.

--- SENTENCE 997 ---
Water at the head of Vatia Bay is turbid and light green, while it is clear near the mouth of the bay.

--- SENTENCE 998 ---
Corals grow in abundance in the bay, but one of the dominant species, finger coral (P. cylindrica), is otherwise only found in abundance in back reef pools where they are protected from waves.

--- SENTENCE 999 ---
Corals from the middle of the slope on down are separated by sediment, which is a mixture of white carbonate sand and grey silt which appears to have washed off the land.

--- SENTENCE 1000 ---
Thus, the corals in Vatia Bay appear to be protected from waves and stressed by sediment and nutrient runoff.

--- SENTENCE 1001 ---
Like in Pago Pago Harbor, there is a gradient of impacts from high at the head of the bay to low at the mouth of the bay.

--- SENTENCE 1002 ---
In 2007, there was a sudden appearance of the brown alga Dictyota on the slopes of Vatia Bay.

--- SENTENCE 1003 ---
There were considerable amounts, which grew over sand, other algae (Halimeda) and corals.

--- SENTENCE 1004 ---
Dictyota is chemically defended, and fish do not like to eat it.

--- SENTENCE 1005 ---
Dictyota is one of the algae that have participated in ―phase shifts‖ where a coral reef shifts from being coral-dominated to being dominated by macroalgae.

--- SENTENCE 1006 ---
It appears likely that Vatia Bay does not receive more impacts than many other places around Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 1007 ---
Rather, the very limited flushing in the bay allows nutrients and sediment to build up to higher levels than on the much better-flushed outer reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 1008 ---
Fagasa is another bay on the north shore, not quite as long as Vatia.

--- SENTENCE 1009 ---
Areas of the reef slope in Fagasa have very low coral cover, no sign of recently dead coral, and reef rock surfaces covered with brown sediment caught in turf algae.

--- SENTENCE 1010 ---
Below the main reef is a gently sloping sandy slope with reef outcrops.

--- SENTENCE 1011 ---
Sand on this slope has a component of silt.

--- SENTENCE 1012 ---
The reef in Fagasa Bay appears to be under significant sediment stress.

--- SENTENCE 1013 ---
Masafau Bay is another large bay on the north.

--- SENTENCE 1014 ---
There is little coral in the inner bay, though there are exposed beds of staghorn rubble that show that corals once grew there abundantly, and probably not too many decades ago.

--- SENTENCE 1015 ---
Not all bays on Tutuila show signs of poor water quality or sedimentation.

--- SENTENCE 1016 ---
For instance, Tafeu Bay on the north coast has clear water and high coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 1017 ---
Tafeu Bay is, however, in a watershed with no humans.

--- SENTENCE 1018 ---
Likewise, Fagatele Bay and Larson's Bay on the south coast both have clear water and healthy coral, but few if any people in their watersheds.

--- SENTENCE 1019 ---
# Invertebrates other than corals

Coral reefs are habitats for other invertebrates in addition to corals, as well as for fish.

--- SENTENCE 1020 ---
Algae on the reefs provide food for the many herbivorous fish, corals provide food for the few corallivorous fish, and other invertebrates provide food for the many fish that eat invertebrates.

--- SENTENCE 1021 ---
Many wrasse species, for instance, eat invertebrates, and the wrasse family is large.

--- SENTENCE 1022 ---
Coral reefs have high diversities of invertebrates, so many that the totals can only be estimated, and estimates are very high indeed.

--- SENTENCE 1023 ---
About 2700 coral reef plant and animal species are known from American Samoa presently (Fenner et al. 2008), of which about 1500 are invertebrates, including coral, about 1230 excluding coral.

--- SENTENCE 1024 ---
But we know this is a tiny fraction of the total.

--- SENTENCE 1025 ---
For fish that eat invertebrates, the abundance of invertebrates may be at least as important as the number of species or more so.

--- SENTENCE 1026 ---
Quantitative counts are almost always restricted to invertebrates that can be seen by divers during the day, so they have to be large enough to be seen, exposed (not hidden or cryptic), and they have to be sessile (immobile) or diurnal (not nocturnal).

--- SENTENCE 1027 ---
Probably most invertebrate species are cryptic, nocturnal, and/or too small for divers to see.

--- SENTENCE 1028 ---
However, the larger, less cryptic species may be an important food source.

--- SENTENCE 1029 ---
The abundance of non-cryptic diurnal macroinvertebrates is presented in Figure 17, with similar data reported by Brainard et al. (2008).

--- SENTENCE 1030 ---
The abundances of invertebrates on the reefs of American Samoa are low compared to some areas, such as the Philippines and Indonesia, or the Caribbean.

--- SENTENCE 1031 ---
However, at least some other areas of the Pacific also have relatively low abundances of invertebrates, such as Hawaii, Fiji (personal observations), and the Marianas (Starmer et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 1032 ---
The cause of the low abundances of invertebrates is not known.

--- SENTENCE 1033 ---
The low abundance of invertebrates recorded could be because of a large number and variety of fish that eat invertebrates, which keep the populations low (Wulff, 1997).

--- SENTENCE 1034 ---
Or the low abundance of invertebrates could provide little food for fish, so that the abundance of fish that eat invertebrates is low.

--- SENTENCE 1035 ---
Information on the abundance of invertebrate-eating fish in Tutuila are not currently available.

--- SENTENCE 1036 ---
Though the data is present in various data sets, division by trophic group has not yet been carried out to this level of detail.

--- SENTENCE 1037 ---
Only one of the largest reef fish species, which are all uncommon to rare, is an invertebrate eater, the humphead wrasse (Chelinus undulatus), and it is one of the more abundant of the large fish.

--- SENTENCE 1038 ---
Lack of invertebrates cannot be why the others are uncommon to rare.

--- SENTENCE 1039 ---
The abundance of invertebrate eaters should depend on the productivity of the invertebrate community, that is, how many are produced and eaten, not the standing stock.

--- SENTENCE 1040 ---
Standing stocks of visible invertebrates are easy to measure, but productivity would require much more effort and sophistication to measure.

--- SENTENCE 1041 ---
The fact that some other Pacific reefs also have visibly low invertebrate populations suggests that the causes may be natural, but until the causes are known, this is only speculative.

.

!

--- SENTENCE 1042 ---
Invertebrate abundances on reef slopes, from belt transects

#### Impacts and threats

Impacts are defined here as negative effects on the reef that have already happened.

--- SENTENCE 1043 ---
Threats are defined as things that may cause impacts in the future, or not.

--- SENTENCE 1044 ---
Many factors have both caused impacts and are future threats, but vary in the magnitude of the past impacts compared to the future threats.

--- SENTENCE 1045 ---
For threats, the probability of a future impact and the magnitude of that possible impact are independent factors-

--- SENTENCE 1046 ---
a threat may be highly likely yet have a small impact, or may be improbable yet have a massive impact should it occur.

--- SENTENCE 1047 ---
Most natural impacts have been happening for the entire life of the island (1.5 million years) and are highly likely to continue.

--- SENTENCE 1048 ---
Human produced impacts were not present before 3000 years ago, many have increased in recent years, and they are often major threats for the future.

--- SENTENCE 1049 ---
Many natural impacts are brief, recurrent disturbances, which allow reef recovery between events, while human impacts are often chronic and may produce continuous degradation of the reef.

--- SENTENCE 1050 ---
Nevertheless, natural impacts as well as human impacts are important in shaping reef communities.

#### **Crown-of-thorns starfish**

Crown-of-thorns starfish (Acanthaster planci) are normally relatively rare on coral reefs, but on occasion they can have population explosions in what are called "outbreaks."

--- SENTENCE 1051 ---
Outbreaks in American Samoa occurred in 1938 and 1978 (Flanigan and Lamberts, 1981).

--- SENTENCE 1052 ---
Like most starfish this species is predatory and feeds by everting its stomach out its mouth.

--- SENTENCE 1053 ---
Like the cushion star (Culcita novaeguineae) and a few other starfish, it eats coral tissue.

--- SENTENCE 1054 ---
Unlike the cushion star, crown-of-thorns is soft and flexible and able to wrap around coral branches to eat them, making most corals available for consumption, while the cushion star cannot eat branching coral well.

--- SENTENCE 1055 ---
Crown-of-thorns (COTS) are very well defended, with their back covered with sharp spines that have a painful toxin.

--- SENTENCE 1056 ---
Few organisms eat it (Birkeland, 1989).

--- SENTENCE 1057 ---
Outbreaks were first noticed in southern Japan in the 1940's and 50's, then on the Great Barrier Reef in the 1960's.

--- SENTENCE 1058 ---
Initial

hypotheses about the cause pointed to human removal of natural predators, most prominently the triton shell (Charonia tritonis).

--- SENTENCE 1059 ---
But some outbreaks occurred where triton shells had not been taken.

--- SENTENCE 1060 ---
Other predators were discovered: small shrimp called Harlequin Shrimp (Hymenocera picta) and polychate worms.

--- SENTENCE 1061 ---
A few fish are capable of eating it, but none have been found to commonly eat it.

--- SENTENCE 1062 ---
Most of these predators will eat a variety of starfish and other echinoderms.

--- SENTENCE 1063 ---
Also, most take some time to eat a single crown-of-thorns, and the triton shells in particular are fairly rare even where they have not been collected.

--- SENTENCE 1064 ---
They could be a significant predator on crown-of-thorns only when the starfish is rare, since once an outbreak begins the predators are swamped with prey and can have no effect.

--- SENTENCE 1065 ---
A second hypothesis was that outbreaks are caused by increased survival of their larvae.

--- SENTENCE 1066 ---
Adults produce large numbers of tiny eggs, like many invertebrates and fish.

--- SENTENCE 1067 ---
If spawning occurs when there is little plankton for the larvae to eat, most will starve to death.

--- SENTENCE 1068 ---
If spawning happens to occur when plankton is abundant, a much higher fraction of larvae may survive.

--- SENTENCE 1069 ---
Research found that when the larvae settle, they metamorphose into tiny sea stars which hide in holes in the reef and eat coralline algae.

--- SENTENCE 1070 ---
As they grow, eventually they start coming out at night to eat coral, but returning to holes during the day.

--- SENTENCE 1071 ---
When they are fairly large they start to spend more time out in the day, but only during an outbreak are most individuals out during the day.

--- SENTENCE 1072 ---
It takes about three years from spawning to a size at which they come out in the day and are seen by divers.

--- SENTENCE 1073 ---
A common report in outbreaks is that the adult starfish seem to suddenly appear, millions of them, out of nowhere.

--- SENTENCE 1074 ---
At one point they were thought to march in mass from one reef to another, which would explain why they suddenly appear as adults, but there is no evidence of them walking from one reef to another en mass.

--- SENTENCE 1075 ---
Plankton increase when nutrients fuel the growth of phytoplankton.

--- SENTENCE 1076 ---
If a large rainstorm follows a dry period, there will be a pulse of accumulated nutrients washed off the land, which could fertilize the bloom of phytoplankton, the increased survival of larvae, and a sudden outbreak of adult crown-of-thorns about 3 years later.

--- SENTENCE 1077 ---
Just such a correlation has been found, and it only occurs around high islands which can have nutrient runoff, not around atolls (Birkeland, 1982).

--- SENTENCE 1078 ---
Outbreaks continue to happen periodically on the Great Barrier Reef, and a recent examination of this hypothesis for their cause was able to find evidence supporting each link in the logical chain except one, for which there was no evidence either way (Brodie et al. 2005).

--- SENTENCE 1079 ---
Thus, at this point this is the best supported hypothesis for the origin of outbreaks.

--- SENTENCE 1080 ---
Although the hypothesis posits the cause in natural nutrient runoff, it appears possible that human-produced nutrient runoff might increase the chance of having plankton populations that could feed COTS larvae and cause an outbreak.

--- SENTENCE 1081 ---
Thus, although most outbreaks are likely natural, it is possible that humans may play a role in some locations.

--- SENTENCE 1082 ---
All this is a prelude to the outbreak in 1978 on Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 1083 ---
There were millions of COTS, and after 486,933 were removed there were still uncountable aggregations of thousands (C. Birkeland, personal comm.).

--- SENTENCE 1084 ---
They ate about 90% or more of all the coral.

--- SENTENCE 1085 ---
It was the most massive disturbance of American Samoan reefs on record.

--- SENTENCE 1086 ---
It was the impetus for the beginning of the Birkeland and Green long-term monitoring program (e.g., Birkeland et al. 1987; Green et al. 1999; Fenner et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 1087 ---
COTS has a very specific effect on coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1088 ---
It eats the tissue off the outsides of corals, killing them, but does not harm or alter the skeleton at all.

--- SENTENCE 1089 ---
As a result, the coral skeletons stand in exactly the shape they were before the starfish ate the tissue.

--- SENTENCE 1090 ---
On some corals uneaten patches

may survive, but often they are small parts of the coral.

--- SENTENCE 1091 ---
COTS prefer to eat some corals more than others, and Acropora is at the top of the menu.

--- SENTENCE 1092 ---
According to reports and photographs taken at the time, Acropora table corals and staghorns were among the most abundant corals, if not dominant (C. Birkeland, personal comm.).

--- SENTENCE 1093 ---
No coral surveys were carried out before the outbreak, so quantitative information on coral communities is not available.

--- SENTENCE 1094 ---
Still, COTS prefer Acropora, and it was abundant, and so it was eaten.

--- SENTENCE 1095 ---
The result was dead coral skeletons in the same shape as before.

--- SENTENCE 1096 ---
Fish species that specialize in eating coral, such as several butterflyfish and a couple of filefish, may well have decreased to very low levels feeding on the remaining live coral.

--- SENTENCE 1097 ---
There are very few such species.

--- SENTENCE 1098 ---
Fish that specialize in living among the branches of corals such as small gobies (Gobiodon spp.) and coral crouchers (Caracantus spp.), would have decreased only if they depended on the coral for food as well as shelter.

--- SENTENCE 1099 ---
Again, there are only a few species in this category.

--- SENTENCE 1100 ---
Other fish species that depend on the shapes for hiding may not have decreased at all.

--- SENTENCE 1101 ---
This is likely to be a large category of species.

--- SENTENCE 1102 ---
Initially, the dead coral skeletons would have rapidly grown filamentous or turf algae, turning a light yellow, then vivid green, then black.

--- SENTENCE 1103 ---
Within about six months or so, patches of coralline algae would begin to grow and cover more and more of the skeletons.

--- SENTENCE 1104 ---
The skeletons would also erode over the years, being bored by sponges that weaken the skeleton (Figure 18), and other organisms.

--- SENTENCE 1105 ---
Table corals tend to loose more and more of their edge until only a stem and center of the table remain.

--- SENTENCE 1106 ---
Hurricanes tend to rip up weakened skeletons.

--- SENTENCE 1107 ---
Staghorns and other branching species eventually become weakened to the place they collapse and end up as rubble beds.

--- SENTENCE 1108 ---
The coralline algae that encrust these skeletons then tend to bind the rubble together to some degree, though storms and tsunamis may still be able to mobilize rubble beds.

--- SENTENCE 1109 ---
The initial turf algae may provide food for herbivorous fish, but that phase likely lasts too short to affect populations which must reproduce to change in number.

--- SENTENCE 1110 ---
Coralline algae provides little food for fish, but erect skeletons still provide the hiding places that the live corals provided.

--- SENTENCE 1111 ---
When skeletons collapse, they suddenly provide many fewer hiding places.

--- SENTENCE 1112 ---
Dead coral tables covered with coralline algae often provide excellent habitat for coral recruitment and juvenile corals, though if the table disintegrates many juvenile corals may not survive.

--- SENTENCE 1113 ---
If the small patches of coral that survived grow and reproduce, and juvenile corals begin to repopulate the reef, the reef may recover live coral over time.

--- SENTENCE 1114 ---
Coral cover data from the long-term monitoring program of Birkeland and Green shows an initial increase in coral cover, followed by a decrease probably produced by other disturbances, and more recent increases (Figure 19).

!

--- SENTENCE 1115 ---
Orange and yellow sponges inside of an old dead table coral covered with encrusting algae.

!

--- SENTENCE 1116 ---
Coral cover changes since the COTS outbreak of 1978, from Craig et al. (2005).

--- SENTENCE 1117 ---
In summary, the COTS outbreak of 1978 had the greatest impact of any single event or process on the reefs of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1118 ---
They decimated the live corals, changing the habitat dramatically.

--- SENTENCE 1119 ---
The effects on fish populations may depend on the sequence of events, whether skeleton collapse occurred before significant new coral growth appeared.

--- SENTENCE 1120 ---
This assumes that the loss of hiding places following skeleton collapse would affect fish populations, a premise that will be examined in the section on coral as habitat.

#### **Hurricanes**

Hurricanes struck American Samoa in recent years in 1981, 1987, 1990, 1991, 2004 and 2005.

--- SENTENCE 1121 ---
The hurricanes in 1990 and 1991 were particularly destructive.

--- SENTENCE 1122 ---
Unfortunately, the damage to reefs was not documented for any of these hurricanes.

--- SENTENCE 1123 ---
They all occurred before monitoring programs were begun.

--- SENTENCE 1124 ---
When the Territorial Monitoring Program was begun at DMWR in 2005, there were no obvious signs of hurricane damage at any of the 11 sites monitored, though hurricane damage becomes less and less obvious over the years.

--- SENTENCE 1125 ---
Hurricanes damage coral reefs primarily through the mechanical breakage of coral and even of the rock composing the reef.

--- SENTENCE 1126 ---
The concussive pressure wave from a crashing wave is the most powerful force for breaking coral.

--- SENTENCE 1127 ---
Waves in a powerful hurricane can reach as much as 20 m high and are very destructive.

--- SENTENCE 1128 ---
The amount of destruction depends on the power of the hurricane, the distance to the eye of the hurricane, whether the wind direction is driving waves onshore, and whether there are any obstacles such as other land masses that shelter the reef from the waves.

--- SENTENCE 1129 ---
So if a powerful hurricane has winds blowing from the north, waves are likely to be damaging the reef on the north side, but the land mass of Tutuila protects the coral on the south side.

--- SENTENCE 1130 ---
There are no other land masses around Tutuila large enough to have any ability to shelter the reefs from waves, but the shelf and the bank reefs on the shelf do reduce wave action on the coast nearest them.

--- SENTENCE 1131 ---
Hurricanes have a significant ability to cause rapid erosion of the shoreline.

--- SENTENCE 1132 ---
For instance, on the southeast coast of Tutuila, there are many black blocks of basalt rock sitting on the reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 1133 ---
They got there by giant waves ripping them out of the shoreline and scattering them on the reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 1134 ---
On the north shore, much of the shoreline is sheer cliffs down to the water line, which is why there is no road along the coast there like on the south shore.

--- SENTENCE 1135 ---
This is due to wave erosion at the waterline.

--- SENTENCE 1136 ---
A smaller effect can be seen on the south shore, where from a distance the ridges can be seen to slope down toward the sea, and then end in a near-vertical drop down to the road and the shoreline.

--- SENTENCE 1137 ---
From a distance this can be also be seen clearly in the outline of Ta'u.

--- SENTENCE 1138 ---
This shape occurs because erosion is more rapid at the waterline due to waves than higher up on the hills due to rain.

--- SENTENCE 1139 ---
On the north shore, reefs are restricted to bays, which also indicates that maximum wave power in the north is greater than in the south.

--- SENTENCE 1140 ---
Hurricanes in this area of the South Pacific commonly form northwest of Tutuila, and then track east, at some point starting to track southeast.

--- SENTENCE 1141 ---
If hurricanes pass to the north of Tutuila more often than to the south, then maximum wave power would strike the north coast more often than the south.

--- SENTENCE 1142 ---
One result is that reefs are restricted to bays, and corals outside of bays tend to be small.

--- SENTENCE 1143 ---
If corals are good habitat for fish, particularly if they are large enough to provide hiding places, then the smooth basalt with small corals outside bays on the north may be poorer habitat than reefs inside bays.

--- SENTENCE 1144 ---
Sabater and Tafaeono (2007) actually found more fish outside bays on the north than inside bays.

--- SENTENCE 1145 ---
This will be discussed further in the section on coral as habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1146 ---
Because hurricanes cause mechanical damage to corals, they break the corals.

--- SENTENCE 1147 ---
If damage is moderate, coral fragments may survive, attach, and regrow.

--- SENTENCE 1148 ---
In the process of breaking corals, a hurricane will reduce rugosity and thus hiding places for fish.

--- SENTENCE 1149 ---
This can happen even if many coral fragments survive as a flat rubble bed provides few hiding places for fish.

--- SENTENCE 1150 ---
If the rubble is big pieces with spaces between them, there may still be many hiding places.

--- SENTENCE 1151 ---
If damage is severe, most of the coral fragments may be abraded or buried and not survive.

--- SENTENCE 1152 ---
Thus, hurricanes can greatly degrade coral reef quality as habitat, but only if damage is severe.

--- SENTENCE 1153 ---
Hurricanes are natural, and humans cannot stop them.

--- SENTENCE 1154 ---
They appear to strike American Samoa an average of about once in 5 years or so.

--- SENTENCE 1155 ---
The timing of their strikes is of course largely random and irregular.

--- SENTENCE 1156 ---
It is likely that low-impact hurricane events, due to weak hurricanes and/or greater distances from the eye, are more common events that the more powerful strikes, which require the eye of a powerful hurricane to pass close to the island (the most powerful winds are in the eye wall).

--- SENTENCE 1157 ---
Thus, high-impact hurricane damage on reefs is likely to be much less frequent than once in 5 years, it may be several decades between such events on the average.

--- SENTENCE 1158 ---
This is usually enough time for recovery.

--- SENTENCE 1159 ---
Because hurricanes are natural, reefs have evolved to cope with them.

--- SENTENCE 1160 ---
If a hurricane hits on the average once in 5 years, in the 1.5 million years of the island about 300,000 hurricanes have hit, and yet the reefs have survived.

--- SENTENCE 1161 ---
Even in the 3000 years of human habitation, there would have been about 600 hurricanes.

--- SENTENCE 1162 ---
That is a lot of hurricanes.

--- SENTENCE 1163 ---
Climate change is predicted to increase the intensity of hurricanes, but probably not their frequency.

--- SENTENCE 1164 ---
However, that means that category 5 hurricanes will be more frequent, and do more damage.

--- SENTENCE 1165 ---
The energy source for hurricanes is the warm surface waters, so it makes sense that warmer surface waters should provide more energy for hurricanes.

--- SENTENCE 1166 ---
More frequent powerful hurricanes means more reef damage.

--- SENTENCE 1167 ---
Thus, not only have hurricanes had impacts, they are threats for the future as well, though it appears likely to this author that the increase in hurricane strength will not by itself cause significant degradation of coral reefs.

#### **Tsunamis**

On Sept 29, 2009, at 6:48 am, a large tsunami struck American Samoa and Upolu in (independent) Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1168 ---
This tsunami was generated by a magnitude 8.0-8.3 earthquake just east of the Tongan Trench and just south of where the Tongan Trench curves west.

--- SENTENCE 1169 ---
Wave runup on land was as much as 12.4 m. Several villages were severely damaged, such as Pago Pago, Leone, Asili, Amanave, Paloa, Fagasa, and Tula.

--- SENTENCE 1170 ---
At the time of writing, damage to the reefs is being assessed, with about 38 sites assessed so far.

--- SENTENCE 1171 ---
Mechanical damage to reefs from powerful water movements varied from negligible to severe.

--- SENTENCE 1172 ---
More reefs had negligible damage than had severe damage, much like on land where only a small proportion of the land was badly damaged.

--- SENTENCE 1173 ---
So while damage at a few small spots was severe, overall damage to the reefs was minor.

--- SENTENCE 1174 ---
There may have been a tsunami here in 1917 as well, when another large earthquake struck in the Tongan Trench.

--- SENTENCE 1175 ---
Earthquakes less than about 7.5 generally do not produce tsunamis.

--- SENTENCE 1176 ---
It should be born in mind that tsunamis are totally natural and unavoidable.

--- SENTENCE 1177 ---
Further, the Tongan Trench has been producing tsunamis such as this for the entire 1.5 million year lifetime of Tutuila island.

--- SENTENCE 1178 ---
If tsunamis averaged one per 150 years, there would have been 10,000 tsunamis in the life of Tutuila, and they will continue to happen in the future.

--- SENTENCE 1179 ---
The reefs recovered from previous tsunamis, or they would not be here now.

--- SENTENCE 1180 ---
The reefs should recover from this tsunami, if the

reefs are not weakened by chronic human influences.

--- SENTENCE 1181 ---
New growth has already begun where branches were broken off.

--- SENTENCE 1182 ---
The effects of tsunami damage to the reef on fish populations are not yet known.

--- SENTENCE 1183 ---
The 2009 tsunami produced rubble fields on some areas of the reef, both reducing life coral cover and rugosity severely.

--- SENTENCE 1184 ---
Turf algae are highly likely to bloom on the newly exposed dead surfaces as already seen in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 1185 ---
The turf may serve as good fish food.

--- SENTENCE 1186 ---
In roughly 6 months to a year, much of the turf may be replaced by coralline algae.

#### **Sediment**

Tutuila and the other high islands of American Samoa are undergoing relatively rapid natural erosion.

--- SENTENCE 1187 ---
Exposure to oxygen oxidizes the minerals in the basalt rock, and water hydrates the minerals, and together they convert hard rock into fine sand and soil, starting by widening any cracks in the rock until pieces of rock fall off.

--- SENTENCE 1188 ---
The rainfall is high, about 100 inches per year at the airport and up to 300 on mountain tops (Craig, 2009).

--- SENTENCE 1189 ---
Tutuila is much more eroded than younger islands like Ta'u, with the classic V-shaped valleys produced by rainwater erosion on Tutuila, and mostly the rounded slopes of the original shield volcano on Ta'u.

--- SENTENCE 1190 ---
Further, Tutuila is much more eroded than the Kohala mountains on the northwestern corner of the Big Island of Hawaii, where the mountains are of a similar age and origin, but rainfall is much less.

--- SENTENCE 1191 ---
