Active erosion on Tutuila can be seen in the form of frequent small landslides on the steeper slopes, the very brown water in some streams after heavy rains, and the stream deltas on the reef flats.

--- SENTENCE 1192 ---
Streams commonly have small deltas consisting of basalt rocks and sand, carried down by the streams and deposited on the reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 1193 ---
Coral cannot live under the delta or on it.

--- SENTENCE 1194 ---
During heavy runoff, silt is carried by the fresh water in the stream out onto the reef flat, where it floats on top of the other water.

--- SENTENCE 1195 ---
Often there is an ava near a stream mouth (the stream may be the original cause of the ava).

--- SENTENCE 1196 ---
Water is pumped onto the reef flat by waves breaking over the crest, and flows across the reef flat to the ava, and then flows rapidly out the ava.

--- SENTENCE 1197 ---
So the fresh water from the stream which floats on top of the salt water is drawn rapidly out the ava.

--- SENTENCE 1198 ---
Once out the ava, the fresh water carrying silt spreads out, and is mixed with deeper water by waves, particularly near the crest where the waves break.

--- SENTENCE 1199 ---
Over time, the silt sinks in the water column.

--- SENTENCE 1200 ---
However, wave surge on the slope tends to re-suspend the silt continually if any settles on the bottom.

--- SENTENCE 1201 ---
On occasion, this can be seen clearly- once at Leone, when water was rough, there were so many sediment particles in the water that visibility was reduced to perhaps 10 m, and sand grains could be seen settling onto my slate.

--- SENTENCE 1202 ---
The silt is then continually diluted and carried off in the water.

--- SENTENCE 1203 ---
Although water on the reef slopes is relatively clear on a normal day, with around 25 m visibility, the reef slope water is noticeably less clear than water far offshore.

--- SENTENCE 1204 ---
In bays, water from streams is likely to be carried away much more slowly.

--- SENTENCE 1205 ---
The turbidity in some bays, such as Fagasa and Vatia, is lower, which may be due in large part to suspended sediment.

--- SENTENCE 1206 ---
In some places, such as Fagasa, brown sediment covers dead surfaces, and in Vatia and the slope at Fagasa, the sand between corals is grey, and lifting it in the water and dropping it produces a grey cloud of silt.

--- SENTENCE 1207 ---
Quantitative measurements of sedimentation rates using sediment traps by Maloy Sabater and his team have shown that sediment accumulation on the bottom is fastest in bays, and slowest outside bays, as shown in Figure 20.

--- SENTENCE 1208 ---
Part of this may simply be a matter of the distance from the stream mouth, which tends to be at the head of the bay.

--- SENTENCE 1209 ---
But part of it is also likely to be due to the reduced flushing at the head of the bay.

!

--- SENTENCE 1210 ---
Mean sedimentation rates for different locations, redrawn from Fenner et al. (2008).

--- SENTENCE 1211 ---
The amount of sediment released by different streams from a heavy rain will vary with the amount of rain, but will also vary between streams.

--- SENTENCE 1212 ---
The stream at Fagaalu appears to be among the streams that produce the most sediment, though streams in Fagatogo also produce large amounts of sediment.

--- SENTENCE 1213 ---
The dispersal process outlined above depends on wave action.

--- SENTENCE 1214 ---
The larger the waves, the more water that is pumped over onto the reef flat and the stronger the current going out the ava.

--- SENTENCE 1215 ---
In the inner harbor, there are no waves and there is no reef flat, so water released from streams into the harbor floats and spreads over the entire surface of the inner harbor, turning it all brown.

--- SENTENCE 1216 ---
Then in a few hours the color returns to green as the silt sinks below the visible surface waters.

--- SENTENCE 1217 ---
On occasion, heavy rains may be accompanied by still waters, and waves do not pump water over the reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 1218 ---
In that case, sediment can settle on the reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 1219 ---
When wave action resumes, most of that sediment will quickly be removed from the reef flat, where turbulence is powerful.

--- SENTENCE 1220 ---
Thus, silt is not present on the reef flat, and corals and other organisms there are not impacted by sediment from streams, except for the very small areas covered by stream deltas.

--- SENTENCE 1221 ---
It should be borne in mind that erosion is natural, and has been going on vigorously for 1.5 million years on Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 1222 ---
The shelf, which extends from 1-3 miles from the shoreline, is highly likely to be the outline of the original island, which was taller as well.

--- SENTENCE 1223 ---
The ―cockscomb‖ at Vatia which includes Paloa Island, is a huge dike.

--- SENTENCE 1224 ---
A vertical dike like that is formed when lava intrudes into a vertical crack in the side of a soft volcano.

--- SENTENCE 1225 ---
The lava which hardens is harder than the soft volcano, and resists erosion longer than the volcano.

--- SENTENCE 1226 ---
In this case, a huge amount of material has eroded away, leaving only part of the dike.

--- SENTENCE 1227 ---
This illustrates just how much material has been eroded away in 1.5 million years, probably more material than remains in the island above waterline today, literally cubic miles of material.

--- SENTENCE 1228 ---
Further, all that material had to go over the reef into deeper water, and it did so without killing the reef.

--- SENTENCE 1229 ---
It did it by the process described earlier with the freshwater floating and being carried out the ava.

--- SENTENCE 1230 ---
Although erosion is a natural process that cannot be stopped, and natural sedimentation is not more than the reef can stand, erosion and sedimentation are very real threats for our coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1231 ---
Heavy sedimentation smothers and kills corals, and muddy water absorbs light and can reduce the light needed for corals to produce energy (Rodgers, 1990;

--- SENTENCE 1232 ---
McCulloch et al. 2003; Fabricius, 2005).

--- SENTENCE 1233 ---
The higher levels of sediment in streams such as at Fagaalu and Fagatogo happen because of human disturbance in their watersheds.

--- SENTENCE 1234 ---
Natural erosion rates are much lower than after human disturbance.

--- SENTENCE 1235 ---
Removing vegetation can increase erosion rates (and thus sedimentation rates) by orders of magnitude, perhaps a thousand times greater.

--- SENTENCE 1236 ---
Corals can clean themselves with tiny hairs called ‗flagella‖ but there is a limit to how fast and how much they can clean themselves.

--- SENTENCE 1237 ---
Further, coral larvae can't attach and start to grow on silt or mud.

--- SENTENCE 1238 ---
If the limits for coral are exceeded, the corals will be smothered by the silt.

--- SENTENCE 1239 ---
Even if they aren't smothered, they have to spend energy cleaning themselves which they can't spend in growth or reproduction.

--- SENTENCE 1240 ---
So sedimentation is a stressor for corals, and can kill them.

--- SENTENCE 1241 ---
A good example of an area where corals were killed by sediment appears to be on the reef flat in Fagaalu near the school.

--- SENTENCE 1242 ---
Near the shore, all the corals are dead, whereas out at the edge of the reef flat they are alive.

--- SENTENCE 1243 ---
At the edge of the reef flat wave surge cleans the sediment off, but there is not enough wave action farther from the edge.

--- SENTENCE 1244 ---
On the reef slope, corals are sparse near the school.

--- SENTENCE 1245 ---
In the harbor, sediment may be one of the major factors that limited coral there before human populations increased, and may be a factor in their death since then.

--- SENTENCE 1246 ---
On the outer slope at Fagaalu there is silt mixed with the sand, but so far sedimentation rates appear not to have exceeded the ability of corals to clean themselves, and deep on the slope where wave surge cannot clean them there were high levels of healthy looking coral cover before damage by the 2009 tsunami.

--- SENTENCE 1247 ---
The areas killed by sedimentation so far may include some areas in the harbor, and near the school at Fagaalu, and certainly under all the stream deltas.

--- SENTENCE 1248 ---
Although this damage is very real, these are small areas of the reefs of Tutuila, and have nearly no impact on the total amount of habitat available.

--- SENTENCE 1249 ---
Coralline algae are even more sensitive to sediment than corals, yet they are abundant on much of our reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1250 ---
So impacts to date have been relatively small.

--- SENTENCE 1251 ---
On the other hand, sedimentation is a huge potential threat.

--- SENTENCE 1252 ---
Most of the island is covered with dense vegetation.

--- SENTENCE 1253 ---
Should that vegetation be cut, massive erosion and sedimentation would result, which could kill much of the reef coral.

--- SENTENCE 1254 ---
This appears to be an unlikely scenario, as the trees are not commercially valuable for logging and the slopes far too steep to allow logging.

--- SENTENCE 1255 ---
Some clearing for houses and taro plots occurs on steep lower slopes, and they can indeed lead to increased erosion.

--- SENTENCE 1256 ---
Better planting practices such as heavy mulch, contour rows and planting vetevier barricades can reduce erosion even in steep taro plots.

--- SENTENCE 1257 ---
Better building practices can reduce soil erosion during construction.

--- SENTENCE 1258 ---
So it appears that while there is a potential for disastrous sediment runoff, this is not likely to happen.

--- SENTENCE 1259 ---
So sedimentation has caused relatively little damage to the reef habitat (as can be seen in the relatively good coral cover and abundant coralline algae) and the threat of disastrous sedimentation is remote.

--- SENTENCE 1260 ---
Although at times sedimentation has been blamed for a perceived lack of fish, the fact that there is good coral cover shows that the intermediate step in the logical chain is missing.

--- SENTENCE 1261 ---
To show that sedimentation has reduced fish stocks would require showing that sediment had reduced coral cover, and that reduced coral cover caused reduced fish stocks.

--- SENTENCE 1262 ---
Neither of these has been demonstrated, and in fact the coral cover is relatively good.

--- SENTENCE 1263 ---
So if there are any deficits in the fish populations, sedimentation is not the cause.

#### **Nutrients**

The term ―nutrients‖ is used here for organic nitrogen and phosphorus.

--- SENTENCE 1264 ---
Most coral reefs live in ―oligotrophic‖ waters, that is with low levels of nutrients, similar to tropical ocean surface waters.

--- SENTENCE 1265 ---
When there is a significant nutrient input to a coral reef, this may stimulate the growth of macroalgae.

--- SENTENCE 1266 ---
Algae are like plants on land, they grow slowly if they do not have nutrients, but grow faster if nutrients are available.

--- SENTENCE 1267 ---
The low level of nutrients in surface waters is why tropical ocean surface water is a clear blue.

--- SENTENCE 1268 ---
One view of the effect of nutrients is that in low nutrients corals dominate, in medium nutrients benthic algae dominate, and at high nutrients phytoplankton in the water column dominates.

--- SENTENCE 1269 ---
In the section on Pago Pago Harbor above, the red tide blooms in the harbor and the link to nutrients are discussed.

--- SENTENCE 1270 ---
Herbivory is also an important controller for algae.

--- SENTENCE 1271 ---
In the absence of herbivores, newly opened substrate is likely to be quickly colonized by algae.

--- SENTENCE 1272 ---
If the algae are not removed by herbivores, then they can overgrow and may be able to out compete corals.

--- SENTENCE 1273 ---
When Hurricane Allen in 1980 broke and killed much of the coral on the north shore of Jamaica, corals began to regrow but when a disease went through the entire Caribbean in 1983-4 and killed almost all sea urchins, too few herbivores were left to control the algae, because Jamaican reefs had been overfished for centuries and almost all herbivorous fish were long gone.

--- SENTENCE 1274 ---
Macroalgal cover increased on Caribbean reefs from about 4% in 1981 to 43% in 1987 (Côté et al. 2006).

--- SENTENCE 1275 ---
The result was a shift from a coral-dominated reef to algae beds in what has been called a ―phase shift‖ (e.g., Done, 1992; Knowlton, 1992; Hughes, 1994; Scully and Ostrander, 2001; Bellwood et al. 2004).

--- SENTENCE 1276 ---
Presently there is considerable controversy on the relative importance of top-down control of algae by herbivores vs. bottom-up control by nutrients (e.g., McCook, 1999; McCook et al. 2001; Szmant, 2002; Birrell et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 1277 ---
The literature is too voluminous to review here, but there is evidence to support the importance of both factors in different situations.

--- SENTENCE 1278 ---
Thus, nutrients are at least a potential threat.

--- SENTENCE 1279 ---
Nutrients may already be having impacts.

--- SENTENCE 1280 ---
In Vatia Bay, in 2007 there was a sudden increase in the amount of the brown macroalga Dictyota.

--- SENTENCE 1281 ---
This is a chemically-defended alga which is one of the genera that have been found in phase shifts.

--- SENTENCE 1282 ---
It grew over sand, other algae, and corals at Vatia, and may damage those corals.

--- SENTENCE 1283 ---
Smaller amounts of Dictyota are now present at some other locations.

--- SENTENCE 1284 ---
At Coconut Point, another brown macroalga, Padina, has bloomed.

--- SENTENCE 1285 ---
It is abundant near the shoreline starting not far west from the stream, and extending southward in a widening swath near the shore of Coconut Point.

--- SENTENCE 1286 ---
This is the path that water takes as it leaves the pool, and appears to be the path that nutrients would take if they were coming out of the stream.

--- SENTENCE 1287 ---
Nutrients from all sources can add to the total nutrient levels in the water which stimulate algal growth.

--- SENTENCE 1288 ---
Major nutrient sources likely include piggeries, fertilizer put on playing fields and croplands, leakage from septic systems in areas where there are such systems, and highphosphate laundry detergent.

--- SENTENCE 1289 ---
Nutrient runoff from piggeries has been declining due to a vigorous program by ASEPA to train people about how to better manage their piggeries and relocate them away from streams as well as enforcement of laws limiting how close they can be to streams.

--- SENTENCE 1290 ---
High-phosphate detergent is a recent introduction by Asian run stores that import cheap high-phosphate detergent from other countries.

--- SENTENCE 1291 ---
High-phosphate detergent was made illegal in the U.S. long ago, and an executive order banned the import of it in AS right after the

problem was discovered, but two years later it is still in the stores as enforcement has been lacking.

--- SENTENCE 1292 ---
Macroalgae on the reef slopes consists mainly of the green calcareous alga Halimeda, and the leafy red calcareous alga Peysonellia.

--- SENTENCE 1293 ---
Neither of these are known threats.

--- SENTENCE 1294 ---
Brown macroalgae are known threats, but they remain rare on reef slopes, and abundant in only a few near-shore sites, where they have increased in abundance.

--- SENTENCE 1295 ---
Another possible bioindicator of nutrient overload is cyanobacteria.

--- SENTENCE 1296 ---
These are a type of photosynthetic bacteria that make very fine strings in slimy filamentous masses.

--- SENTENCE 1297 ---
They are particularly sensitive to nutrient input.

--- SENTENCE 1298 ---
Around Tutuila they are fairly uncommon, but are sometimes found in patches that could be locations of seepage of terrestrial water carrying nutrients.

--- SENTENCE 1299 ---
However, no signs of fresh water springs have been observed by the author.

--- SENTENCE 1300 ---
It is also possible that they are a natural phenomenon not due to elevated nutrients.

--- SENTENCE 1301 ---
EPA testing found slightly elevated phosphate levels all around the island including away from people, so it appears that the island naturally produces some phosphate runoff (Fenner et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 1302 ---
On Rose Atoll, the steel wreck of a longline fishing vessel has stimulated the heavy growth of cyanobacteria and turf in a large area around the wreck (Schroeder et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 1303 ---
The wreck has now been removed, but some cyanobacteria linger.

--- SENTENCE 1304 ---
It appears that iron may be a trace nutrient that stimulates the growth of the cyanobacteria.

--- SENTENCE 1305 ---
Thus, there is at this point no hard evidence of nutrient overload on the reef slopes outside bays, nor damage to the habitat in terms of abundant brown algae or cyanobacteria, or reduced coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 1306 ---
So while it remains a possible future threat, it does not have demonstrated impacts on the reef slope yet.

#### **Pollution**

Chemical pollution in the waters of Tutuila are likely of two main sources: from the shipyards in the harbor, and diffuse non-point sources elsewhere on the island.

--- SENTENCE 1307 ---
The shipyards likely produced significant chemical pollution from cleaning boat hulls and other work, much of which probably now resides in the mud in that area of the harbor.

--- SENTENCE 1308 ---
The shipyards are at the inward end of the tuna canneries, and are not near coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1309 ---
Non-point pollution likely comes from human activities around the island, and is likely heavier in areas of greater human population density.

--- SENTENCE 1310 ---
The EPA program studying the effects of non-point pollution on reefs is described elsewhere, but the effects on the reef habitat are likely relatively minor, since coral cover does not correlate with the human population.

### **Debris and Trash**

Levels of debris and trash on the reefs are relatively low.

--- SENTENCE 1311 ---
Currents do not accumulate plastic trash on the reefs as they do on the Northwestern Hawaiian Islands.

--- SENTENCE 1312 ---
All debris and trash appears to be locally derived.

--- SENTENCE 1313 ---
Occasionally, plastic bags are seen caught on corals, but this is the exception rather than the rule.

--- SENTENCE 1314 ---
No trash has ever been recorded in the DMWR Territorial Monitoring Plan belt transects for invertebrates.

--- SENTENCE 1315 ---
There are a few ship wrecks, with one longliner lying close to the reef at Taema Banks, and one of the tender boats from a purse seiner on Taema Banks.

--- SENTENCE 1316 ---
The 2009 tsunami placed debris on reefs, primarily at Fagasa and Leone, but it was still much less than the debris on land.

#### **Fishing including gleaning**

Fishing can have a wide variety of effects on ecosystems in addition to direct effects on fish populations.

--- SENTENCE 1317 ---
On coral reefs, the effects of fishing on the coral reef habitat can be divided into direct and indirect effects of the fishing.

--- SENTENCE 1318 ---
The direct effects of fishing activities on coral reefs include damage from destructive fishing methods (blast fishing and toxic chemicals), trampling during fishing and gleaning, net entanglement, damage from fish traps, damage from collecting efforts for invertebrates or fish, and the collecting of corals and live rock.

--- SENTENCE 1319 ---
The indirect effects include the loss of herbivores that keep algae in check in its competition with coral.

--- SENTENCE 1320 ---
Blast fishing is thought by some to occur in American Samoa, and on occasion reported.

--- SENTENCE 1321 ---
An event reported from Fagatele Bay was investigated, and damage was observed, however any direct evidence of the blast such as fragments of the equipment used, was not found.

--- SENTENCE 1322 ---
Reports of blast fishing and damage that could be attributed to it are rare.

--- SENTENCE 1323 ---
An ichthyocide derived from a native plant was used in traditional Samoan fishing before the arrival of Europeans.

--- SENTENCE 1324 ---
It appears that it is relatively rarely used currently.

--- SENTENCE 1325 ---
All types of destructive fishing are prohibited by the fisheries regulations.

--- SENTENCE 1326 ---
In addition, the collection or damaging of coral is illegal, and there has never been a coral or live rock export industry.

--- SENTENCE 1327 ---
Sand and dead coral rubble are collected from beaches for construction although a Department of Parks and Recreation regulation prohibits it.

--- SENTENCE 1328 ---
The amounts collected do not appear to be particularly large, and the damage appears to be primarily to the beaches, not the reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1329 ---
Cyanide is not reported to be used to collect fish, and reef fish are not exported for the aquarium trade or food.

--- SENTENCE 1330 ---
Residents do at times glean invertebrates from the reef flats at low tide, but currently all reef fishing including gleaning occurs at low levels.

--- SENTENCE 1331 ---
Some fishing methods, such as throw net and pole and line, commonly involve walking on the reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 1332 ---
Like all forms of reef fishing it has decreased in frequency in recent years to low levels.

--- SENTENCE 1333 ---
It is true that walking on the reef flat damages the reef by breaking live coral.

--- SENTENCE 1334 ---
However, in recent years, low tide events have had a far more serious effect on reef flat corals than humans walking on the reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 1335 ---
Low tide events kill all coral on the entire reef flats throughout the territory (except where the reef flat is lower than the low tide level), while walking only damages corals under the feet of the walkers, which are tiny relative to the reef.

--- SENTENCE 1336 ---
Only if there are large numbers of reef walkers do they have a significant effect on the coral.

--- SENTENCE 1337 ---
Observation of the reef flats over the last six years has shown areas of live unbroken branching (Acropora) coral in the beginning of that period which subsequently died in place due to low tide events.

--- SENTENCE 1338 ---
Significant amounts of coral on the reef flat that were mechanically broken were not observed.

--- SENTENCE 1339 ---
Nets are very rarely observed entangled on the reefs of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1340 ---
To the author's knowledge, only one such net has been observed in the last six years, at Aunu'u on the reef slope, where it was removed by DMWR divers in 2005.

--- SENTENCE 1341 ---
It did little damage to coral, and some corals were seen growing on it.

--- SENTENCE 1342 ---
Fish traps are not used in American Samoa, other than small traps used to catch newly recruited goatfish, and they are only still used in Manu'a.

--- SENTENCE 1343 ---
One invertebrate that is traditionally harvested is the corallimorph polyp, Rhodactis sp. (malu-malu).

--- SENTENCE 1344 ---
This polyp attaches very tenaciously to rock, and in order to remove it, it is necessary to remove some of the rock.

--- SENTENCE 1345 ---
The result of efforts to collect it to eat is damage to reef rock.

--- SENTENCE 1346 ---
However, only very small amounts of it appear to be harvested, and the damage appears to be tiny relative to the size of the reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1347 ---
The removal of herbivorous fish (or invertebrates such as urchins) can affect the reef habitat, by allowing more algae to grow.

--- SENTENCE 1348 ---
This is thought to be the critical factor causing the shift from coral communities to algae beds in some Caribbean reefs, such as in Jamaica.

--- SENTENCE 1349 ---
In Jamaica, a hurricane broke most of the coral, opening new surfaces for colonization by algae.

--- SENTENCE 1350 ---
Two years after the hurricane, a disease swept through the Caribbean killing almost all sea urchins.

--- SENTENCE 1351 ---
In Jamaica, the reefs had been chronically overfished for a very long time, with few fish of any type remaining.

--- SENTENCE 1352 ---
After the urchins died, the algae bloomed, in some cases growing over and damaging corals, but also greatly reducing coral recruitment which could have restored the coral reef.

--- SENTENCE 1353 ---
The reefs there remain dominated by algae decades later.

--- SENTENCE 1354 ---
The amount of herbivores, and particularly herbivorous fish, that are needed to keep algae levels low and ward off phase shifts to algal beds is not known, and may depend in part on other events, such as disturbances that open new substrate.

--- SENTENCE 1355 ---
Reefs in American Samoa currently have low abundances of macroalgae and high abundance of coralline algae, suggesting that herbivory levels are currently good.

--- SENTENCE 1356 ---
The reef fish communities are dominated by surgeonfish, small parrotfish, and damselfish, all of which are abundant.

--- SENTENCE 1357 ---
The most abundant of all fish species, Ctenochaetus striatus, is actually not an herbivore though it is often lumped with herbivores because most surgeonfish are herbivores.

--- SENTENCE 1358 ---
Sabater and Tofaeono (2007) report that herbivorous fish including C. striatus compose 66% of the total fish biomass, and that C. striatus composes 22% of the total biomass, so true herbivorous fish compose about 44% of the total biomass.

--- SENTENCE 1359 ---
This appears to be a very good biomass of herbivorous fish.

--- SENTENCE 1360 ---
Two relatively pristine reefs in the Line Islands are reported to have 6 and 16% of their biomass to be herbivores (Sandin et al. 2008), while the Northwest Hawaiian Islands are reported to have 29% (Friedlander and DiMartini, 2002).

--- SENTENCE 1361 ---
It is not clear whether Ctenochaetus sp. were included as herbivores in those studies or not, or whether they were abundant.

--- SENTENCE 1362 ---
The comparison of the percentage of total biomass that herbivores comprise is affected by the biomass of other trophic groups, in particular those three relatively pristine areas have much higher biomass of apex predators than American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1363 ---
The amount of herbivory does not depend on the proportion of all fish biomass that herbivores represent, but rather the absolute biomass.

--- SENTENCE 1364 ---
Absolute biomass of herbivorous fish that were obtained with the same methods are not yet available for comparison between American Samoa and pristine reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1365 ---
Comparison can be made between different studies that used different methods.

--- SENTENCE 1366 ---
Sabater and Tofaeono (2007) report about 1.5 T/ha of herbivores, while the Line Islands were reported to have about 0.3 and 0.4 T/ha, and the Northwest Hawaiian Islands about 0.7 T/ha.

--- SENTENCE 1367 ---
This supports the view that American Samoa has very good stocks of herbivorous fish, which is consistent with the low levels of macroalgae recorded on the reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1368 ---
#### **Introduced and invasive species**

Introduced species are species that were not naturally present before humans brought them to an area.

--- SENTENCE 1369 ---
An invasive species is one that expands rapidly after introduction, and often causes significant damage to the ecosystem and can damage crops or other resources.

--- SENTENCE 1370 ---
A study of introduced marine species in American Samoa found 28 non-indigenous or cryptogenic species, none of which were invasive (Coles et al. 2003).

--- SENTENCE 1371 ---
Non-indigenous species are introduced species, and cryptogenic means that their origin is not clear.

--- SENTENCE 1372 ---
This is much less than the 490 known introduced and cryptogenic marine species in Hawaii (Carleton and

Eldredge, 2009), several of which are invasive, including several algae, a soft coral, and a red sponge.

--- SENTENCE 1373 ---
The lower number of introduced species in American Samoa compared to Hawaii is probably due to the much smaller amount of shipping that comes to American Samoa than Hawaii, since most introduced species arrive on some form of ship.

--- SENTENCE 1374 ---
Two native species show some signs of being invasive, meaning that they may expand at the expense of other native species for one reason or another.

--- SENTENCE 1375 ---
One of these is a colonial ascidian or sea squirt, Diplosoma simile.

--- SENTENCE 1376 ---
This species is not rare on the reefs of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1377 ---
It is capable of growing over living corals, but had not previously been reported to have caused problems.

--- SENTENCE 1378 ---
It was reported to have become more common in one area of Fagatele Bay at one point (W. Kiene, personal comm.), but later was not found to be abundant any more.

--- SENTENCE 1379 ---
In 2006, it was found to have suddenly become very abundant on the reefs of Swains Island, and was the dominant organism on the northeast of that island.

--- SENTENCE 1380 ---
It had grown over live corals and likely killed them (Vargas-Ángel et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 1381 ---
The cause of this outbreak is not known for sure, but may be because Hurricane Heta opened new substrate in 2004.

--- SENTENCE 1382 ---
It seems unlikely to be caused by humans, as Swains has only about 10 workers on it.

--- SENTENCE 1383 ---
Early in 2010 the CRED program will return to American Samoa, and will find out the fate of this outbreak.

--- SENTENCE 1384 ---
Another species of concern is the corallimorph, Rhodactis sp.

--- SENTENCE 1385 ---
This is a soft polyp about 4 cm diameter that does not produce a skeleton but is very tightly bonded to rock substrate.

--- SENTENCE 1386 ---
It divides frequently and forms clusters of polyps.

--- SENTENCE 1387 ---
It is able to sting some corals, kill them, and grow over them.

--- SENTENCE 1388 ---
It is about in the middle of the aggression pecking order of hard corals, so while it can defeat some, other corals can defeat it (Langmead and Chadwick-Furman 1999a,b; Chadwick-Furman and Spiegel, 2000; Chadwick-Furman et al. 2000; Kuguru et al. 2004; 2007).

--- SENTENCE 1389 ---
Work et al. (2008) report in the Line Islands that it has become abundant around a steel shipwreck, now covering about a million square meters of reef.

--- SENTENCE 1390 ---
Smaller patches were found around two steel buoys, suggesting that iron may stimulate their growth.

--- SENTENCE 1391 ---
In American Samoa, it is well known in traditional Samoan culture, where it is called matu-matu, and eaten (Madrigal, 1999).

--- SENTENCE 1392 ---
It must be cooked, otherwise it is poisonous.

--- SENTENCE 1393 ---
Patches of it are fairly common in Vatia Bay and Tafeu Bay.

--- SENTENCE 1394 ---
In Tafeu, it increased in cover in 2007, while turf decreased, indicating it probably expanded over turf.

--- SENTENCE 1395 ---
It was photographed stinging some corals, and in Vatia it could be seen growing over some corals.

--- SENTENCE 1396 ---
It has not caused significant damage to any reefs yet, but it bears watching.

--- SENTENCE 1397 ---
Thus, it has had minimal impact, but is a threat.

--- SENTENCE 1398 ---
Overall, there are relatively few introduced marine species, none of which are invasive, and only a couple of native species that appear to have invasive potential, one of which has had a major impact on Swains Island reefs, but not elsewhere in the archipelago so far.

#### **Bleaching**

When corals are exposed to temperatures 1-2<sup>0</sup>C above the average summer high temperatures in the area they live, the single-celled algae called zooxanthellae (which are in the dinoflagellate group) leave the coral.

--- SENTENCE 1399 ---
Most of the color in coral tissue is from these zooxanthellae, so once they are gone, the coral tissue becomes transparent.

--- SENTENCE 1400 ---
This allows light to penetrate to the white skeleton and be reflected, so that the white skeleton can be seen and the coral appears white.

--- SENTENCE 1401 ---
If the temperatures go no higher and then recede, the corals will survive and regain their zooxanthellae and color.

--- SENTENCE 1402 ---
If temperatures go higher to 3-4<sup>0</sup>C above the average summer high,

then the corals are likely to be killed.

--- SENTENCE 1403 ---
Actually, the amount of time that the corals are exposed to these temperatures also is an important factor, and bleaching can be predicted on the basis of ―degree-heating weeks,‖ that is, the number of weeks of exposure times the number of degrees above the normal summer high.

--- SENTENCE 1404 ---
This relationship is so strong that coral bleaching events can be predicted quite well with satellite-sensed sea surface temperatures (SST's) compared to the average summer highs, coupled with time spent at that temperature.

--- SENTENCE 1405 ---
Corals differ somewhat in their susceptibility, and in general branching species are more susceptible than massive, and Acropora, Millepora, and Pocillopora are among the most sensitive genera.

--- SENTENCE 1406 ---
It is often said that corals live just a few degrees below lethal temperatures.

--- SENTENCE 1407 ---
Global warming means that temperatures are rising around the world, including in American Samoan waters, as shown in Figure 21.

--- SENTENCE 1408 ---
Bleaching events occur in years when temperatures exceed the normal summer maximum, as shown in Figure 21.

--- SENTENCE 1409 ---
Because of the overall increasing temperature trend, corals are predicted to bleach more intensely and have more mortality in the future in years with higher than average temperatures.

--- SENTENCE 1410 ---
In addition, eventually temperatures will rise to the point when every summer reaches a temperature at which corals bleach, and mortalities are so great during the higher temperature years and they come so often, that reef coral communities cannot recover between bleaching events (Sheppard, 2003).

--- SENTENCE 1411 ---
At that point coral communities will be on a downhill course, loosing more and more corals with each event.

--- SENTENCE 1412 ---
In American Samoa, mass bleaching events when many or most of the corals on reef slopes bleach, were observed in 1994, 2002, and 2003.

--- SENTENCE 1413 ---
The events in 1994 and 2003 were more intense events than in 2002.

--- SENTENCE 1414 ---
Goreau and Hayes (1994) reported some coral deaths at some locations in 1994.

--- SENTENCE 1415 ---
More recently, staghorn corals (A. muricata, A. nobilis, and A. pulchra) were found to bleach every summer in back reef pools in Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 1416 ---
The intensity of bleaching has been recorded in the airport pool and Alofau pool starting in early 2004 (Figure 22).

--- SENTENCE 1417 ---
Bleaching in the pools correlates with sea surface temperatures (Figure 23).

!

--- SENTENCE 1418 ---
Sea surface temperature trends in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1419 ---
Figure from U.S. EPA (2007).

!

--- SENTENCE 1420 ---
Bleaching events at Airport Backreef Pool.

--- SENTENCE 1421 ---
Bleaching at Airport Backreef Pool, 2003-2007.

--- SENTENCE 1422 ---
There has been relatively little mortality in the pools, though many of the staghorns in Alofau are close to death most summers.

--- SENTENCE 1423 ---
Other species of Acropora (which are rare in the pools) and fire corals (Millepora dichotoma) also bleach, but finger corals (P. cylindrica) do not bleach except one small patch at Fagaalu, and other corals also do not bleach.

--- SENTENCE 1424 ---
Corals which bleach are unlikely to be able to grow while bleached, and are unlikely to be able to reproduce for at least a year after bleaching.

--- SENTENCE 1425 ---
The staghorns in Alofau spend very little time each year with none of them bleached.

--- SENTENCE 1426 ---
The largest pool, at the airport, has had essentially no mortality so no reduction in habitat quality, but the pools are a very small part of the overall reef area.

--- SENTENCE 1427 ---
Bleaching in the pools is the first annual summer mass bleaching of a multi-species coral community reported in the world.

--- SENTENCE 1428 ---
Such bleaching is predicted to be commonplace around the world within a few decades.

--- SENTENCE 1429 ---
In some of the pools, as much as half of the staghorn coral was already dead when monitoring of bleaching began early in 2004.

--- SENTENCE 1430 ---
Most of the dead staghorn coral was still standing.

--- SENTENCE 1431 ---
Clearly it was not killed by a hurricane, since that would have broken it.

--- SENTENCE 1432 ---
More likely it was killed by the bleaching events, since the staghorns in the pool bleach more than those on the slope and some are near death most summers while there is no bleaching on the slope.

--- SENTENCE 1433 ---
When temperatures rose higher during events and produced bleaching and some death on the slopes, they almost certainly caused a great deal of death among the staghorns in the pools.

--- SENTENCE 1434 ---
So bleaching has already had a significant impact on small groups of corals, and is probably the biggest threat faced by our corals in the next several decades.

#### **Disease**

Coral disease, mainly White Band Disease, was the primary cause of the loss of coral in the Caribbean (Aronson and Precht, 2001; Precht and Aronson, 2006), which went from about 55% coral cover in the 1970's, to about 8% cover today (Gardiner et al. 2003).

--- SENTENCE 1435 ---
Thus, it stands as a major threat to coral reefs, based on the actual impact so far.

--- SENTENCE 1436 ---
Until recently, nothing was known of coral diseases in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1437 ---
The impetus to begin the study of diseases here came from the observation by P. Craig of a considerable amount of coral disease that killed many colonies.

--- SENTENCE 1438 ---
Subsequently, baseline surveying of coral diseases in

American Samoa revealed a diverse set of diseases (13 types distinguished) but low levels of incidence, a prevalence of only 0.4% on the average (Fenner et al. 2008; Aeby et al. in press).

--- SENTENCE 1439 ---
In addition, two diseases of coralline algae were found.

--- SENTENCE 1440 ---
It turned out that the disease outbreak noticed by P. Craig was following a mass bleaching event in 2003.

--- SENTENCE 1441 ---
There are now several reports of disease outbreaks following mass coral bleaching events (e.g., Jones et al. 2004; Bruno et al. 2007; Harvell et al. 2007; Wilkinson and Souter, 2008; Miller et al. 2006).

--- SENTENCE 1442 ---
There is independent evidence that some coral diseases spread much faster at higher temperatures, and it is likely that bleaching weakens the corals resistance to disease.

--- SENTENCE 1443 ---
After the outbreak of disease following mass bleaching, disease abundance returned to low levels, and currently has little impact on coral abundance.

--- SENTENCE 1444 ---
However, it remains a major threat for the future and we must remain vigilant.

#### **Summary**

Crown-of-thorns starfish outbreaks have had the greatest damaging effects on the reefs of Tutuila, followed by hurricanes and tsunamis.

--- SENTENCE 1445 ---
All of these are natural events, though the crownof-thorns outbreaks could have a human component.

--- SENTENCE 1446 ---
Mass coral bleaching and the disease outbreaks it produces may rank with the hurricanes and tsunamis in terms of impact.

--- SENTENCE 1447 ---
Mass coral bleaching and the disease outbreaks that it produces are primarily an effect of global warming, which is produced by humans, primarily by burning fossil fuels.

--- SENTENCE 1448 ---
The other threats and impacts are all produced by humans.

--- SENTENCE 1449 ---
Although each of these other threats and impacts is relatively minor, they are chronic stresses on the reef.

--- SENTENCE 1450 ---
These stresses are likely additive (Hughes and Connell, 1999), and add to the stresses produced by major natural events, and the significant effects of mass coral bleaching and disease outbreaks.

--- SENTENCE 1451 ---
The reefs are well adapted to the natural stressors, having survived them for the past 1.5 million years.

--- SENTENCE 1452 ---
Further, the natural stressors are acute, that is, brief, and when over, the reef can recover free of stressors.

--- SENTENCE 1453 ---
Most of the human stressors, however, are chronic.

--- SENTENCE 1454 ---
So sediment is continual, as is fishing, chemical pollution, and so on.

--- SENTENCE 1455 ---
They tend to be diffuse and mild, and therefore hard to locate and measure.

--- SENTENCE 1456 ---
Although most are minor, together they may have an impact.

--- SENTENCE 1457 ---
Their impact could be to hold the average coral cover lower than the natural level, or to cause the reef to recover more slowly from major natural disturbances, or there may be additional effects.

--- SENTENCE 1458 ---
Although the chronic stresses humans produce may be weakening the resilience of the reefs, we do not yet know that to be a fact, so although it remains a possibility, it is not a known fact.

--- SENTENCE 1459 ---
So far we cannot prove they have damaged the reef habitat value, but in the future they make it more difficult for the reef to recover from the major coral bleaching events we know are coming.

--- SENTENCE 1460 ---
Benthic communities as fish habitat

#### **Coral reef**

It is often said that reef fish populations depend on the existence of healthy corals.

--- SENTENCE 1461 ---
This is often simplified as something like: ‗if we want to have good fish catches, we need good coral, because coral is the home of the fish.'

--- SENTENCE 1462 ---
Such statements are often made to try to encourage local village people to protect and conserve the corals on their reefs, since their fish catches depend on healthy coral populations.

--- SENTENCE 1463 ---
Essentially, the argument uses the value of fish catches to fishers to try to increase the value of the coral, to encourage conservation.

--- SENTENCE 1464 ---
The goal of such educational efforts is commendable, but the assumptions it rests on are generally not examined in such

educational efforts.

--- SENTENCE 1465 ---
We would do well to examine those assumptions in scientific efforts to evaluate fish habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1466 ---
Habitat is where organisms live.

--- SENTENCE 1467 ---
Different species have different habitats, so bats live in trees and the air, freshwater fish live in freshwater, tuna live in the pelagic open ocean, and reef fish generally live on the reef.

--- SENTENCE 1468 ---
Different species of fish on reefs can and do live in different habitats, so, for instance, a few species specialize in living in sand, a few species specialize in living among the branches of specific corals, other species may live among algae, some may live only on the reef slope and others on the reef flat, and so on (e.g., Chittaro, 2004;

--- SENTENCE 1469 ---
Brokovich et al. 2006).

--- SENTENCE 1470 ---
Each species fills a niche.

--- SENTENCE 1471 ---
A niche differs from habitat, in that the niche is the range of variables the species can live in, for example the range of temperatures, depths, types of corals, light, size of holes, food they eat and so on.

--- SENTENCE 1472 ---
A niche is the capability of the species, an abstraction, while the habitat is the actual place the organism lives, which has many different aspects like temperature, depth, and so on, some of which may be important to the fish and others not important.

--- SENTENCE 1473 ---
Coral reefs are very patchy as stated before, spatially heterogeneous, and so the habitat can vary greatly over short distances.

--- SENTENCE 1474 ---
Fish may respond to that, and be found commonly in one type of habitat patch but not in another.

--- SENTENCE 1475 ---
Further, within species there are often changes in habitat utilized with age.

--- SENTENCE 1476 ---
Young fish may require something quite different from older fish.

--- SENTENCE 1477 ---
A newly recruited fish might be found in seagrass, then as it grows move to mangroves, and finally as an adult move to coral reef.

--- SENTENCE 1478 ---
Or it might begin life in shallow habitats like reef flats or lagoons, and move to deeper habitats like reef slopes with age and growth.

--- SENTENCE 1479 ---
Or it may spend its entire life in one small patch of coral on a reef slope.

--- SENTENCE 1480 ---
Habitat can also differ by time of day, so a fish can move from one area at one time of day to another at a different time of the day or night.

--- SENTENCE 1481 ---
It can also differ seasonally or on a lunar cycle, or tidal cycle.

--- SENTENCE 1482 ---
In a place like a reef flat, tides are likely to be a powerful variable, with many fish present at high tide but very few at the lowest low tides.

--- SENTENCE 1483 ---
Most fish species also have depth ranges and depths at which they are most common.

--- SENTENCE 1484 ---
Further, species home ranges differ hugely, from a single coral head of about 30 cm diameter to several km.

--- SENTENCE 1485 ---
Species that range over large areas may include many habitats in their range.

--- SENTENCE 1486 ---
Habitats cannot be defined by humans, they are a property of the organisms.

--- SENTENCE 1487 ---
One cannot define reef flats as juvenile fish habitat, or else I could define my front porch as juvenile fish habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1488 ---
Whether reef flats are juvenile fish habitat or not is an empirical question, and it is a different matter for each species of fish, and may be for each life stage of a fish, time of day, tidal cycle, etc.

--- SENTENCE 1489 ---
Empirically, clearly my front porch is not habitat for any species of fish.

--- SENTENCE 1490 ---
The question of what is habitat for a species must be answered by recording where that fish lives.

--- SENTENCE 1491 ---
Or, to find out what species a place is habitat for, by recording what species live there.

--- SENTENCE 1492 ---
So surveying a place like a reef flat can tell you what species of fish that reef flat is habitat for.

--- SENTENCE 1493 ---
But that doesn't tell you the habitat of the species.

--- SENTENCE 1494 ---
Reef flats may be only the juvenile habitat of the species, or it may be only one of several or many habitats of that species.

--- SENTENCE 1495 ---
So juveniles of that species may be found on the reef flat, and also on the reef slope, and among mangrove roots, and in seagrass beds, or in only some of those habitats.

--- SENTENCE 1496 ---
Which are habitats for a species can only be determined by surveying them all.

--- SENTENCE 1497 ---
If only reef flats are surveyed, and the species is found there, and it is proclaimed that reef flats are that species' habitat, it is clearly one habitat of that species, but finding it there does not preclude finding it elsewhere.

--- SENTENCE 1498 ---
It is also important to realize that English words such as ―lagoon‖ may not correspond well with actual habitat distinctions made by reef

organisms.

--- SENTENCE 1499 ---
The word ―lagoon‖ can be applied to a wide variety of structures, which fish species may or may not distinguish among.

--- SENTENCE 1500 ---
―For example, a lagoon can be a seagrass-dominated area adjacent to a bank-barrier reef on a small (< 1 km) or large (tens of km) scale, an enclosed lagoon connected through a narrow opening, or estuaries of different salinity regimes.‖ (Adams, et al. 2006)

--- SENTENCE 1501 ---
Or they can be on a scale as large as several thousand km (Great Barrier Reef) or lagoons of thousands of atolls in a wide range of sizes and depths (primarily in the Indo-Pacific).

--- SENTENCE 1502 ---
Use of such a word may not define a habitat at all.

--- SENTENCE 1503 ---
Another question is whether habitats are really discrete entities, whether there are sharp boundaries between them, physical or in definition, or is the reality actually that they fade into one another.

--- SENTENCE 1504 ---
Finding some members of a species in a particular habitat tells you relatively little about the biology of that fish.

--- SENTENCE 1505 ---
A list of species found in that habitat likewise provides limited information.

--- SENTENCE 1506 ---
That place is in fact habitat for those fish, but there is not much further that you can conclude.

--- SENTENCE 1507 ---
In particular, one of our problems is the small amounts of some habitats available, such as mangroves, seagrass, and perhaps reef flats.

--- SENTENCE 1508 ---
Just because there is a relatively small amount of mangroves present on Tutuila, and some individuals of a particular reef species were found in the mangroves, does not mean that the small amount of mangroves limits the abundance of that species on the reef.

--- SENTENCE 1509 ---
Many fish are highly mobile, and can choose where they go.

--- SENTENCE 1510 ---
If a small number choose to live in mangroves, and a large number choose to live on reefs, but only mangroves were surveyed, then finding individuals in the mangroves means mangroves are a habitat for a small number of individuals.

--- SENTENCE 1511 ---
It does not follow that the small amount of mangroves means that the small amount of mangroves causes low total abundances of adults of the species.

--- SENTENCE 1512 ---
A species could absolutely require a particular habitat like mangroves, or it could prefer mangroves, or it could prefer some other habitat but be capable of using them if other habitats are not available.

--- SENTENCE 1513 ---
If a fish species absolutely requires a particular habitat like mangroves, and it is not available, there will be no members of that species present.

--- SENTENCE 1514 ---
But if they are capable of living in other habitats even though they prefer to live in a habitat like mangroves, they can be present even if mangroves are not present.

--- SENTENCE 1515 ---
They may have lesser numbers without mangroves, or not.

--- SENTENCE 1516 ---
It is a logical fallacy to conclude from finding a species in a habitat that without that habitat the species will not be present, or present in reduced numbers.

--- SENTENCE 1517 ---
Many species may be capable of living in a variety of habitats.

--- SENTENCE 1518 ---
That depends on the niches that they fill, and for many species, surely most reef fish, the exact niches of the species have yet to be determined empirically.

--- SENTENCE 1519 ---
It is possible to look at whether the presence or absence of a habitat influences the abundance of a fish species, or see whether the distance to such a habitat influences the species abundance, or whether the amount of that habitat influences the abundance of the species.

--- SENTENCE 1520 ---
A few such studies have been carried out.

--- SENTENCE 1521 ---
As stated before, it is commonly assumed that live coral is required habitat for coral reef fish.

--- SENTENCE 1522 ---
Habitats are complex things, and it is not immediately obvious which aspects of those habitats are the most important features for each different species of fish.

--- SENTENCE 1523 ---
Which aspects are important is an empirical question, and each fish species may be different.

--- SENTENCE 1524 ---
Some fish, like a small number of butterfly fish (e.g., Chaetodon unimaculatus, C. ornatissimus, C. multicinctus, Chaetodon trifascialis, C. trifaciatus, C. baronessa, C. triangulum, and C. plebius) eat tentacles off coral (some are even specific to a genus like Acropora or to table corals), as do two small species of filefish (genus Oxymonacathus) and a small wrasse (Labrichthys unilineatus).

--- SENTENCE 1525 ---
These species cannot live without the corals they feed on, and when the corals die they disappear (Kokita and

Nakozono, 2001; Jones et al. 2004; Bellwood et al. 2006).

--- SENTENCE 1526 ---
Some pufferfish and parrotfish graze on coral as part of their diet, but if it is only part of their diet they may only be moderately affected by the loss of coral.

--- SENTENCE 1527 ---
Other species (such as the small gobies Gobiodon sp., and coral crouchers Caracanthus sp.) live deep among the branches of coral, and likely eat mucus or tentacles off the coral, or something else present in the live coral.

--- SENTENCE 1528 ---
They are likely to be quite specific about which species of coral they live with, perhaps based on the size and shapes of spaces, and or the food resources.

--- SENTENCE 1529 ---
Those species cannot live without those exact corals and very likely the corals have to be alive.

--- SENTENCE 1530 ---
Others may shelter among coral branches but feed on passing plankton, such as some damsels.

--- SENTENCE 1531 ---
Most of these species are small and unlikely to be utilized for food in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1532 ---
They also comprise a very small portion of the fish biodiversity on a reef.

--- SENTENCE 1533 ---
In Figure 24, the biomass of various genera of fish are presented, from sites around Tutuila, data courtesy of Benjamin Carroll.

--- SENTENCE 1534 ---
Surgeonfish and damselfish are not included in this graph.

--- SENTENCE 1535 ---
The highest biomass of fish is at Faga'alu, where the site is on the outer reef slope at 8-10 m depth.

--- SENTENCE 1536 ---
That site from about 2-15 m is dominated by a staghorn rubble bed covered with coralline algae, with little live coral, only about 12% live coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 1537 ---
Yet that site has far and away the highest fish biomass.

--- SENTENCE 1538 ---
Clearly, those fish are not heavily dependent on live coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 1539 ---
The dominant family at Faga'alu is parrotfish.

--- SENTENCE 1540 ---
Parrotfish are herbivores, and may be eating filamentous algae off of coralline algae surfaces.

!

--- SENTENCE 1541 ---
[](_page_88_Figure_2.jpeg)

Figure 24.Fish biomass of sites around Tutuila

 Figure 25 shows a scattergram of coral cover with the fish biomass presented in the previous figure.

--- SENTENCE 1542 ---
There is essentially no relationship between these two variables (the very small correlation is not significant).

--- SENTENCE 1543 ---
Figure 25 provides no support for the view that reef fish populations depend on live coral cover.

!

--- SENTENCE 1544 ---
Relationship of coral cover and fish biomass

Other evidence of this type comes from the study by Sabater and Tolaeono (2007).

--- SENTENCE 1545 ---
On the north side of Tutuila, outside of bays the substrate is "volcanic pavement" (basalt) that has low coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 1546 ---
In spite of the low coral cover, those areas actually were found to have higher fish biomasses than coral reef areas with higher coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 1547 ---
Other types of evidence can also be brought to bear on this question.

--- SENTENCE 1548 ---
There are a few events on coral reefs that can drastically reduce live coral cover, yet leave the skeletons standing, so that hiding spaces do not change.

--- SENTENCE 1549 ---
One such event is when a crown-of-thorns seastar (COTS) outbreak occurs.

--- SENTENCE 1550 ---
COTS eats the tissue off of the living corals, but does not damage the skeletons.

--- SENTENCE 1551 ---
The skeletons can stand for quite a few years before they are eventually weakened by boring organisms like sponges and collapse.

--- SENTENCE 1552 ---
During that period, the live coral cover has been dramatically decreased but the skeleton shapes are not changed.

--- SENTENCE 1553 ---
Just such an event occurred in 1978 in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1554 ---
Fish populations were surveyed before the COTS outbreak by Wass (1982) and resurveyed periodically later by others.

--- SENTENCE 1555 ---
The graph of fish populations over time starting with the Wass data, shown in Fenner et al. (2008) and Sabater and Carroll (2009), shows no drastic drop in fish populations after the COTS outbreak.

--- SENTENCE 1556 ---
Instead, it shows a 50% increase.

--- SENTENCE 1557 ---
If living coral tissue was a critical habitat variable (or niche) for many fish species, the populations should have dropped dramatically after the outbreak, yet there is no evidence of that in the total populations.

--- SENTENCE 1558 ---
This is not to say that the community composition was identical, in particular populations of those species totally dependent on corals for food surely dropped dramatically.

--- SENTENCE 1559 ---
But there are few such species, and the smaller and more cryptic species are surely not counted in transects.

--- SENTENCE 1560 ---
Another event in which only living coral tissues are removed, is mass coral bleaching.

--- SENTENCE 1561 ---
In a strong mass bleaching event, many or most living corals may be killed, yet all the skeletons are

left remaining intact.

--- SENTENCE 1562 ---
In the study of Jones et al. (2004), a study site in New Guinea experienced drastic live coral loss, but no change of the coral skeletons.

--- SENTENCE 1563 ---
Fish communities were surveyed in detail before and after.

--- SENTENCE 1564 ---
This study found that about three quarters of fish species declined in abundance but only very few declined drastically.

--- SENTENCE 1565 ---
On the other hand, one quarter of the species increased in abundance, some of them greatly.

--- SENTENCE 1566 ---
The net result for the total fish population was little change in the total abundance of the community, although community composition was clearly altered.

--- SENTENCE 1567 ---
This finding is consistent with the lack of drastic change in total fish populations following the COTS outbreak in Tutuila, but also consistent with what is known about the dependence of fish species on live coral tissue.

--- SENTENCE 1568 ---
The strong increase in some fish species may well have been due to the great increase in filamentous algae growing on the newly dead coral skeletons.

--- SENTENCE 1569 ---
In American Samoa, at least, that filamentous algae is replaced in some areas by encrusting coralline algae, starting in about six months after coral death.

--- SENTENCE 1570 ---
An increase in some species is consistent with the report of Sabater and Tofaeono (2007) of larger fish populations on north shore areas outside of bays that have low coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 1571 ---
It is also consistent with the view that the highest coral cover communities do not necessarily have the highest fish populations, because corals provide food for very few species.

--- SENTENCE 1572 ---
Reefs that have a mixture of coral and turf algae may have higher fish populations because many more fish species are herbivores that eat turf algae, which in turn have very high productivity levels, particularly when grazed.

--- SENTENCE 1573 ---
The finding of little change in the overall size of fish communities but changes in community composition following the loss of live coral tissue from bleaching or crown-of-thorns starfish has been reported in a variety of papers on this topic (e.g., Munday, 2004; Bellwood et al. 2006; Wilson et al. 2006; Graham, 2007; Wilson et al. 2008; and references therein).

--- SENTENCE 1574 ---
Bellwood et al. (2006) studied ―cryptobenthic‖ fish (small fish that often hide in holes) using complete sampling with ichthyocide.

--- SENTENCE 1575 ---
These fish have very short lifespans, from months to about a year, and so populations able to survive in a changed environment but unable to recruit new members would show changes over study periods, while such fish may show no changes over normal scientific study periods if their lifespans are 10, 20, or more years as is typical of many larger reef species.

--- SENTENCE 1576 ---
They found no change in total fish abundance, but they did find a change in community composition from specialists to generalists.

--- SENTENCE 1577 ---
Graham (2007) also found specialist corallivores declined more than generalists, and Munday (2004) found that gobies using corals as habitat declined more if they specialized in using just a few species than if they were generalists using many species.

--- SENTENCE 1578 ---
Wilson et al. (2006) reviewed the available literature and performed metaanalysis on data from 22 studies of the effects of habitat loss on coral reef fish.

--- SENTENCE 1579 ---
They found decreases in the abundance of 62% of fish species studied within a year of coral death.

--- SENTENCE 1580 ---
Increases in other species were not consistent, probably in part because the recruitment of additional fish requires more time after the disturbance.

--- SENTENCE 1581 ---
As in other studies, they found the greatest impact on coralivores and obligate coral dwellers.

--- SENTENCE 1582 ---
Coral dwellers declined rapidly after coral tissue death before erosion of the coral skeletons, indicating that the live coral tissue is essential for their survival, often because they eat it.

--- SENTENCE 1583 ---
Eight of 20 invertebrate eating fish species declined following coral death, indicating indirect dependence on corals, as did some small-bodied planktivores.

--- SENTENCE 1584 ---
Some algae/detritus feeders declined a relatively small amount, including C. striatus.

--- SENTENCE 1585 ---
There were increases in abundance of 38% of species, with the greatest increases in algae/detritus feeders and invertebrate eaters.

--- SENTENCE 1586 ---
Diversity of the fish community was correlated with the severity of the coral decline, with small coral declines actually leading to increases in fish diversity, but severe coral declines produced declines in fish diversity.

--- SENTENCE 1587 ---
Structural damage to

coral reefs produced by hurricanes not only kill coral but also reduce habitat complexity and rugosity and reduce refuges, exposing fish to predation and competition.

--- SENTENCE 1588 ---
Such physical damage caused greater reductions in fish populations, and caused greater reductions than coral death in each functional group.

--- SENTENCE 1589 ---
The overall conclusion from studies of the effects of the death (but not destruction) of corals on fish communities, is that coral death has remarkably small effects on the total community size, but does affect community composition.

--- SENTENCE 1590 ---
The effects of physical destruction of coral in addition to coral death are greater, causing a greater loss of fish in more species, as would be expected from the loss of refuges for many species in addition to food sources for a limited number of species.

--- SENTENCE 1591 ---
There are many different features of coral reef habitats that are possible important variables in the niches occupied by different fish species, and it is not easy to tease the different variables apart.

--- SENTENCE 1592 ---
It appears that turf algae cover may be an important variable for some species, while live coral is important for a few others.

--- SENTENCE 1593 ---
A variable that may be very important for many species is hiding spaces.

--- SENTENCE 1594 ---
Reef fish populations are likely strongly influenced by predation.

--- SENTENCE 1595 ---
Places to hide from predators are very important for many fish species.

--- SENTENCE 1596 ---
This is easily illustrated by schools of planktivorous damselfish, such as Chromis sp.

--- SENTENCE 1597 ---
A school may swim well above a colony of finger coral, but a sudden movement by a human observer and the school will dive down between the branches.

--- SENTENCE 1598 ---
Different species are likely to have different requirements for just what sort of hiding space they need or prefer.

--- SENTENCE 1599 ---
Coral reefs often are highly rugose, with lots of bumps and holes of various shapes, though different reefs and different patches of a reef can have different amounts of rugosity.

--- SENTENCE 1600 ---
The effect on fish populations of rugosity can be illustrated by comparing fish populations over standing live staghorn thickets, standing dead staghorn thickets, and collapsed dead staghorn rubble.

--- SENTENCE 1601 ---
In the backreef pools of Tutuila, just such patches of habitat are available.

--- SENTENCE 1602 ---
Reef fish are abundant over the live staghorn, still present at some abundance over dead standing staghorn, but low over collapsed staghorn rubble.

--- SENTENCE 1603 ---
Quantitative measurements of these fish communities have not been made, but are visually apparent.

--- SENTENCE 1604 ---
These sorts of observations have led to a common hypothesis that the rugosity or presence of hiding places is a more important variable for fish communities than the exact amount of live coral tissue.

--- SENTENCE 1605 ---
The connection between live coral and hiding places or rugosity is that if the coral dies, eventually the branching and foliose corals will collapse, and rugosity will decrease and hiding places lost, and fish populations decrease.

--- SENTENCE 1606 ---
A recent review of fish population trends on reefs in the Caribbean over decades (Paddack et al. 2009) found that fish populations in the Caribbean were relatively steady during the period after disease killed the most abundant herbivore, the sea urchin Diadema, and algal populations increased, and also disease and other factors killed corals and coral cover decreased from 55% in 1975 to 8% today.

--- SENTENCE 1607 ---
That is, fish populations were relatively steady until recently, whereupon they have decreased strongly.

--- SENTENCE 1608 ---
The lack of a drop in fish when the corals were dying supports the view that coral tissue is not critical for most reef fish.

--- SENTENCE 1609 ---
The recent drop in fish populations is across all groups, including species that are not caught by fishers as well as those that are.

--- SENTENCE 1610 ---
This clue indicates that the decline in fish is unlikely to be due to increased fishing pressure.

--- SENTENCE 1611 ---
Instead, the authors suggest that the decrease may be because of the collapse of dead coral skeletons decades after the corals were killed, leading to the loss of rugosity and hiding spaces and thus to fish loses.

--- SENTENCE 1612 ---
A recent study (Alvarez-Filip et al. 2009) demonstrates that the Caribbean has indeed lost rugosity, likely due to the death of corals.

--- SENTENCE 1613 ---
The role of the loss of rugosity in this fish decline has not yet been demonstrated, but the decline was only documented in 2009.

--- SENTENCE 1614 ---
One small problem with the skeleton collapse theory is the data from Faga'alu presented in Figures 22 and 23.

--- SENTENCE 1615 ---
Here, the fish biomass is highest among sites after the corals collapsed.

--- SENTENCE 1616 ---
It is true that there are some hiding holes in the resulting substrate, but the rugosity is surely much less than before the coral skeletons collapsed.

--- SENTENCE 1617 ---
Only rarely have the effects of changes in habitat been compared to changes in fishing pressure on reef fish populations.

--- SENTENCE 1618 ---
Wilson et al. (2008) studied reefs in Fiji which had varying levels of fishing pressure between sites and over time, and varying amounts of live coral cover loss from a crown-of-thorns outbreak in 2000 and bleaching in 2000.

--- SENTENCE 1619 ---
They found that both habitat changes and fishing played critical roles in determining fish abundance and community structure.

--- SENTENCE 1620 ---
The relative importance of these two differed between different groups of fish.

--- SENTENCE 1621 ---
As in previous studies, species with tight associations with corals where most affected by habitat changes.

--- SENTENCE 1622 ---
Even changes in coral community composition without changes in total community cover can affect specialists that depend on particular coral species, as those species change in abundance.

--- SENTENCE 1623 ---
Branching corals like Acropora provide habitat complexity that is important for small-bodied fish, for finding refuges from predators.

--- SENTENCE 1624 ---
Topographic complexity (caves, overhangs and other larger features) are more important for larger fish species.

--- SENTENCE 1625 ---
Rugosity can be retained for years or even decades after coral death, and may not increase quickly with the growth of juvenile corals or encrusting corals.

--- SENTENCE 1626 ---
Habitat complexity was found to affect larger predators indirectly by affecting the abundance of small prey species of fish.

--- SENTENCE 1627 ---
This study also found that models did a better job of accounting for the abundance of individual fish species than functional groups, likely because of the complex differences in the ecology of individual species.

#### **Mangroves**

American Samoa has three species of mangroves (Ellison, 2009), in several small groves, the largest bordering the Pala Lagoon in Nu'uuli on the south side of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 1628 ---
The small size of these mangrove forests may be a limiting factor on coral reef fish populations, since mangroves are reported to be juvenile coral reef fish habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1629 ---
American Samoa is the eastern limit of the Indo-Pacific distribution of mangroves, except for one species in French Polynesia, and an introduced species in Hawaii.

--- SENTENCE 1630 ---
Although the groves in American Samoa are small, so too are the islands, and a quantitative comparison of the area of the groves with the area of the coral reefs is not yet available, nor are comparable figures for places with larger groves and more abundant fish.

--- SENTENCE 1631 ---
Coral reefs have captured the interest of the public and scientists, with their warm clear water, abundant colorful fish, high diversity, and the imposing size of some reef systems (such as the Great Barrier Reef), among other attributes.

--- SENTENCE 1632 ---
Mangroves and seagrass have received much less attention from the public and scientists.

--- SENTENCE 1633 ---
Mangroves are hot, humid, smelly places with bottomless mud and swarms of mosquitoes, which the public does not find attractive.

--- SENTENCE 1634 ---
Seagrass beds are probably more attractive than mangroves, but they lack the abundant colorful fish of coral reefs and are often in shallow water that lacks the dramatic dropoffs of many coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1635 ---
Coral reefs are simply much more charismatic to humans than mangroves or seagrass, and not only do coral reefs receive much more press coverage, but also they are the subject of much more research (Duarte et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 1636 ---
Conservationists find it easier to get public attention and

support for work to save coral reefs than for mangroves or seagrass.

--- SENTENCE 1637 ---
One strategy to try to support the conservation of mangroves and seagrass has been to try to find evidence that they are important for the much more charismatic coral reefs or for protection from tsunamis.

--- SENTENCE 1638 ---
One example of the desire to find justifications to save or replant mangroves comes from the Indonesian tsunami of 2004.

--- SENTENCE 1639 ---
Several publications (e.g., Dahdouh-Guebas et al. 2005; Danielsen et al. 2005; Kathiresan and Rajendran, 2005; Wilkinson et al. 2006, p. 59) afterwards reported that mangroves protected communities inland from them from the damage of the tsunami.

--- SENTENCE 1640 ---
However, subsequently a paper criticized the methods of the most quantitative of those reports (Kerr et al. 2006), and another paper (Kerr and Baird, 2007) reported that in northwest Sumatra where the earthquake was, the tsunami was so powerful it completely removed mangroves and other nearshore forests.

--- SENTENCE 1641 ---
As a result, the mangroves were unable to provide any protection from communities inland from those mangroves, and future planning should not rely on mangroves to protect those communities.

--- SENTENCE 1642 ---
One possible resolution of the differences between the different reports is that near the earthquake center where the tsunami was largest, mangroves could not protect communities from the tsunami.

--- SENTENCE 1643 ---
Much farther away, in places like India and Sri Lanka, where the tsunami was smaller, mangroves were able to offer protection.

--- SENTENCE 1644 ---
But the point is that a desire to find support for conserving mangroves can lead people to uncritically accept information about the benefits of mangroves, some of which may prove to be exaggerated.

--- SENTENCE 1645 ---
One way to increase support for the conservation of ecosystems like mangroves and seagrass might be to show that they are critical to the survival and well-being of a charismatic ecosystem like coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1646 ---
The nursery hypothesis is that mangroves and seagrass serve as nurseries for coral reef fish, and thus are important for good reef fish populations for fisheries and the healthy ecological functioning of coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1647 ---
The desire to assist conservation of mangroves and seagrass has led to studies of these ecosystems as nursery habitat for juvenile reef fish, to try to document that they serve as nurseries.

--- SENTENCE 1648 ---
This appears to have a parallel in the desire to state that corals serve as the home of fish, since the public sees the importance of reef fish as food, even when it doesn't see the importance of corals.

--- SENTENCE 1649 ---
The nursery concept is at least a hundred years old, and was applied in temperate areas long before it was applied to mangroves and seagrass.

--- SENTENCE 1650 ---
Inshore areas and estuaries have long been thought to be nurseries for juveniles of fish and invertebrate species that inhabit deeper water as adults (Beck et al. 2001).

--- SENTENCE 1651 ---
For sharks, ―In some cases regions are labeled shark nurseries simply because of the presence of a few juvenile sharks.‖ ―…occurrence of juvenile sharks in an area is insufficient evidence to proclaim it a nursery.‖ The nursery concept implies that juveniles inhabit a particular area, grow and then move to an adult area.

--- SENTENCE 1652 ---
It also implies that such juveniles provide a substantial input of juveniles into the adult area; a few juveniles showing the pattern does not demonstrate a nursery area.

--- SENTENCE 1653 ---
Beck et al. (2001) reviewed the history and concept and proposed a definition of a nursery: ―A habitat is a nursery for juveniles of a particular species if its contribution per unit area to the production of individuals that recruit to adult populations is greater, on average, than production from other habitats in which juveniles occur.‖ To prove that an area is a nursery is a difficult thing, rarely if ever accomplished.

--- SENTENCE 1654 ---
Movements from juvenile to adult habitat must be measured.

--- SENTENCE 1655 ---
Densities of juveniles in all habitats that juveniles occupy must be measured and compared, to show that a putative nursery area has a higher density than other habitats.

--- SENTENCE 1656 ---
And it must be shown that those juveniles migrate to adult habitats.

--- SENTENCE 1657 ---
In most past

studies, an area was designated a nursery if it had juveniles at higher density than some other habitat (for mangroves and seagrass that other habitat was usually nearby un-vegetated habitat, such as open sand), survival was higher, or juveniles grow faster there than elsewhere.

--- SENTENCE 1658 ---
All these lines of evidence are suggestive, but none provide strong evidence, since they could all happen in areas where juveniles do not migrate to adult habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1659 ---
A juvenile habitat is anywhere some juveniles live, a nursery is a place they live in higher numbers and grow and survive and then migrate to adult habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1660 ---
The authors recognize that these are difficult things to demonstrate, and recommend that managers not wait for absolute proof, but use the precautionary principle to protect areas that some evidence indicates may be nurseries.

--- SENTENCE 1661 ---
This has a parallel in the evidence needed in fisheries to prove overfishing.

--- SENTENCE 1662 ---
That evidence is often lacking, hence the provision in places like the Magnuson-Stevens Act that the best available science and the precautionary principle must be used.

--- SENTENCE 1663 ---
Whatever standard of evidence is used to compare hypotheses such as juvenile habitat limitation and reduction by fishing, the same standard should be used with all, so absolute proof should not be required for one while suggestive evidence is accepted for the other.

--- SENTENCE 1664 ---
Areas that provide more than the average number of individuals to adult habitats per unit area are called nursery areas, while areas that produce a larger total number of individuals are called effective juvenile habitats (Adams et al. 2006).

--- SENTENCE 1665 ---
The difference comes because an area that has lower than average production per unit of area, could be a much larger area and thus produce more total input to the adult habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1666 ---
Most of the research on and reviews of nursery function have been for temperate ecosystems, not tropical.

--- SENTENCE 1667 ---
Mumby et al. 2004 compared 164 coral reef fish species on Caribbean reefs with many nearby mangroves compared to little or no mangroves.

--- SENTENCE 1668 ---
They found 44 species of fish in the mangroves.

--- SENTENCE 1669 ---
They found the reef fish communities were quite different.

--- SENTENCE 1670 ---
They also found higher biomass on the reefs near mangroves for six species and two families (Haemulidae and Lutjanidae).

--- SENTENCE 1671 ---
For one species (Haemulon sciurus) populations on patch reefs near mangroves were 25 times as great as on reefs with little or no mangroves.

--- SENTENCE 1672 ---
Differences for other species/reef zone combinations were less, with just one combination being more.

--- SENTENCE 1673 ---
For one species, the largest herbivorous marine fish in the Atlantic, the rainbow parrot (Scarus guacamaia), juveniles were seen only in mangroves, and adults were much less abundant on reefs without nearby mangroves than on reefs with mangroves.

--- SENTENCE 1674 ---
They are now locally extinct on one atoll where mangroves had been removed and in which they were heavily fished.

--- SENTENCE 1675 ---
Thus, one fish absolutely requires mangroves for juvenile habitat, two families and six species were influenced by the presence of mangroves (they were more abundant on reefs near mangroves and presumably use the mangroves as juvenile habitat (although this was not reported) and move from the mangroves to the reef (also not reported)).

--- SENTENCE 1676 ---
For one fish, H. sciurus, sizes suggest they may start in seagrass, move to mangroves, and then move to reef.

--- SENTENCE 1677 ---
But for the majority of the 164 species recorded, there was no evidence to indicate that the presence of mangroves had an effect on their reef populations.

--- SENTENCE 1678 ---
Negelkerken et al. (2002) reported that 17 of 85 fish reef fish species had only juveniles in back-reef habitats and only adults on reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1679 ---
Adams et al. (2006) state ―…many species show considerable plasticity in habitat use as juveniles.‖ And ―Moreover, many species are abundant in numerous habitats as juveniles suggesting a facultative strategy of habitat use.‖ Perhaps juveniles of many species settle in whatever shallow habitat they end up in, try to survive and grow, and if they grow large enough migrate to the reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1680 ---
One problem is the question of how far juvenile fish can migrate from mangroves or seagrass to get to reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1681 ---
For the reef slopes, that is not a problem, since they are adjacent to reef flats and seagrass beds.

--- SENTENCE 1682 ---
For some mangroves such as at Leone, the distance is not much further.
